MEMORANDUM OF THE CHAIRMAN 

This publication is the 17th in a series of documents which are being prepared to assist the committee in its study of national fuels and energy policy pursuant to Senate Resolution 45. Entitled, "Energy 

Activities of the 92d Congress," this committee print contains a review of various energy related legislative and administrative activities during the first session of the 92d Congress. 

In January 1971, the Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs published "A Review of Energy Issues and the 91st Congress." At that time, I noted that several events, including power failures and projected fuel shortages on the East Coast, had generated a variety of activities in the Congress. Over a short period of time there emerged a general recognition that the United States had no comprehensive energy policy but had, through an "ad hoc" decisionmaking process, constructed in its place a group of "de facto" energy policies which are limited in scope and fragmented in nature. The events of recent years have demonstrated that this method of formulating energy policy can no longer suffice in a situation where energy supply systems have proven inadequate to meet the growing energy needs of the Nation.

The information presented in this new publication provides evidence that the Nation's ad hoc, crisis-oriented energy decisionmaking process has changed little over the past year. At the same time, the greater volume of material contained in this publication as compared to the review of the 91st Congress indicates that congressional concern for the adequacy of the Nation's energy supply and for the ability of present policies to deal with a wide range of issues continues to grow. 

During the first session of the 92d Congress alone, 18 committees and subcommittees held 130 days of hearings to consider energy policy issues and legislative proposals. 

This current review was prepared at my request by Mr. Harry 

Perry, senior specialist of the Environmental Policy Division, Congressional Research Service. I believe that the materials contained in this document will help to create a better understanding of the manner in which this Nation's energy policies have been formulated. 

Furthermore, this document will be useful to the committee in its study of the substance, procedures, and organization of energy and fuels policymaking and, eventually, in the formulation of recommendations for changes therein. Therefore, I have directed that this review be reproduced as a committee print to make it readily available to the members and ex-officio members of the committee and to other interested parties. 

---

LETTER OF TRANSMITTAL THE LIBRARY OF CONGRESS, CONGRESSIONAL RESEARCH SERVICE, Hon. HENRY M. JACKSON, Washington, D.C., December 30, 1971.

(VII)

A REVIEW OF ENERGY POLICY ACTIVITIES OF THE 92D CONGRESS, FIRST SESSION Compiled by HARRY PERRY, Senior Specialist with the assistance of 

Daniel Beard and Howard Brown

Environmental Policy Division Congressional Research Service Library of Congress at the Request of

HENRY M. JACKSON, Chairman Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs United States Senate January 1972 (IX)

INTRODUCTION 

The early 1970's marked a significant turning point in the Nation's energy system and economy. Heretofore, adequate supplies of energy, at reasonable prices, have been primary characteristics of our energy economy. Even though major changes in the sources of energy supplies have periodically taken place (wood to coal, coal to oil, oil to gas), institutional, industrial and household consumers made these transitions relatively easily because of the availability of alternative and economical energy supplies. Thus the history of energy usage in the 

United States has reflected both a trend of smooth transitions between energy resources and adequate production of energy supplies to meet steadily growing needs.

These two basic characteristics, resource adequacy and transition capability between energy forms, in large measure, explain the lack of development of a comprehensive national policy to deal with unforeseen problems. The need for such a national policy was simply not pressing, and, since indigenous resources appeared to be unlimited, no over-riding concern was expressed for comprehensive management and conservation of energy resources. As difficulties came to be experienced with individual resources, policies and programs were developed to fit specific cases. Over a long period of time a patchwork of laws, regulations, actions and inactions-at various levels of government-came to pervade the management of each energy resource. Instead of a comprehensive national energy policy being developed, de facto policies, prompted by the pressures of narrowly based interests seeking solutions to specific economic or technical problems developed. Moreover, these de facto energy policies frequently resulted in actions which worked toward conflicting or contradictory ends. The Department of the Interior, for example, had administered a program to restrict oil imports while the Environmental Protection Agency has set standards for sulfur content of residual oil that tend to require the importation from other nations of increasingly larger amounts of low sulfur oil. The Tennessee Valley Authority has encouraged increased use of electric energy while at the same time, many local regulatory agencies have established cumbersome requirements for the siting of new power plants. Finally, the coal industry has remained unregulated by State and Federal agencies, with the exception of transportation, health and safety aspects, while the natural gas industry is controlled from the well-head to the consumer. 

These de facto policies, with all their internal contradictions, formed a workable basis for decision making in a period of adequate and economical energy supplies. But in the past few years conditions have changed. After decades of abundance the Nation is suddenly faced with the possibility of long-term supply shortages and persistent interruptions in the production and delivery of energy. In less than a decade the nation has moved from a position of energy surplus to energy shortages. (1)

What are the reasons behind these dramatic changes in the U.S. energy position?

Growth in Population and Per Capita Consumption 

First, the dual growth in population and energy use per capita has placed a burdensome strain on our ability to meet energy needs. 

Energy consumption in the United States increased 10,817 trillion Btus between 1950 and 1960, then upward to 18,000 trillion Btus between 1960 and 1970. Predictions of consumption for the decade of the 1970's are for an additional 31,000 trillion Btus, and a further increase of 32,000 trillion Btus by 1990.

Per capita energy use increased from 225 thousand Btus to 250 thousand Btus per year in the 1950's, and by 1970 per capita usage had reached 340 thousand Btus. Projections based on these figures place per capita consumption at 500 thousand or more Btus by the year 2000.

Recent increases in absolute consumption, together with projected increases in per capita use, will require massive changes in the structural characteristics of both our energy economy, our governmental institution and our basic energy policies. 

Development of Environmental Quality Goals 

A second reason is that the increased concern in the last few years for environmental quality has placed new responsibilities on all energy industries. Environmental protection pressures have been heaviest on those energy industries which are major contributors to the pollution of water, air and land through their production processes, transportation, and other energy conversion and utilization techniques. The strip mining of coal, acid mine drainage, oil spills, sulfur oxide and nitrogen oxide emissions from power plants, thermal pollution from electric generating stations, solid waste disposal of the ash resulting from coal combustion, as well as the hydro-carbons, oxides of nitrogen and carbon monoxide emitted from the automobile exhaust are among the major causes of pollution related to energy production and use. 

The growing public awareness of environmental problems has sustained public demands for remedial and regulatory action. These demands have had some profound impacts on individual energy industries in the last few years. Large reserves of high sulfur coal, for example, are no longer usable due to the promulgation of new air pollution standards; the drilling and production of offshore oil has been halted or slowed in some areas; siting of electric power plants and transmission lines has encountered increased citizen opposition, resulting in delays in their construction; and many projects of local, regional and national importance have been delayed or halted. These environmentally-oriented constraints on energy production and utilization have placed new management burdens on government and the energy industries and, equally important, they have added a new dimension and raised new questions in the debate concerning the adequacy of existing "de facto" energy policies.

Natural Gas Shortage 

Third, with the exception of burgeoning demand for electricity, natural gas consumption has grown at a faster rate over the past two decades than any other energy form. The non-polluting characteris-

tics of natural gas have further increased consumer demand. This increased demand could not have come at a more inappropriate time. 

Beginning in 1968, for the first time in history, more natural gas was consumed than was discovered. In that year, the deficiency between consumption and discovery of new supplies was 5 trillion cubic feet; in 1969 it increased sharply to 12 trillion cubic feet. 

The cause for the decline in gas reserves is a matter of considerable controversy. But one fact about which there appears to be universal agreement is that the natural gas reserves of the lower forty-eight states will not be able to supply domestic demand in the foreseeable future. If, and until, means can be provided to make up for the growing deficiency, shortages and/or haphazardly imposed restrictions in natural gas use will continue to occur. Adjustment to potential shortages, or at best to a significant decrease in the growth rate for gas, has been a major factor in the changing structure of the nation's energy system and in public and governmental concern about energy policy.

Oil Imports 

Fourth, the nation's reliance on foreign fuel supplies is increasing.

In 1948 the United States became a net importer of liquid fuels. By the mid-1950's, there was growing concern over the future of the domestic oil industry because foreign oil supplies could be produced and supplied at much lower costs than U.S. oil. For national security reasons, it was decided, as a matter of national policy, that the domestic oil industry must be protected and a quota on oil imports was established. While there have been many changes in the original quota system (the most important being the de-control of residual oil for East Coast use), the original objectives still remain in force. 

The development of a gas shortage and a temporary surge in demand for coal that could not be met immediately (see discussion below) created a sharp increase in the demand for liquid fuels. In addition, restrictive environmental standards on the amount of sulfur in fuels became operative at the same time, thereby increasing the demand for clean fuels to replace high sulfur coal. Domestic liquid fuels with the required low sulfur content were, however, not available in the quantities needed. The limitations on production of the low sulfur crude of 

Libya imposed by the Libyan government, the breakdown of the Trans-Arabian pipeline and lack of refining capacity to produce a low sulfur residual oil from high sulfur crudes all combined to limit supplies. At the same time, the demand for liquid fuels was increasing rapidly in Europe and tankers to transport oil to markets were in short supply.

Demands for a relaxation of the oil import quotas by congressional delegations from the energy-deficient Northeastern States, the con troversial recommendations of the Cabinet Task Force on Oil Import 

Control, and the obvious need for greatly increased quantities of future oil imports (even from politically insecure sources) created uncertainties that made business planning and investment decisions hazardous. 

As a result of these and other factors, delays have been experienced in the construction of necessary plant and equipment which, in turn, have further aggravated serious local and regional shortages of domestic oil supplies and raised important questions concerning national energy policy.

Coal Availability 

Fifth, although coal reserves are considered sufficient for many years, serious supply problems arose at electric utility plants during 1970. The delays in the construction of nuclear power plants created an unexpected increase in demand for coal to fire older electric power plants. These facilities were operated beyond their expected life and at much higher load factors than originally intended. At the same time, demand for export coal (sold at premium prices) also reached record peaks. Moreover, as this unexpected demand for coal rose, the coal industry was faced with a new, complex and extremely rigid Federal health and safety law and with rising public concern over surface mining practices. New equipment had to be installed and safer methods of operation had to be initiated. These factors, combined with the unrest in the coal fields caused by a union election, resulted in a decrease in productivity.

Along with production problems there were difficulties in supplying freight cars to transport coal. Many available cars were being used as storage for the expanded coal export market and longer rail hauls were frequently required to deliver coal of the quality that would meet new environmental quality standards.

In view of the large domestic coal reserves available to suppliers, many of these problems are, in all probability, only temporary. Others, however, such as the new environmental standards and the costs associated with the new health and safety law, can be expected to result in permanent changes in the structure and institutional character of the coal based energy economy. 

Electric Power Generation 

Finally, the demand for electricity during the latter half of the 1960's grew faster than had been projected. This, combined with the delays in delivery of nuclear generating plants and reliability problems, caused brownouts (voltage reductions) in some parts of the country during 1970 and, less frequently, during 1971. Given sufficient time, these problems can be alleviated. However, the increasing difficulties of acquiring suitable, uncontested power plant sites and of processing the necessary permits and licenses to build new generating and transmission facilities could result in continued future delays that could lead to a lack of capacity and serious problems in meeting future demands.

The new environmental quality standards are a pervasive problem which will continue to complicate the operations of conventional power plants. Air quality standards, for example, have disqualified many coal reserves for utility use unless technology is developed to control sulfur oxide emissions. Problems of sulfur oxide and thermal pollution control, the increasing costs of solid waste disposal, the perpetual storage of nuclear wastes and more stringent particulate emission standards will result in higher prices for electricity. Other environmentally-related innovations which are leading to higher costs include the undergrounding of distribution lines, the difficulties of bringing high voltage transmission lines into urban areas and the need to make long distance high voltage transmission lines aesthetically acceptable.

These, then, are six of the major factors behind the recent problems being experienced by energy industries and consumers. These problems

indicate that the smooth transitions which characterized pre-1970 developments may not be possible in the years ahead. The long lead time necessary to explore and develop oil and gas resources, to plan and construct power plants, and to build the transportation facilities to deliver energy where it is needed could lead to temporary shortages of energy in various localities. 

To prevent such shortages from becoming chronic a more systematic and continuing examination of our future needs in relation to sources of supply as well as a more comprehensive approach in the development of national fuels and energy policies will be required. 

Public and private actions in the energy field require coordination and cooperative planning to prevent waste and inefficiency and to assure that contradictory policies are not followed by different groups seeking their own ends. Determination must be made concerning the relative importance to be attached to security of supply, economic growth, environmental protection, balance of payments and a host of other national social and economic objectives that are reflected in national energy policy.

It is with this background of events and problems that the 92nd Congress initiated extensive investigations of Federal energy programs. 

Both the Senate and the House considered measures calling for the formulation of a national energy policy. Hearings have been held on a 

Presidential message on the state of energy development. And individual problems which affect energy resources have been the subject of hearings and debates within Congress. 

These policy reviews have indicated the need for a report synthesizing the energy activities which have taken place and the studies which have been issued during the first session of the 92nd Congress. 

This report attempts to fill that need by surveying Congressional activities on energy problems and providing a broad policy perspective within which these activities can be viewed. 

The report is organized into four basic parts. The first section outlines those activities which received final action by either the Senate or the House. The second section summarizes the major issues and positions which were highlighted in hearings on various energy topics. 

The third section briefly analyzes a large number of energy-oriented reports issued in the past year, drawing from them significant recommendations or ideas which will be of interest to various committees. 

The last section of the report discusses several emerging issues and problems which the second session of the 92nd Congress will have to address.

LEGISLATIVE ACTIONS I. National Energy Policy 

In 1970 many facets of the "energy crisis"-the natural gas and freight car shortages, oil imports, offshore oil development and a host of other problems were the subject of hearings, debates and legislation. The conflicting and often contradictory nature of many U.S. energy policies began to emerge from these activities. This new understanding, in turn, was a prime motive for the introduction of legislation which authorized a Senate study designed to formulate a comprehensive national energy policy. 

S. 4092, introduced in the 91st Congress, would have authorized the creation of a Commission on Fuels and Energy to make a detailed investigation and study of U.S. energy requirements and fuel resources. 

This Commission would also examine present policies with respect to each fuel and energy type, project the Nation's energy needs, inventory the fuel resources available to meet these needs, and describe the impact of energy development, utilization and transportation on the environment. In addition, existing policies and programs of Federal, State and local governments having an impact on the availability or economic utilization of basic fuel resources would have been the subject of detailed investigation.

Hearings were held on S. 4092 before the Subcommittee on Minerals, Materials and Fuels of the Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs on September 10 and 11, 1970.2 Witnesses identified such problems as conflicting regulatory, development, and environmental policies; the growing dependence on foreign energy sources; and supply shortages of selected fuels.

The witnesses stated very similar reasons for these and other energy problems. General agreement also was expressed concerning the need to develop a coordinated national energy policy which would prevent the repetition of existing problems. The National Coal Association, for example, expressed this concern by noting that the "energy crisis" has led to an almost universal recognition that we must have a coordinated national energy policy and perhaps even a national energy agency, if we are to succeed in meeting the great energy requirements of the future without unnecessary damage to the environment, without a dangerous degree of reliance on unstable foreign sources of supply, and without an economy-wrecking deficit in our balance of trade.³

Senator Randolph, who introduced the measure, commented that the reason why "coordinated national energy policy" had not been developed in the past was the Nation's failure to consider general energy policy, rather than specific fuels policies. This failure to focus on the broad spectrum of activities influencing a policy area was the 1 For a thorough discussion of these activities, see: U.S. Senate, Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs, A Review of Energy Issues and the 91st Congress, Committee Print, Prepared by the Congressional Research 

Service, 92d Cong., 1st Sess., January 29, 1971, 38 pp. 

2 U.S. Senate, Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs, Subcommittee on Minerals, Materials and 

Fuels, Fuels and Energy, Hearings on S. 4092, 91st Cong., 2d Sess., September 10 and 11, 1970. 

8 Id. at, 68. 71-900-72- -2 (7)

major reason for the many inconsistencies which had been outlined by other witnesses.

Despite a unanimity of opinion among witnesses on the need for a national energy policy, no action was taken on S. 4092 during the 91st 

Congress. This was due to two factors: First, in the rush to conclude the session, other legislation took precedence over S. 4092. Second, and more important, the Administration, in two separate letters to the 

Chairman of the Interior Committee, opposed enactment of S. 4092. 

One letter from the Director of the Office of Emergency Preparedness stated:

It would appear that the study proposed by S. 4092 would closely parallel and duplicate the study requested by the President which is now well underway. By contrast, enactment of S. 4092 and appropriation of funds to support the proposed 

Commission, appointment of Commission members, selection of Commission staff and other necessary organizational steps would necessarily delay the commencement of the Commission's study. 

Consequently, to avoid duplicative studies and to avoid the delays that would result if the Commission study were substituted for the Council study. I recommend against the enactment of S. 4092.5 

On December 22, 1970, Senator Randolph expressed disappointment at the Administration's rejection of a legislative-executive-public partnership as suggested in S. 4092. In light of this opposition, Senator 

Randolph suggested an alternative at the close of the 91st Congress: 

Senator Jackson and I . . . are in agreement that the most feasible vehicle for an urgently needed congressional effort would be a resolution empowering the Senate Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs to make a detailed fuels and energy study and to report its recommendations during the 2-year life of the next Congress, beginning in January 1971 and extending to January 1973. 

No other means is known that can activate this vital effort without further lengthy delay.Ⓡ 

Senator Randolph introduced Senate Resolution 45 on February 4, 1971, which would authorize the Senate Interior and Insular Affairs Committee, with the chairmen and ranking minority members of the 

Committees on Commerce and Public Works and the ranking majority and minority Senate members of the Joint Committee on Atomic Energy acting as ex officio members, to undertake a national fuels and energy policy study. The resolution specifically directed the study group to: (a) make a full and complete investigation and study of the current and prospective fuel and energy resources and requirements of the United States and the present and probable future alternative procedures and methods for meeting anticipated requirements, consistent with achieving other national goals and (b) make a full and complete investigation and study of the existing and prospective governmental policies and laws affecting the fuels and energy industries with the view of determining what, if any, changes and implementation of these policies and laws may be advisable in order to simplify, coordinate, and provide effective and reasonable national policy to assure reliable and efficient sources of fuel and energy adequate for a balanced economy and for the security of the United States, taking into account: the Nation's environmental concerns, the investments by public and private enterprise for the maintenance of reliable, efficient, and adequate sources of energy and fuel and necessary related industries, and the need for maintenance of an adequate force of skilled workers. 4.66 study the national energy situation and develop for his consideration new or revised energy policies. 

The [Domestic] Council has been specifically requested to recommend Federal actions which may be taken to alleviate the acute shortage of clean fuels for this winter and to ensure an adequate fuel supply for next five years." (Weekly Compilation of Presidential Documents, August 10, 1970, Vol. 6, No. 32, p. 1030). 

Supra, Note 2, at 255. 

6 Remarks of Senator Randolph, Congressional Record, Daily Edition, December 22, 1970, p. S21068.

The resolution also called for the study group to consider the following factors in the course of its investigation: resources (1) the proved and predicted availabilities of our national fuel and energy as well as worldwide trends in consumption and supply; (2) projected national requirements to meet short range needs and to provide for future demand for the years 2000 to 2020; 

(3) the interests of the consuming public, including the availability in all regions of the country of an adequate supply of energy and fuel at reasonable prices and... the maintenance of a sound competitive structure in the supply and distribution of energy and fuel to both industry and the public; (4) technological development affecting energy and fuel production, distribution, transportation, and/or transmission (5) the effect that energy producing, transportation, upgrading and utilization has upon conservation, environmental and ecological factors (6) the effect upon the public and private sectors of the economy of any recommendations made under this study, and of existing governmental programs and policies now in effect; (7) the effect of any recommendations made pursuant to this study on economic concentrations in industry particularly as these recommendations may affect small business enterprises.. (8) governmental programs and policies now in operation and (9) the need, if any, for legislation designed to effectuate recommendations in accordance with the above and other relevant considerations The resolution authorized the Interior Committee to employ such staff as necessary to carry out the study and to report to the Senate by 

February 1973. 

On February 25, 1971, hearings were held on S. Res. 45 before the full Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs. The general tenor of the hearings was quite similar to those held on S. 4092 just four months earlier. The majority of witnesses again stressed the need for a national energy policy, without specifying what should be contained in such a policy. The witness for the United Mine Workers emphasized the urgent need for a re-examination of old policy concepts. 

We suggest that the past policy framework within which energy operated is no longer adequate. We suggest that fragmentation of regulation, and laissez-faire in competitive relationships, no longer meet the realities of the seventies and beyond. 

What must be done is to totally reexamine the current energy framework and to change those parameters which must be changed, to redefine those goals which must be redefined, and to establish a new energy policy in keeping with our 

Nation's present and future needs."

Some dissatisfaction was voiced during the hearings with respect to the type of institutional structure within which a national energy policy would be formulated. In S. 4092, a commission, patterned after the Public Land Law Review Commission, would have conducted a a national fuels and energy policy study. Senate Resolution 45, however, placed the responsibility for the study solely on the shoulders of the Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs staff.

Both the American Public Power Association and the Consumer Federation of America suggested that the committee staff was already overworked and that the enormity of the task called for in S. Res. 45 would simply be too great a burden. In addition, both these groups felt that a "commission approach" offered greater opportunity for public participation; they expressed reservations about the possibility of obtaining all points of view under the format provided by S. Res. 45. 

When the bill was reported out of committee, two amendments had been added. The first amended section one to ensure that the study was 

7 U.S. Senate, Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs, National Fuels and Energy Policy, Hearings on S. Res. 45, 92d Cong., 1st Sess., February 25, 1971, p. 79.

carried out in "accordance with the national policy enunciated in the 

Mining and Minerals Policy Act of 1970." The second amendment authorized the committee to expend $200,000 from the time of enactment through February 29, 1972.

The Committee on Rules and Administration added several other amendments. One authorized the ranking majority and minority members to designate members of their committee to serve in their place as ex officio members for purposes of the study; as introduced, S. Res. 45 allowed the appointment of any Senate member to replace those automatically chosen. Another amendment authorized the Interior Committee to expend an amount "not to exceed $12,000" for the procurement of consultants. Finally, the deadline contained in the original resolution was deleted. However, the $200,000 authorized for the study was expendable only through February 29, 1972.

On May 3, 1971, the Senate considered and passed S. Res. 45, as amended.⁹

Since the enactment of the resolution, the Senate Interior Committee study has moved forward in several areas. A preliminary study plan for the project was prepared with a view toward maximum accomplishment within the limitations imposed by time and monies available. 

These limitations dictate that: (1) existing data and information be relied upon wherever possible; (2) a very small staff be assembled with substantial reliance on the Congressional Research Service and consultants on an "as needed" basis; (3) the focus of the study be on realistic and attainable policy options; (4) cooperation and close assistance of Executive Branch agencies be fostered; and (5) many decisions restricting the potential scope of the study be made. 

The plan also calls for a division of the study into four parts which will be investigated concurrently. The first part deals with a review of national goals and their relationship to existing energy policy. The second part deals with the known and potential supply of energy resources in relation to projections of future energy demands. The third part of the study contemplates a review of existing policy constraints on energy supply and demand and the identification of alternative policy options which could influence supply and demand. 

In the fourth part of the study selected topical energy issues will be reviewed in depth and tested in relation to the systematic studies conducted in the other three parts. 

Two major time frames will be kept in mind throughout the study. 

The first, from 1971 to 1980, will cover the short range energy situation. Because of the long lead time required for capital construction, corporate planning, and research and development of new energy technologies, the range of policy options for this period will be quite limited. The second time frame, 1980 to 2020, presents fewer constraints on policy options and provides the lead time necessary to undertake virtually any new policy initiatives which may be necessary to influence the energy situation in the long term. Accordingly, separate recommendations are envisioned for short and long term energy situations.

The Senate Interior Committee has undertaken a number of hearings on individual problems identified in the study plans. These hearings are listed in Appendix C under the Senate Interior and Insular

Affairs Committee. In addition, a number of publications have been issued pursuant to the mandate issued under S. Res. 45. These are listed, along with other committee prints of the 92nd Congress, in 

Appendix Ď. 

Apart from Senate action, national energy policy received attention in the House where two types of measures were introduced. One group of bills called for a Commission on National Fuels and Energy similar to the proposal contained in S. 4092 as introduced in the 91st 

Congress. The second group of bills called for the creation of a House 

Select Committee on Energy. 

The only bill which received House action was H. Res. 155, introduced by Mr. Fulton of Tennessee and co-sponsored by more than one hundred Congressmen. H. Res. 155 sought to create a select committee in the House of Representatives to make a "full and complete investigation of all the aspects of the energy resources of the United States." The Select Committee sought to focus its activities on the following subject areas: (1) the availability of oil, gas, coal and nuclear energy reserves; (2) the identification of the ownership of such reserves; (3) the reasons and the possible solutions for the delay in new starts of fossil fuel powerplants; (4) the effect of pricing practices by the owners of energy reserves; (5) the effect of the import of low sulfur fuels; (6) measures to increase the availability of pipelines, railways, barges and ships needed to transport fuel materials; (7) measures to close the gap between the supply and demand for electric energy; and (8) the identification of the environmental effects of the electricity industry.

The select committee would have submitted a report to the House "as soon as practicable during the present Congress." 

The Committee on Rules held hearings on H. Res. 155 on May 18, 1971, and it was reported to the Whole House. 

The debate on the resolution produced agreement with respect to the need and indispensability of energy to an industrialized society, the need for adequate energy supplies to promote economic growth, and the adverse impact that energy utilization can have on the environment. There was also agreement that energy shortages were a problem and that the availability of future energy supplies was dependent on present-day actions.

Support for H. Res. 155 was based on the premise that finding a solution to energy problems would require the development of a coordinated national energy policy to replace our present system of de facto policies. The dynamic nature of energy supplies, evolving environmental concerns, and ever-increasing demand in the face of fuel and energy shortages were given as reasons for a comprehensive analysis of the entire spectrum of energy problems. By examining the resource base, concentration of resource ownership and end-uses, a rational, orderly and sound policy could be developed. 

When debate on H. Res. 155 began, however, considerable opposition to the resolution was apparent. Several opponents pointed out that many studies were already underway and that a large volume of information has already been gathered on individual problems identi-

fied in the resolution. Examples of those efforts which would be duplicated by implementation of H. Res. 155 were cited in the debate: the already published data on fuel reserves by the Federal Power Commission; the extensive hearings on electric power reliability, power plant siting, and the environmental impacts of producing electricity; hearings on the ownership of energy resources; hearings on the delay in nuclear power plant starts; and others. In addition to hearings, opponents pointed out that many studies on energy problems identified in H. Res. 

155 were presently underway in several other House committees; they included studies on: 

(1) Resource Availability-Interior and Insular Affairs and 

Judiciary Committees (the latter committee had concentrated its attention on the outer continental shelf). 

(2) Ownership of Energy Reserves-Interior and Insular Affairs, Select Small Business and Judiciary Committees. 

(3) Delays in Nuclear Power Plant Starts-Joint Committee on 

Atomic Energy, and the Subcommittee on Communications and 

Power of the Interstate and Foreign Commerce Committee. 

(4) Energy pricing practices-Committee on the Judiciary. 

(5) Importation of Low Sulfur Fuels-the Select Small Business, Interior and Insular Affairs and Ways and Means Committees. 

(6) Energy Transportation Problems-Subcommittee on Communications and Power, Joint Atomic Energy and Public Works 

Committees; (7) Supply and Demand for Electricity Subcommittee on

Communications and Power, Joint Atomic Energy and Public Works Committees; and (8) Environmental Impacts of Electricity Generation and 

Transmission-Subcommittee on Communications and Power,

Joint Atomic Energy and Public Works Committee. 

Major opposition to H. Res. 155 was based on the issue of the overlapping jurisdiction a select committee would have with existing standing committees. Much of the opposition expressed over this issue came from chairmen and subcommittee chairmen whose committees held jurisdiction over energy related legislation. Testimony in opposition to H. Res. 155 was received from the Chairmen of the Committee on the Judiciary, Interior and Insular Affairs, Government Opera tions, Merchant Marine and Fisheries, Education and Labor, and

Interstate and Foreign Commerce. 

The resolution was defeated, 128 to 218.

Thus, unlike S. Res. 45, the House resolution did not receive support from the chairmen of those committees involved in energy matters. Moreover, the resolution failed to include the usual disclaimer which could have placed the objections of some members at rest." 10 In addition to these factors, H. Res. 155 appeared to receive little notice when it was considered by the Committee on Rules. At one point in the debate, Mr. Anderson of Tennessee observed that: it should be made very, very clear that notice of the hearings was put on the calendar twice and printed in the Record four times, and not a single witness 10 Usually, the House Committee on Rules, in authorizing studies and investigations, inserts the following language: "However, the committee shall not undertake any investigation of any subject which is being investigated for the same purpose by any other committee of the House."

appeared before the Committee on Rules in opposition to the resolution. It was reported out without a dissenting vote from the Committee on Rules.11 

However, Mr. Macdonald replied:

Mr. Speaker, I would like to point out the fact that the reason no one appeared before the Rules Committee was the fact that no affected committees were notified that hearings were being held. I can guarantee that many committees would have appeared had there been proper notification.12 

Nevertheless, the defeat of H. Res. 155 has not diminished the strong House interest in national energy policy. For example, a Re publican Task Force on Energy and Resources, and the Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs have subsequently undertaken projects which focus on special aspects of energy policy. 

In addition, at the request of a number of interested House members, Chairman George P. Miller of the House Committee on Science and Astronautics established a Task Force on Energy Research and Development in July 1971. This Task Force was constituted under the jurisdiction of the Subcommittee on Science, Research and Development, chaired by John W. Davis. The legislative responsibility for scientific research and development in the House is lodged with this committee, and the important role that research and development can contribute in meeting future national energy needs and environmental protection was a prime stimulus in the development of this Task Force.

The Task Force, after organizational meetings, started a systematic examination of energy R & D opportunities. This is being accomplished through a series of meetings to which speakers from various government agencies, scientific organizations and industries are invited to present their views on the energy situation. Emphasis is on the role of energy R & D with evaluation of the present state of the art of technology and research opportunities. Field visits are being undertaken to examine the nature of research problems, the methods that are being used to investigate these problems, and the size and cost of the research efforts required. The field visits also permit the Task 

Force to obtain the views of those directly involved in seeking new ways to supply non-polluting energy forms. 

The Task Force intends to issue a series of committee prints which will summarize the material presented at meetings of the Task Force. 

It also expects to issue appropriate reports covering priorities for energy research and development. 

The Republican Task Force on Energy and Resources, established by the Republican Research Committee of the House Republican Conference, was charged with "taking a long-range look at the increasingly serious energy problem in the United States, taking into consideration the associated problems of environmental protection, economic growth, and fuel supply."

The Republican Task Force is composed of 16 House members representing different geographic areas of the country. In addition to organizational meetings, the Task Force held seventeen meetings between May and October 1971. Speakers included representatives of the major executive departments with energy responsibility (Depart ment of the Interior, Environmental Protection Agency, Atomic 

11 Congressional Record, Daily Edition, May 26, 1971, p. H4390. 

12 Id.

Energy Commission, Federal Power Commission), major trade associations, conservation groups, consulting firms, universities, and financial institutions.

The major topics discussed have been natural gas supply problems, energy consumption and GNP, future energy demand, atomic energy, petroleum issues, coal availability, new energy sources, and the impact of energy on the environment. The Task Force is planning several field hearings in the future.

After its most recent meeting the Chairman of the Republican Task Force, Rep. James McClure of Idaho, commented that the major need at the Federal level was "an energy and fuels program enabling the government to point out the consequences of short-term, spur-ofthe-moment solutions to long-range, wide-impacting problems." While this comment was specifically addressed to the government policy for handling shortages, it appears that the Task Force is moving toward the consideration of the need for a national energy program and an examination of what form the program should take. 

The House Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs announced on October 27, 1971 that it would be scheduling hearings on the subject of the "Nation's Ability to Meet Escalating Demands on Its Energy Resources." A budget for staff and other expenses has been approved by both the Interior Committee and the Committee on House Administration, but the number, location, and starting date for hearings remain indefinite. It has been reported that the proposed hearings will concentrate on the next fifteen years rather than on the longer term supply demand requirements.

II. Appropriations 

The wide spectrum of Federal activities that bear on energy policy are not handled in any single House and Senate appropriations measures. A major problem of appraising energy appropriations is defining the ubiquitous term "energy". Some programs or functions are obviously energy-related; these include the Federal Power Com mission, the Office of Coal Research, and the Bonneville Power Ad ministration. Other agencies, however, have programs with dual responsibilities, only some of which are related to energy. The Bureau of 

Reclamation and the Corps of Engineers, for example, are actively involved in energy production yet each agency is primarily charged with other functions.

One problem relative to budget estimates is created by those agencies whose functions are only indirectly related to energy production, transportation or utilization. The Interstate Commerce Commission, for example, regulates selected surface transportation activities (especially railroads) which can have a significant impact on the production and distribution of energy resources. While appropriations to these agencies are not directly stated as being related to energy activities. they could be included to give a fully accurate picture of the total number of agencies involved in energy matters. 

Table 1 attempts to resolve these problems as follows: The table has been divided into three parts: Part A, provides capital expenditure appropriations; Part B gives the funds appropriated for operations, regulation, research and development, and other noncapital related appropriations; Part C includes appropriations for agencies having an indirect impact on energy activities. The table presents the appropriations awarded for fiscal year 1972.13 In all cases, an attempt was made to estimate only that portion of the total appropriation which was related to energy activities. The Corps of Engineers, for example, had a total appropriation of $927,926,000 for general construction. Of this total, budget officials identified only $330,644,000 as being directly related to energy_production. 

Several conclusions regarding the Federal Government's role in energy activities can be drawn from the table. The first involves distribution of funds. Nearly seventy percent of the funds appropriated for all energy purposes go to the large capital expenditure items shown in Part A. One-third of these funds go toward the payment of special benefits to coal miners resulting from Title IV of the 

Federal Coal Mine Health and Safety Act of 1969. This title of the 

Act provides monthly benefits to coal miners who are totally disabled due to pneumoconiosis (black lung disease) contracted in underground coal mines and to widows of coal miners whose deaths resulted from the disease. 

18 In selected cases, appropriations were not available and FY 72 budget requests were substituted. (15)

The remainder of the items in Part A (with the exception of ship construction subsidies) are all indirectly related to the generation or transmission of electricity. This results in over sixty-three percent of the capital expenditure funds being awarded to electricity uses. 

Part B of the table presents items of a continuing nature-appropriations for operations, maintenance, personnel, regulation, research and development. As in Part A, two items account for a majority of the appropriations. In this case, the Atomic Energy Commission's and the Department of the Interior's activities represent the major source of the Federal Government's activities. In fact, forty-three percent of all the funds appropriated for energy operation, regulation, research and development go to the R & D program of the Atomic Energy Commission; the addition of the production of nuclear material and regulatory functions raises the percentage to 50 percent. 

The predominance of funds for electricity generation seen for Part A also persists if Table 1 is viewed in the aggregate. If all the appropriations are divided into either electricity or non-electricity items, the percent appropriated to each is 70 percent and 30 percent, respectively. Thus, nearly seven of every ten dollars appropriated for energy activities in fiscal year 1972, went toward the production, regulation, operations or transmission of electricity. This is surprising since the generating capacity of Federally-assisted utilities amounts to only 20 percent of the total generating capacity for all electricity. 

In addition, electricity generation represents only 25 percent of the total demand for fuels in the Nation.

Table 1 also shows the imbalance of various Federal expenditures among the fuel forms. In 1970, nuclear energy contributed only 1 percent of the total energy supply of the Nation, yet received 49 percent of all funds included in Part B of Table 1. Petroleum and natural gas, which contributed 75 percent of the supply of energy in 1970, received only 2 percent of Part B funds. Coal, by far the largest fossil fuel resource and one which the Nation will rely on for many years, received only 16 percent of the Part B funds. Similarly, new and unconventional energy sources received only 2 percent of these funds.

Revenue Act of 1971 III. OTHER LEGISLATION 

On December 10, 1971, the President signed Public Law 92-178, the

Revenue Act of 1971. The Act was a comprehensive measure designed to provide job development, investment credit, reduce certain excise taxes and provide tax incentives for contributions to candidates for political office.

Several provisions contained in the Act had a direct impact on energy production and development. One provision increased the investment credit for public utility property. Under prior law, a 3 percent investment credit was provided for public utility property which includes property to furnish or sell electricity and gas. Both the House and the 

Senate felt that an increase in the investment credit to 4 percent was justified because of, as the Senate report (S. Rept. 92-437) stated: the increasing problem many utilities are encountering in raising the capital required for modernization and expansion. Additionally, the regulated companies are encountering increased competition from other regulated companies and, in the case of many of their products, from unregulated companies as well. 

In view of these factors, the committee agreed with the House that it was appropriate to lessen the difference between the credit allowable for public utilities and for taxpayers generally.

The conference committee also accepted a Senate floor amendment which allowed a regulated utility to use the option of "flow through" in its financial reporting of new income. Under the Revenue Act of 

1964, Congress provided that in the case of the investment credit on public utility property (the 3 percent property), no Federal regulatory agency could, according to the House report (H. Rept. 92-533): "flow through" the credit to income [of a regulated company] more rapidly than ratably over the useful life of the property. In the case of any other regulated company's property (the 7 percent property-chiefly, the interstate gas pipelines), no Federal regulatory agency could flow-through to income any part of the credit.

The Revenue Act of 1971 changed this system and allowed utilities to flow through the credit to income in certain instances. 

The Act also contained a provision to allow investment credit for property used in resource exploration in "international or territorial waters." Since the inception of the investment credit in 1962, it has been generally provided that property which is used predominantly outside the United States is not eligible for the investment credit.

Before the Revenue Act of 1971, an exception was allowed for property of a United States company which was used for the purpose of exploring for, developing or transporting resources from the Outer Continental Shelf. In view of the fact that a substantial amount of offshore drilling activities will, in the future, be taking place in foreign waters and because of the increasing demand for oil, both the House and the Senate committees provided that equipment of this type be eligible for the credit. (19)

Atomic Energy Commission Authorizations The Atomic Energy Commission's authorization for appropiations for fiscal 1972 (P.L. 92-84) contained an important provision concerning the environmental research and development responsibilities of the AEC. The provision is contained in Section 201 of the Act and authorized the AEC to conduct research and development activities relating to "the preservation and enhancement of a viable environment by developing more efficient methods to meet the Nation's energy needs."

Under existing authority (42 U.S.C. 2053), the Commission provides non-nuclear research for others in the fields of public health and safety which includes environmental matters. This research for others is provided on a reimbursable basis and is subject to statutory limitations which provide a showing that the AEC's special competence is needed by those seeking research assistance. More importantly, present authority prohibits the AEC from conducting in its own facilities for its own purposes, research and development activities into nonnuclear missions.

The Joint Atomic Energy Commission, in light of the increasing concern for the environmental impact of nuclear power, viewed the restrictions on nonnuclear research as unnecessary. The House report (H. Rept. 92-325) stated, for example:

The national laboratories of the AEC are major national assets which were created, exist, and are needed for AEC's nuclear missions. These laboratories are staffed by outstanding scientists in both the physical and life sciences and they are equipped with facilities that are unique in many respects. The amendments would allow the Commission to use these national laboratories, either for its own account or for others, to assist in the balanced and imaginative research and development efforts which are needed for the Nation to continue to know the blessings of both a high-energy civilization and a beautiful and healthy environment. Fields such as underground electric transmission and magnetohydrodynamic power cycles are illustrative examples of the variety of research projects which could be undertaken by AEC in these national laboratories.

COMMITTEE HEARINGS I. Energy Policy Issues (A) The President's Energy Message 

The President's energy message of June 4 (Appendix H) was the subject of a hearing held on June 15, 1971 before the Senate Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs. Administration officials who were intimately concerned with the preparation of the message appeared as witnesses. These included Dr. Paul McCracken, Chairman of the Council of Economic Advisers, the Honorable Rogers C. B.. 

Morton, Secretary of the Interior, Dr. Edward David, Presidential Science Adviser, Dr. Glenn Seaborg, Chairman of the Atomic Energy Commission, and General George Lincoln, Director of the Office of Emergency Preparedness. 

The objectives of the hearing were to review the scope of the President's message, to ascertain the administration's plans for implementing new energy programs, to identify areas of policy concern not covered by the message, and to discuss those national goals which should be advanced by a comprehensive energy policy. In opening the hearings Senator Henry M. Jackson, the Chairman, said:

I have reviewed the President's message and, for the most part, I concur with the objectives and the recommendations which are set forth. I was, however, somewhat disappointed with the scope of the message. In many areas, the actions which are proposed are indefinite. It is difficult to determine if any major changes in direction, in policy, or in magnitude of effort are intended. 

I am sure that the President's advisers and the members of the administration who contributed to the energy message have given substantial thought to the elements that ought to be a part of a national energy policy. As a part of today's hearing, we will want to discuss these elements. 

Dr. McCracken testified that the President's energy study was prompted by the occurrence of several short term problems including "brownouts", sharply rising prices for some fuels, a low level of stocks at utility plants, and shortages of gas in some areas. He explained that the longer term problems also studied by the Administration were the decline in domestic discovery of oil and natural gas and the impact of environmental considerations on energy supplies. The Administration's study first defined the goals of energy policy so that solutions to long term problems could be found. This in turn involved analysis of how much energy would be required by the nation, alternative sources of supply, and what the costs of production would be. Estimating future demand is difficult and should be continuously revised as new and better data become available. Energy policies must take account of the uncertainties of the supply-demand estimates. 

According to Dr. McCracken, an examination was made of all the potential sources of energy supply, both conventional and unconventional. The most promising sources were determined and ways to (21)

encourage their development were analyzed. Also examined were methods to eliminate wasteful use of energy. The President's message emphasized several direct ways to increase the supply of clean fuels including the development of the breeder reactor, acceleration of oil and gas leases on the Outer Continental Shelf, a leasing program for oil shale and geothermal resources, expansion of uranium enrichment capacity, and broader support for energy research and development. 

In addition, methods to reduce sulfur oxide pollution from combustion of coal, improved methods of power plant siting, conversion of coal to clean natural gas, better ways to insulate structures to reduce waste, a proposed sulfur tax, and the reorganization of the Executive Branch to administer energy problems more efficiently were proposed in the 

President's message.

During the hearing, there were a number of informational questions concerning the role of economic growth in energy demand and the viability of a policy of "no-growth" as an option to existing growth patterns. In response, Dr. McCracken stated that the evidence clearly suggests that the average consumer still has aspirations for a higher level of living which requires more energy. He contended, however, that if we are to achieve prudent economic growth to fulfill social, economic and environmental goals the consumer will have to pay increased energy costs. Other witnesses subsequently argued, that it may be possible to secure a higher quality of life with less energy and, in any case, there are opportunities to use less energy at no cost to economic expansion simply by reducing waste. 

A number of questions were asked concerning the problems of gross national product relative to energy consumption and the reasons for the recent reversal in the long term decrease in the GNP-Energy ratio. Additional questions included what impact an increase in the price of energy would have on consumer demand; the need for and acceptability of end use controls for energy to reduce shortages; and the role of the public in the decision making processes involving energy supply and demand. Dr. McCracken emphasized throughout the hearings that it is only possible to forecast a range of energy demands. It would be a mistake to base our policies on any rigidly estimated projections.

The possible role of Canadian fuel imports into the U.S. and the general attitude of Canada toward establishing a joint energy policy of benefit to both nations were examined by Committee members. 

Interior Secretary Rogers Morton argued that, based on the information that the Canadians were making available, the U.S. would have to continue to pursue a negotiated relationship with its neighbors. He also advised the Committee that the discussions on means of moving recently discovered Alaskan oil to market had, on his suggestion, included the option of a Canadian pipeline as an alternative to the proposed Alaskan pipeline. He noted, however, that the cost of a pipeline through Canada appeared to be nearly twice that of one constructed in Alaska. In addition, Secretary Morton noted, the feasibility of a Canadian pipeline would require extensive study and consequently a long delay, before an Interior Department permit could be issued.

Other details concerning the Administration's plans which were discussed during the hearings included nuclear stimulation for the recovery of gas in formations too tight to be economic; the state of

contingency planning for electric power shortages; the plans that the 

Department of Interior had made concerning the offering of oil shale lease; the position of the Administration on bills to ban strip mining; and a satisfactory research and development strategy for bringing a demonstration breeder reactor into operation. An issue raised concerning proposed breeder reactor research was the advisability of experimenting with two different breeder technologies-each tested on a smaller scale instead of the planned single plant approach which would use the liquid metal fast breeder technology. Dr. Seaborg of

AEC responded that the cost of the research program for a single approach was so high as to preclude trying to test two different technologies. 

A number of questions were raised concerning omissions in the President's message. It had very little to say, for example, about various policy issues relating to oil supply. Particularly important to the Committee was the lack of information on ways of revitalizing the search for and development of domestic oil and gas; the lack of discussion on the importance and methods of achieving security of supply; of methods of achieving domestic self-sufficiency in a reasonable time frame; the importance of finding ways to reduce dependence on foreign oil; and the impact of oil imports on cur balance of payments. 

Senator Clifford P. Hansen in commenting on the President's message said:

Independent producers, the backbone of the oil and gas exploration industry, would feel better had the President made some positive reference to an all-out effort toward self-sufficiency in both oil and gas rather than the shin-plasters of facilitating imports of both natural and liquefied gas. 

And the independents would have felt a little more secure had the President mentioned the oil import program which is still under constant attack by those who would sacrifice national security and long-term energy guarantees for the temporary and insecure benefits of lower-priced foreign oil. 

Senator Jennings Randolph commented that severe problems would be created by our increasing dependence on foreign sources. He suggested that consideration be given to an end use policy for specific fuels, and that new methods be sought for the orderly development of our indigenous potential of undiscovered resources. He also expressed the view that the most important element missing from the message was full consideration of the imports issue. 

Senator Frank E. Moss expressed surprise that there was no reference in the message to the concentration of ownership of energy resources and production in view of the extensive amount of attention this issue had received in various forums. Dr. McCracken responded that since considerations of anti-trust and the preservation of competition are very important in the energy area the full force of government will continue to be used to monitor these aspects of energy policy.

Senator Henry Bellmon was concerned that the message lacked solid encouragement for the domestic energy industry. He noted that there was no mention of an upward adjustment of prices or increased research expenditures for secondary recovery. He pointed out that if the price of natural gas is raised so that gas could be produced competitively by coal gasification (a proposal suggested by the President to provide additional supplies of gas) a very large amount of additional natural gas would then be found through increased exploration. The 71-900-72-3

Administration witnesses agreed that while new supplies could be found through increased exploration such new supplies would involve higher costs. The witnesses were, however, unable to quantify the actual cost increase.

Senator Bellmon noted that many Senators had hoped the President would give firmer indication of the nature of the energy crisis. He argued that the only short term answer is to develop new supplies from traditional sources. In order to assure the U.S. energy industry that its investments would not be adversely affected by high levels of oil imports, Senator Bellmon tried to elicit from Administration witnesses information on the level at which imports would endanger the Nation's security. One witness answered that he did not think that any arithmetical "peril point" had ever been set but that this problem was under constant surveillance. 

Committee Chairman Jackson closed the hearing by stating that discussions had been helpful in gaining a better understanding of the President's message but that the Committee would have to undertake fuller investigations of many unanswered questions and key policy issues. 

(B) Energy Goals Symposium On October 20 the Senate Interior and Insular Affairs Committee held a Symposium on Energy Policy as part of its broad study of energy problems. The Committee's ongoing study looks upon energy policy as not end in itself but rather the instrument to promote and obtain a wide range of nationally agreed upon social goals. National goals in and of themselves are an expression of the desires and aspirations of the American people. The test of energy policy, therefore, is the degree to which it contributes to the efficient achievement of these desires and aspirations.

Seventeen solicited papers were discussed at the Energy goals symposium. The authors represented the whole gamut of institutions involved in energy activities. Included were representatives from industry, academic institutions, trade associations, environmental groups, and other sectors concerned with the production, development, and use of energy. While there were differing views expressed with respect to present character of our national goals there was a surprising amount of agreement on the major categories of goals that should be given high priority.

In general most of the authors included as primary goals national security, economic opportunity, environmental quality, individual freedom, and human and social needs. In many instances different elements were included under these broad headings and were given varying priority rank and emphasis by individual authors. Discussion of economic opportunity, for example, included such diverse subjects as full employment, economic freedom, maintenance of a free market, a strong economy and a suggestion that economic opportunity would also involve the choice of the optimum rate of economic growth. The discussion of human and social needs included such topics as full development of human potential, opportunity for social progress, elimination of poverty, overcoming social inequities, freedom from poverty and hunger, and improving the quality of life. 

There was little explicit discussion in the prepared papers of the role that energy policy should play in achieving these goals. It was

generally accepted, however, that none of our society's major goals was attainable without an energy supply capable of sustaining industrial progress. Symposium participants also agreed that a dependable energy base is an essential element in assuring national security; achieving a high level of environmental quality; providing an atmosphere in which the individual has the maximum freedom of choice consistent with the freedom of others; and, attaining the wealth and developing the tools to meet man's other human and social needs. 

The symposium was largely directed towards seeking agreement on what goals were important rather than identifying specific strategies for achieving them. The choice of alternative routes to attain agreedupon-goals leads to different decisions depending on one's political and economic philosophy. Nevertheless some of the speakers did suggest the kinds of issues that would arise in the course of reaching a consensus on national energy policy which would enable the achievement of national goals at lowest cost and with minimum impact on the environment. 

Mr. Bagge of the National Coal Association observed that the relationship of national goals to energy policy cannot be a static concept. There have been remarkable changes in our national goals with enormous consequent impact on our energy policies. As an example of flexibility of national priorities, he mentioned the goal that was accepted by the National Power Survey in 1964-namely that of an integration of the Nation's power systems. Acceptance of this goal evaporated in one year as a result of the vast "blackout" in northeastern United States in the fall of 1965. The lesson to be learned from this example is that as national goals change abruptly energy policies be flexible enough to accommodate to the changes. 

Mr. Wright of the Humble Oil Co. noted that the fragmented and conflicting energy policies of the past are no longer justified. 

Laissez-faire must give way to a coordinated total energy approach. 

He suggested that the energy policy which would be consistent in meeting national goals would be one that assured an adequate supply of energy at a reasonable balance among cost, dependability, and environmental quality. The orderly development of our indigenous energy resources is essential to the attainment of other national goals. 

He cited the need to modify existing laws and regulations in order to achieve a balanced energy policy. Mr. Wright offered two specific examples; the Federal leasing policies on the Outer Continental Shelf and the limitations on oil shale development embodied in the Mineral 

Leasing Act of 1920. 

A number of symposium participants stressed the need for efficient energy use to conserve resources as well as reduce the negative environmental impact of inefficient energy consumption, As Mr. Carl 

Madden, Chief Economist of the Chamber of Commerce of the

United States said, "less than perfect utilization of energy leaves byproducts that can effect our environment, creating potentially serious imbalances in the ecosystem." 

Mr. Madden also observed that:

The issue of improving energy-conversion ratios, not only in fundamental massenergy conversions where modern physics has achieved triumphs, but also in end-use convertors such as ordinary machines, might be an appropriate subject of inquiry for a study of energy policy.

Other speakers addressed many specific energy issues and suggested possible routes for the nation to take in developing sound policies. 

These were related to energy R & D opportunities and incentives, the role of self-sufficiency in energy supply, the effect of energy policies on the balance of payments, the advantages to the nation of "energy companies" in providing increased competition, and the need for the government to provide guidance as to how to achieve a balance between environmental needs and resource requirements. 

(c) Cost and Adequacy of Fuel Oil-The Oil Import Program 

A series of hearings were held on Sept. 8, 9, 28, 29, and Oct. 14, 15, 19, 20, 1971 by the Subcommittee on Small Business of the State Committee on Banking, Housing and Urban Affairs to consider the effect of the oil import program on the adequacy and cost of residential and industrial heating oils.

The hearings concentrated principally on the impact of the oil import quota system on small business marketers and consumers; the effect of dual distribution of petroleum products at various marketing levels; the dependence of the New England area on petroleum products; the difficulties of obtaining adequate supplies of oil, and the increased costs at which supplies have become available. 

At the September hearings, testimony was received from representatives of fuel oil distributors and jobbers, two university economists, and four representatives of the petroleum industry. The four oil distributor representatives generally presented similar positions with respect to the major issues covered in the hearings. There was agreement, for example, that: (1) the New England area was dependent on supplies of oil brought in from other sections of the country, (2) although the oil import quota system was justified on the basis of national security a disproportionate share of the costs were borne by consumers in the New England area, (3) the increasing concentration taking place within the energy sector was unhealthy, and (4) deepwater terminal operators must compete with the same individuals who sell them products. Recommendations from representatives of the fuel distribution industries included: (1) Suspension of the requirement that the additional No. 2 fuel oil imports must originate in the 

Western Hemisphere; (2) an immediate confirmation that the temporary program for the additional No. 2 oil would be continued; (3) an increase in this temporary allotment from 40,000 to 100,000 barrels per day; (4) a decontrol of all No. 2 fuel oil imports in the near future; (5) a continuation of the decontrol on residual fuel oil and (6) a change in the basis on which allowable imports are calculated from domestic production to domestic consumption.

The two University witnesses agreed that the oil import program should not be considered in isolation from State-controlled, marketdemand prorationing. For reasons of national security it would be necessary to protect the domestic petroleum industry. One of the witnesses, Dr. Houthakker, suggested that the oil import program may not be the only or the best way to achieve this purpose. He noted that the majority of the Cabinet Task Force on Oil Import Control had concluded that a tariff would be superior to a quota system. However, since he did not believe it was possible at this time to alter the policy which leads to market-demand prorationing, he suggested four measures that could probably be adopted immediately to improve the

performance of the quota system. These were: better policy making procedures; a more direct method of relating the program to national security; the sale of import tickets rather than just exchanging them; and an improvement in the method of treatment of petroleum products. under the quota program. Dr. Houthakker outlined detailed implementation proposals for each of these improvements. 

The second University witness, Dr. Erickson, formerly a consultant to the Cabinet Task Force on Oil Import Control, concurred in the assumption that an import control program should be taken as a "given" for the present. He suggested that in the future consumers should have an opportunity to help shape the program and that those with the responsibility of administering it should consult with other government agencies holding major responsibilities in the energy field. 

With the world supply of oil adequate to meet world demand for the foreseeable future, and economies of scale in transportation still to be realized, he predicted dramatic increases in imports to the U.S. during the next decade. Much of this will come from the Middle East-the amount mitigated to some extent by imports from Canada and oil from Alaska. 

Dr. Erickson would permit residual oil to be imported without restriction into Petroleum Districts 2 to 4 just as it is now imported into District 1. Dr. Houthakker had reservations about such a program because it would result in a further imbalancing of refinery capacity. 

Both witnesses agreed that the program now in operation in District 5, where imports are related to demand and production, would be superior to the quota system presently used elsewhere in the Nation. 

This change would, in effect, remove from the quota system the arbitrary limitations imposed by the requirement that imports must be equal to a certain percentage of domestic production. It would also result in larger quantities of oil being imported. Both university witnesses suggested elimination of the Western Hemisphere restrictions on the extra No. 2 oil imports. This would allow U.S. importers to turn to the Middle East for these supplies, would increase competition, and would not materially affect security of supply. Dr. 

Houthakker also questioned the validity of a long-held assumption that Venezuela is a secure source of supply, in view of that government's actions of imposing a retroactive tax and various nationalization measures.

The three oil industry witnesses agreed that there should be no further increase in No. 2 oil imports; that it is undesirable to export refining capacity; and that the oil industry has the ability to meet all the No. 2 oil demands of the coming winter. The representative of Humble Oil Co. stated that the nation's excess refining capacity stands at about 12% although excess capacity along the East Coast had declined to some extent in the last ten years. As a result, he did not feel that it would be necessary to increase No. 2 oil imports. The pressure on oil supply was said to be the result of a rapidly growing demand for energy, delays in the construction of nuclear power plants, the environmental constraints imposed on the use of coal, and the limitations on the supply of gas. 

The difficulties associated with planning new refineries were discussed. In addition to the very difficult problem of siting refineries in accordance with standards of environmental quality, it is not possible to design refineries with the proper product mix unless reasonably

long term import policies are announced. Without definite knowledge of which products will or will not be subject to controls and the approximate levels at which controls will operate it is impossible for the companies to plan new refinery capacity. Efforts to meet fuel oil needs are impaired further by the seasonal nature of the market. This causes logistics problems in production and in providing suitable storage capacity. In summary the Humble Oil Co. witness said: (1) there will be sufficient supplies of No. 2 fuel oil this winter: (2) the

U.S. needs a policy which will assure minimum dependence on insecure foreign sources; (3) further expansion of product imports will have the undesirable effect of exporting refining capacity; and (4) giving preferential treatment to any segment of the industry or any part of the country can only lead to a further deterioration of the import program. 

The representative from Texaco, in addition to discussing why no additional No. 2 oil imports would be needed, proposed a method for increasing the amount of low sulfur residual. This method consisted of offering incentives to those who would refine low sulfur domestic crudes and produce low sulfur residual oil. He also discussed the broader aspects of domestic oil supply problems.
