He stated that the oil import program was designed to protect the security of the nation and had achieved this objective by preserving a strong domestic industry; by deterring foreign producing countries from threatening the U.S. with supply disruptions; and by providing the domestic industry with economic incentives to explore for and develop new reserves. If oil import controls were removed it would have a significant adverse effect on the Nation's balance of payments. However, the witness suggested that future changes in the oil import program may be desirable. In view of the large number of factors that prevented the adoption of the tariff alternative to the quota system, it was proposed that a flexible program that would not deviate from the primary purpose of preventing an impairment of national security be devised. 

Finally, the Texaco representative denied that the retail prices of 

No. 2 fuel oil had escalated dramatically and supplied data for the record to support this view. 

The Shell Oil Company representative offered the suggestion that any action taken on either the supply or price of fuel oil should be done in the context of the long-term effects on overall energy supply and security. For the short term, only oil can meet the demand that the other fuels will be unable to satisfy for a variety of reasons. What is urgently needed is a stable, equitable and reliable import program. 

If No. 2 fuel oil imports are increased, the excess domestic capacity will not be adequate to offset the impact of unusually severe weather conditions or a major disruption in fuel oil imports. This domestic capacity can be protected only if the price of fuel oil reflects its true economic value.

The Director of the Petroleum Industry Research Foundation, an organization which conducts economic research for the oil industry and which is supported by major oil companies, refiners and independent cargo buyers, testified that the oil import policy has had little effect on the domestic residual oil output. The witness concluded that there was not sufficient evidence to justify an increase in distillate oil at this time. If domestic suppliers should be unable to meet demand

then corrective action would be necessary. He also suggested that, to assure access to supplies by all marketers, the import quotas to independent cargo buyers be continued along present lines but that this import quota be changed to a fixed share of the total market. 

This would result in an increase at this time of from 40,000 to 45,000 barrels per day.

Field hearings were conducted at Boston on October 14 and 15 to obtain information on the effects of the oil import program in New England. In opening the Boston hearings, Chairman McIntyre cited statistics indicating that the national per capita cost of the oil import program was $24 while the cost to consumers in New England varied from $35 in Massachusetts to $45 in Vermont. Both Massachusetts Senators and a member of the House of Representatives testified, along with the Governors of the States of New Hampshire, Maine, and Massachusetts. Senator Brooke pointed out that the higher oil prices paid by 

New England are not caused by the workings of a free market but by the Federal Government administration of the oil import program. He favored continuation of the free importation of No. 6 fuel oil; complete decontrol of imports of No. 2 oil; elimination of the restriction on crude oil and finished products from Canada; and an increase in the level of crude oil imports, particularly on the east coast. 

Senator Kennedy quoted the report of the Cabinet Task Force on 

Oil Import Control: 

The fixed quota limitations that have been in effect for the past ten years and the system of implementation that has grown up around them, bear no reasonable relation to current requirements for protection either of the national economy or of essential oil consumption.

He suggested that the failures of the program could be corrected by: (1) increasing the level of imports to 20% from the 12.2% currently in effect; (2) immediate decontrol of crude oil and products from Canada; (3) immediate establishment of a Western Hemisphere energy free trade area; (4) repeal of the Connolly Hot Oil Act; (5) development of 

Alaskan reserves; and (6) a complete overhaul of the administrative structure and procedures relating to Federal oil import policies. He also proposed four action programs specifically designed to fill New 

England's needs.

The Governors of the three States described the disadvantages of the present program in New England and suggested that the oil import program be abandoned. Development of a national energy policy that would more equitably balance the needs of the consumer States with the requirements of the producing States was proposed. The three 

Governors echoed the suggestions to improve the oil supply situation for New England made by other public witnesses. In addition, the following actions were proposed: investigation of the market practices of the major oil suppliers in the area; investigation of the "staggering" increase in the price of oil in the last three years; initiation of a rollback on oil prices; and limited action at the State level to provide a useable data base for informed action.

A representative of the Public Service Co. of New Hampshire outlined the electric utility companies' reliance on imported residual oil. This dependency on the "whims of foreign governments and whims of foreign tankers" was "bone chilling" to the witness. As a result of the change in fuel patterns in the region the utilities presently use only one fuel and the fuel supplier is now able to pass along all

his risks to the consumer. A representative of the Public Power 

Association noted that, although no actual shortages occurred in the previous winter, prices had increased sharply. These sharp rises have not receded and the witness questioned whether supplies and prices had in fact been manipulated by producers, shippers, and suppliers during the previous winter season. The extra 40,000 barrels of No. 2 oil helped competition at the wholesale level but did not lower prices to consumers. The witness recommended that the Connolly Hot Oil Act be repealed and that import quotas be removed.

The Executive Vice President of the New England Council for Economic Development, representing a non-profit organization devoted to strengthening the economy of the six-State New England region, urged that the import allocation on No. 2 fuel oil be raised from 40,000 to 100,000 per day; the restriction on Western Hemisphere sources be removed; long term special quotas be made available to the region, and the incentive-bonus system used in Petroleum District 5 be made applicable to the New England region. He argued that a great number of appeals had been made to the Executive Branch without obtaining any long term relief and suggested that action could only be expected from the Legislative Branch. 

The Administration views on this subject were given by Assistant 

Secretary of the Interior Dole and George A. Lincoln, Director of the Office of Emergency Preparedness, on October 19 and 20. In opening the hearings the Chairman summarized the testimony that had been received in the previous hearings. He said:

The testimony received by the subcommittee clearly indicates that the mandatory oil import quota system as presently administered is an unwieldy and cumbersome mechanism that is in need of substantial revision and change. It makes no sense to me to continue to base this quota system on domestic production east of the Rocky Mountains at a time when domestic production is leveling off and demand is increasing at a rapid pace.

The quota system supposedly finds its justification in national security. However, its existence has neither stimulated production nor refining capacity at anywhere near the levels needed to meet this country's petroleum needs. 

The continuation of this program is justified by the administration on national security considerations even in the face of a Cabinet-level task force study that clearly showed this is false. What is needed is a complete overhaul of our Nation's petroleum policies beginning with the elimination of the mandatory oil import quota system and, if necessary, its replacement with a more equitable program such as a tariff. 

What is needed immediately, however, is for the President, as counseled by the oil policy committee and the Department of the Interior, to announce his program regarding residential and industrial home heating oil for this winter. Witnesses in Boston last week indicated that the delay in announcing this winter's import policy was creating insurmountable difficulties. The decision must be announced now, and if home heating oil is not going to be decontrolled completely along the east coast it should be increased this winter to a minimum of 100,000 barrels a day and the Western Hemisphere restriction completely removed. 

Secretary Dole repeated the opinion of many others who had appeared before the committee that there would be adequate supplies of heating oil for the coming season if there were no disruptions in foreign supplies and if the winter was not unusually severe. For the longer term he expressed great concern over the increasing reliance on foreign imports from distant and unstable sources. The Geological 

Survey estimates that there is much oil and gas still to be found in the

U.S. and this represents the best potential for increasing the availability of these fuels in the next 3 to 5 years. To assist in encouraging these developments the President's energy message laid new emphasis on leasing Federal lands on the Outer Continental Shelf. The Secretary predicted that, no matter how much is done to obtain additional domestic reserves, dependence on foreign supplies will increase, but "prudence dictates that we do what we can to limit as much as possible the growth of this dependence."

The Administration witnesses also clarified the intent of the new proposed regulations for residual oil imports into District 1 and assured the Chairman that the intent was not to limit imports but only to make the administration of the program more efficient. 

In response to suggestions for solutions to the nation's energy problems the Secretary stated that the recommendations of the President's energy message should help to provide answers. 

He also thought that removing the restrictions on importation of residual oil into Districts 2 to 4 would lead to a disruption of the nation's energy balance and that the best program would be one which permits abundant coal resources of the regions to play a major role in energy supply. 

Secretary Dole summarized the Departmental position by saying:

One final comment is in order. Restraints upon the trade are abnormal, almost always unpopular and they always have a cost. They can be justified only for the most cogent of reasons. The reason for oil import controls is that it is inimical to the national interest of the United States to permit itself to become beholden to any outside source for anything so critical to its welfare and growth as its energy supply, specifically its supply of liquid fuels. The resources exist to prevent this from occurring, if proper emphasis is given to their timely development. 

Currently, however, these resources cannot compete with imported petroleum, and if they are to be developed in time to prevent the United States from becoming irrevocably dependent upon foreign petroleum sources, then restraints must be continued upon the free flow of foreign oil into United States markets. Unless this is done on a consistent, stable, long-term basis, there will be little incentive for investment in conventional oil and gas exploration and development, and none at all for the development of synthetic fuels from coal, oil shale, or tar sands. 

Moreover, care must be taken as well to guard against the further export of refinery capacity, and to encourage the growth of domestic capacity for manufacturing heavy fuel oil which has all but disappeared. This can be done while assuring adequate supplies of all required products to all regions of the country. 

The critical point is that the oil import control program must continually be viewed in terms of its total impact upon the long-range energy outlook for the 

United States.

General Lincoln, Director of the Office of Emergency Preparedness, characterized the national security aspect of the oil import program as being "related to the indispensibility of petroleum to our economy, to our way of life and our strength as a nation". To maintain this concept it is necessary to: (1) maintain a satisfactory level of domestic reserves of crude oil supplemented by secure sources of foreign supply; (2) maintain spare productive and delivery capacity in times of need; (3) maintain refinery capacity in the U.S. sufficient to meet both essential civilian and defense needs as required; and (4) provide a petroleum industry in the U.S. with capacity to meet the nation's defense and essential civilian needs. 

General Lincoln noted that not all of the additional No. 2 oil allocated under the temporary program during 1970 had been utilized and his study of the situation indicated that the No. 2 fuel oil supply situation would be satisfactory during the coming winter heating

season. He explained that the increased price of No. 2 oil during the past two years was the result of price increases for crude oil and of increases in retail margins. The imported No. 2 oil has not been priced significantly lower than domestic oil due to high tanker rates and the rapid transition in both the international oil situation and the fuel oil situation. He also pointed out that in the New England area the economic impact of residual oil price increases would be ten times the impact of price increases for No. 2 oil.

In discussing the alternatives to the present quota system, General 

Lincoln recalled that at the time of the Cabinet Task Force report he had favored a tariff over a quota system. However, he now has grave concern over the possibility of operating this program using a tariff mechanism. This change in opinion came as a result of the tanker shortage in 1970 which forced the differential between the price of foreign and domestic crudes down to zero. Some changes in the quota system appear to be on the way but any action to move import levels from the nominal 12.2% to some higher value would need additional study. At some point, however, we would approach and then pass the "peril point" of our dependence on foreign sources. 

(D) Department of Natural Resources 

In April 1969 an Advisory Council on Executive Reorganization, better known as the Ash Council, was formed. Several major reorganization actions resulted from the Ash Council's recommendations.

These include: The Environmental Protection Agency, The Office of 

Management and Budget, and the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. To continue reorganization efforts, four bills 14 were introduced in both the House and Senate which called for a basic re structuring of the Executive Branch. Seven Executive departments- 

Agriculture; Interior; Commerce; Transportation; Labor; Health,

Education, and Welfare; and Housing and Urban Development would be transformed into four new departments Natural Resources, Economic Affairs, Community Development, and Human Resources. 

The Senate Government Operations Committee held three days of hearings on these proposals in May and June, 1971. A Subcommittee of the House Committee on Government Operations also held several days of hearings in June and July, 1971. From the standpoint of energy and fuels, the proposal of most interest is the Department of Natural 

Resources (DNR) measure (H.R. 6959 and S. 1431). The hearings were primarily concerned with the theory and general aspects of the reorganization. The only aspect of reorganization directly pertinent to energy and fuels was discussion concerning the Atomic Energy Commission. The AEC was cited as an example of an agency that would not receive the Congressional attention that it presently does if its functions were divided up and subordinated to other departments. 

Concern focused on the problem that fragmentation of AEC functions would create with respect to committee jurisdiction. This issue led to discussion of whether the Congressional committee structure should be realigned to match the proposed major Executive realignment. 

Fear was expressed that it might increase the relative strength of the Executive. There was also concern that relationships with Congressional committees might be complicated, especially in regard to appropriations. 

14 H.R. 6959 (S. 1431); H.R. 6960 (S. 1433); H.R. 6961 (S. 1432); H.R. 6962 (S. 1430).

The increase in size of the Departments was a controversial issue. 

Some Committee members felt that increased size would make sound administration more difficult. Comparisons with large industry organizations were often cited. Concern was also expressed that the 

Ash Council was too strongly business-oriented and did not comprehend sufficiently the differences between business and government. 

The Administration's reorganization plan is primarily goal-orientated. This is in contrast to the present functional orientation of many governmental units. According to the Ash Council report, goal orientation in Federal agencies would greatly facilitate the coordination and evaluation of efforts to achieve basic national goals and would foster a strong administrative commitment to those goals. 

One basic objective of the reorganization plan is decentralization. 

The Administration witnesses contended that the reorganization plan might make it easier to decentralize thus promoting more efficient administration and better local access to government. 

Witnesses also expressed the opinion that the reorganization plan would alter the relationship between special interest groups and the various departments. Those groups which had formerly been concerned with the functions of only one department, would find it necessary to deal with more of the various agencies and offices of several departments.

Witnesses also observed that the reorganization plan would alter the relationship between department heads and the President. The plan would give each department head a burden of decision making responsibilities on par with that of the President, and from the outset, each Secretary would be forced to resolve the conflicting functions of the agencies in light of the new department's basic goals. 

During the Senate hearings both the Administration's DNR proposal and S. 1025, a bill to establish a Department of Natural 

Resources and Environment introduced by Senators Moss, Metcalf and Case, were discussed. Senator Moss has been urging legislation of this type for the past several years. Senator Jackson, who served as Chairman for the June hearings, commented in his opening remarks that the concept of a Department of Natural Resources was not new. 

It had been recommended in 1949 by the First Hoover Commission and had been the subject of hearings four years ago.

Senator Moss's bill differs significantly from the Administration bill in that it includes regulatory as well as developmental functions within the proposed Department. Under the terms of this bill, EPA would be incorporated into the Department in order to coordinate the potentially conflicting objectives of resource development and environmental protection within a single department. The choice between maintaining EPA and the Department of Interior separate or combining them has been widely debated, especially in connection with the creation of the Environmental Protection Agency, near the end of 

1970. S. 1025 also differs in excluding the Bureau of Indian Affairs. 

As the first witness, Secretary of the Interior Morton expressed support for S. 1431 and specified five divisions to be included in a new DNR: (1) Land and Recreation Resources, (2) Water Resources, (3) Energy and Mineral Resources, (4) Oceanic Atmospheric and Earth Sciences, and (5) Indian and Territorial Affairs. Supporting testimony and description of effects on other departments were given

by representatives of the Department of Agriculture, Commerce and the Army.

The effect of the proposed reorganization on the Atomic Energy Commission was somewhat more controversial. Commissioner. Larson supported the proposal, saying that during the last quarter century

AEC had developed nuclear energy to the point where it could now be administered together with conventional energy sources. Commissioner Ramey, however, spoke against the proposed transfer of AEC functions to a Department of Natural Resources, stressing the importance of these functions and saying that at this critical time they could best be handled under the present structure. A letter from 

Senator Pastore, Chairman of the Joint Committee on Atomic Energy, also questioned the wisdom of the reorganization proposal as it applied to the Atomic Energy Commission. He felt that the separation of planning functions from the AEC would make its critical job in the coming years more difficult.

Associate Director Weber, of the Office of Management and Budget, testified that the reorganization plan was comprehensive but that each of the bills was separable and stood on its own merits. This is in contrast to implications in earlier hearings. He emphasized that this reorganization would not be merely bureaucratic box shuffling but would be a basic improvement of government by decentralization. 

(E) Nuclear Power Plants-Policy Implications of the Calvert Cliffs 

Decision 

The Baltimore Gas and Electric Company recently received AEC permits to construct nuclear power reactors at Calvert Cliffs on the Chesapeake Bay near Baltimore. Work on each reactor began prior to enactment of the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA). Al though the AEC had issued a construction permit for the power plant, it had not issued an operating license. A coalition of environmental groups interested in influencing AEC's compliance with Sec. 102(2)(C) of NEPA 15 went to court seeking to stop construction of the nuclear facility at Calvert Cliffs. The court ruled that AEC had not properly applied the NEPA in considering licenses for the project. The decision called for: 16 

1. Independent evaluation by AEC and balancing of certain environmental factors, such as thermal effects, notwithstanding the fact that other Federal or State agencies which have certified their own environmental standards are satisfied. In each individual case the benefits of the licensing action must be assessed and weighed against environmental costs. Älternatives must be considered which would affect the balancing of values. 

2. Independent substantive review of environmental matters in uncontested as well as contested proceedings by the presiding Atomic 

Safety and Licensing Boards. 

3. Consideration of environmental issues in connection with all nuclear power reactor licensing actions which took place after 

January 1, 1970, the date NEPA took effect. 

15 Sec. 102(2)(c) requires each agency to prepare an environmental impact statement on projects which are expected to have significant influence on the quality of the human environment. These statements are to be used in agency decision making. 

16 Calvert Cliffs' Coordinating Committee Inc.. v. Atomic Energy Commission. 449 F.2d 1109 (D.C. 

Cir. 1971).

4. NEPA review, and appropriate action after such review, for construction permits issued prior to January 1, 1970, in cases where operating licenses have not as yet been issued. 

On November 3 the Senate Interior and Insular Affairs Committee held hearings on the aftermath of the Calvert Cliffs decision at which the views of both industry and environmental groups were sought. 

In announcing the hearing in the Senate, Chairman Jackson, on 

October 19, 1971, indicated that the Committee would explore: 

1. Any potential immediate reduction in the adequacy and reliability of electric service; 

2. Any reduced capability for carrying the electric loads of dependent industrial, commercial, and residential users; 

3. The economic implications of requiring those facilities with operating licenses to delay operation; 

4. The effect of new requirements on overall licensing procedures; and 

5. The potential long-term conflicts between licensing procedures and other statutory policies regarding energy, the environment, and power plant siting. 

As well as the questions of: 

1. Whether the decision is consistent with the intent of the 

Congress expressed in the National Environmental Policy Act of 

1969; 

2. Whether the decision is consistent with water quality standard certification_requirements contained in Section 21 (b) 

of the Water Quality Improvement Act of 1970; and 

3. Whether the proposed new permit and licensing requirements are consistent with the Court decision. 

In testimony before the Committee, Senator Gravel concluded that the short-term implication of the Calvert Cliffs decision was a temporary moratorium on the construction and operation of nuclear power plants. The long-term implications might include an end of nuclear energy as a source of electricity supply. He urged that the 

AEC examine carefully any claims that a "power emergency" exists which would result in a circumvention of the decision. 

Russell Train, Chairman of the Council of Environmental Quality testified that the Calvert Cliff's decision 1) strengthened the prospect that government will take citizen concern and participation into account before reaching important decisions 2) all government decisions would be subject to the type of environmental review required by NEPA, and 3) in the future the balancing of economic and environmental costs would be a part of the NEPA process. 

Mr. Train expressed the opinion that the NEPA review has not yet affected the reliability of electric power generation and would not be a factor in power availability in the 1971-1972 winter. He recommended that: 1) the new type of statements required by AEC by examined carefully, 2) a careful surveillance on the pace of AEC hearings on the issues be maintained, and 3) the AEC method of determining emergency procedures for partial plant operation be checked. He also underscored the need for prompt action on the 

Administration's Power Plant Siting Bill now before the Congress, as a way of meeting the long term problems associated with the siting of electric facilities.

AEC Commissioner Doub testified that, as of November 3, 1971, the Calvert Cliffs decision affected 65 construction and operating license applications for 97 nuclear plants with 87,000 MW of generating capacity. The AEC, instead of appealing the Court decision, immediately revised its rules for implementing NEPA. The holders of construction and operating permits are being required to submit statements as to why their permits or licenses should not be suspended pending compliance with the new requirements.

Commissioner Doub pointed out the potential conflict between the Calvert Cliffs decision and existing Federal Water Quality legislation, and urged that this be corrected by new legislation. He also estimated that over 200 members of the AEC are involved in the effort to comply with the Calvert Cliffs decision.

One witness, representing both the Western Systems Coordinating Council and the Atomic Industrial Forum, stressed that legislative action is needed to insure reliable electricity in the Western Systems area. He estimated the cost of an average of one year delay on all the nuclear plants affected by the Court decision at between $5 and $6 billion and advised that redundant regulations should be eliminated promptly if the industry is to serve the customer properly. For the short term the following changes in NEPA were suggested: 

(1) Elimination of duplicative review; 

(2) Requirement of cost/benefit analysis only where there is failure to comply with environmental regulations; (3) Provision of guidelines as to what constitutes a major federal action affecting the quality of the environment. 

The Atomic Industrial Forum concurred in this position and suggested that the court decision would 1) impair Federal-State relations, 2) cause extensive delays, and 3) increase lead times for plant planning.

As a result, the Forum recommended that, in the short term, the affected agencies give highest priority to eliminating or reducing delays, establish a procedure for plant operation during the required environmental review, and provide more explicit guidelines on what is required.

The witness from the Edison Electric Institute (representing investor-owned utilities) also thought that potentially serious delays would occur in the near future and that the costs of the delays could result in as much as a 5% increase in electricity rates. He suggested that serious power supply problems could occur in nearly all of the power pools this winter. To remedy this situation EEI suggested that 

AEC and the Federal Government: 

(1) Expedite public hearings; (2) Parallel review of safety and environmental issues; (3) Define scope of cost/benefit analysis; (4) Provide for provisional operation; (5) Permit interim licensing; (6) Secure congressional guidance on interpretation of NEPA; 

(7) Consolidate environmental review. 

The representative of Consolidated Edison Company of New York did not interpret the Court decision as requiring that the AEC proceed in the manner that it had. He proposed that a more reasonable interpretation of the decision would have permitted a plant to operate provisionally while a new environmental review was undertaken by the AEC.

Because the legislative history of NEPA provides only general guidelines as to the application of NEPA to licensing procedures, the witness recommended that hearings be scheduled to consider the need for amendments to NEPA to provide more detailed guidelines. 

This witness outlined a number of policy questions relating to the intent of Congress in the administration of NEPA that might be explored in future hearings.

A representative of consumer groups testified that the problem of the Calvert Cliffs decision was related to the long history of judicial frustration with the operations of AEC and the indutsry it regulates. 

While procedural due process is time consuming, the delays could be greatly reduced if the public had access to the data on which the decisions were based. Early participation by intervenors was suggested as a remedy. In return the intervenors would be expected to specify in advance the issues they intended to raise. Thus both parties would be able to present in advance detailed statements of their positions as well as the issues that would be covered, and the entire hearing process could be greatly accelerated. This witness said that in his view the 

Court decision is not yet responsible for the inability of any utilities to meet peak demands. 

(F) Potential Shortages of Energy Resources 

The Defense Production Act of 1950 was enacted to ensure that in the event of an attack, the United States could be quickly mobilized to produce sufficient materials for national defense and essential civilian needs. A basic problem is that the U.S. is a heavy net importer of raw material resources. In addition to planning for emergency mobilization, the Act also makes an effort to limit dependence upon foreign supplies so that their termination would not cause too great a disruption in our economy. As far as fuels are concerned, this threat of supply termination primarily involves oil and gas. The U.S. is presently a net exporter of coal. Although the U.S. does not depend on foreign sources for its supply of uranium, the expectation of its increasing importance as a fuel makes it also a subject of future concern. 

At hearings held before the Joint Committee on Defense Production, Assistant Secretary of the Interior Dole, representing the Administration, stated that it would be impossible for the U.S. to be self-sufficient in obtaining mineral resources. It is necessary therefore to seek access to world supplies on a mutually favorable basis. He noted, however, that it would become increasingly difficult to obtain minerals from foreign sources because the mineral demands of other countries are rapidly increasing. 

Mr. Ricca, Acting Director of the Office of Oil and Gas predicted increasing dependence upon foreign sources for supplies of oil and gas. 

Most of the oil that would be imported would come from the Middle East where there is reason to fear that the countries of the recently formed OPEC (Organization of the Oil Exporting Countries) block might unilaterally shut off supplies to the U.S. Mr. Ricca also attributed possible shortages of refining facilities to problems of siting due to environmental concerns and uncertainty of future energy policies. He predicted possible future dependence on imported gas in spite of the large potential reserves in the U.S. 

A spokesman for the Commerce Department predicted an increase in our dependence on foreign fuels from 20% to 45% over the next

20 to 30 years. He cited five factors responsible for restraining the development of fuel supplies: (1) environmental demands for clean fuels; (2) changing petroleum policies in other countries; (3) concern for health and safety in coal mining; (4) diminishing reserves of natural gas; and (5) various limitations on the production of electricity from nuclear power plants. Three possible developments that could alleviate fuel shortages are commercial development of oil shale, more rapid development of offshore petroleum discoveries, and coal gasification.

Elmer Bennett of the Office of Emergency Preparedness (OEP) out lined four national defense objectives of a viable oil import control program: (1) Maintain a satisfactory level of domestic reserves of crude oil supplemented from secure sources of foreign supply; (2) Maintain spare capacity to produce and deliver crude oil when international factors disrupt supplies from other sources; (3) Maintain refinery capacity in the United States adequate to meet both defense and essential civilian needs in periods of disruption of normal world oil trade; and (4) Provide a healthy petroleum industry in the United States with the capacity to meet the Nation's defense and essential civilian needs at all times.

He went on to say that the mandatory oil import program, in operation for the last 12 years, is no longer adequate. He suggested that gas prices were being kept too low, which promoted waste and discouraged increased production of coal and domestic residual fuel oil sources. 

The OEP witness further concluded that: (1) the United States cannot avoid an increasing dependence on foreign fuel supplies in the short run; (2) the courses of action to avoid increased dependence on foreign sources include exploitation of conventional gas and oil, or uses of other resources such as coal or nuclear power, (3) given present energy demands, it will be necessary to develop new technologies such as coal gasification, breeder reactors, and oil shale.

In response to a question concerning actions that must be taken to avoid fuel and energy shortages in the next five years, the OEP witness replied that decisions must first be reached with respect to the acceptable level of dependence on foreign supplies, national policies to achieve sufficient domestic supply to avoid greater dependence on imports, and the balancing of energy demands and environmental concerns. The issues to be resolved once these policy decisions are reached include: (1) development of offshore oil deposits, (2) methods of bringing arctic oil deposits to the lower 48 States, (3) optimal use of naval petroleum reserves, (4) recasting of the oil import program to changing conditions, (5) improved techniques for clean combustion of domestic coal, (6) locational problems of power plants, refineries, and harbors, and (7)_objectives and practices of natural gas pricing. 

Frederick Wall of the American Chemical Society raised the issue of inefficiency in electric power generation and advocated that whereever possible fuels such as natural gas should be used directly rather than for producing electricity.

The Atomic Energy Commission (AEC) witness, Commissioner 

Wilfrid Johnson, divided that agency's activities into three areas: mining and beneficiation of uranium, fuel enrichment, and electric power production. In regard to mining and beneficiation he stated

that to meet the needs of the 1980's and 1990's it would be necessary to remove restrictions on enriching foreign ores and the disposal of excess AEC stocks. Commissioner Johnson explained that the intent of the fuel enrichment program is to: increase operating levels at diffusion plants, continue producing and stockpiling enriched fuel, and improve efficiency by employing the Cascade Improvement Program. 

Also, consideration is being given to sharing our gaseous diffusion enriching technology with other nations. A possible development that could bring increased efficiency in the use of uranium is the high temperature gas-cooled reactor. The possible development of fusion reactors was viewed as the solution to our energy problems but not until the 21st century. Mr. Johnson also noted that the AEC considers that the Project Plowshare experiments, to increase gas supplies by_underground nuclear explosions, have been quite successful. 

Federal Power Commission Chairman, John Nassikas stated: 

Under near term conditions the immediate and short range solution to our gas and electric energy problems are to insure the most efficient utilization of available natural gas supplies and electric generating capacity, wherever located and by whomever owned, to meet consumer demands at a reasonable price. 

The ongoing efforts of the FPC with regard to energy were described in detail in Mr. Nassikas' statement and other material submitted for the record. 71-900-72—4

II. Competition, Antitrust and Monopoly Issues 

(4) Concentration of ownership among raw fuel industries

A series of hearings to review concentration of ownership in the energy industries was held on July 12, 13, 14, 15, 20, and 22 by the

Subcommittee on Special Small Business Problems of the House 

Select Committee on Small Business. In previous hearings this subcommittee had recommended that a national energy policy be established. These earlier hearings also revealed that significant changes have taken place in the basic structure of the Nation's fuel industry since the mid-1960's. Traditionally, the fuel industry has been characterized by active interfuel competition involving distinctly separate lines of production and ownership in the areas of coal, oil, gas, and nuclear energy. However, with the rapid growth and expansion of "energy companies" in recent years namely, industrial aggregations formed by major oil and gas companies primarily through the acquisition of major coal producers and the development of large holdings in the uranium industry-the traditional lines of interfuel competition have been significantly altered. 

According to a 1971 study of market trends in the fuels industry, conducted by the American Public Power Association and the National 

Rural Electric Cooperative Association, in 1962 the 15 largest bituminous coal producers in the United States accounted for 40.9 percent of the industry's total output. The bulk of this share-31.5 percent-was accounted for by 11 major independent coal companies, with the remaining share, 9.4 percent, being produced by 4 large integrated industrial companies--including one oil and gas company. By contrast, in 1969 the study showed that the 15 major coal producers increased their share of total industry output to 53.3 percent; however, the composition of firm activity had changed markedly. The number of major independent coal producers, for example, dropped to only 5 and their share of total production had declined precipitously to 9.7 percent. 

The major integrated industrial companies produced 43.6 percent of the total, with 5 major oil and gas companies accounting for 23 percent of total bituminous coal production. This was, of course, in marked. contrast to the 2.1 percent market share held by only one major oil and gas company in 1962.

A study being conducted by the Federal Trade Commission also showed the extent to which the nation's major oil and gas companies had penetrated coal and uranium markets by 1970. Of the 25 major petroleum companies (ranked by assets), 12 had holdings in coal and 17 had acquired interests in uranium. These acquisitions were of various forms, such as the purchase of coal and uranium reserves, the buying of existing companies in other fuel industries and the establishment of new ventures either alone or jointly with other companies within or outside the petroleum industry. 

Particularly within the past two years, these market changescoupled with growing fuel shortages and sharply rising fuel prices—have, (40)

the record indicates generated, widespread concern among consumer advocates and business interests, large and small, who are heavy energy users. There are serious grounds for concern that this apparent trend toward extensive and widespread diversification into other fuel industries by major oil and gas companies-could quickly lead to excessive concentration in the fuels industry. As a result, the competitive structure of the industry could suffer from a decline in interfuel competition; artificial restraints could be readily placed on production to drive up prices and maximize "monopoly profits;" and badly needed technological advancement could be manipulated to serve the needs of controlling interests rather than the compelling needs of users in the marketplace.

Defenders of "energy companies", on the other hand, maintained that new patterns of "interwovenship" in the fuels industry would not lead to a decline in competition. In the case of coal production, they pointed to the fact that oil companies own a relatively smalĺ fraction of the total U.S. coal production capacity-slightly over 20 percent of total industry output in 1970. Oil industry representatives also pointed to their record of investment in new mines following mergers with coal companies as evidence that coal production is not being restricted to increase prices.

In a closely related area, concern was also voiced over the increasing concentration of power in the electric generating industry which has resulted in fewer privately owned firms (down from 1,000 in 1945 to about 300 in 1970) and overlapping directorships among utilities, the ranking firms that are the source of utility capital, and fuel supplies to the electric industry. The question of whether the pluralistic ownership now in being is desirable-a mix of Federally, cooperatively, and privately owned companies-has not been resolved. 

In October 1970, after Congressional urging, the Federal Trade 

Commission announced that it would make a study of the antitrust aspects of mergers in the energy field. The study was described by the 

FTC witness at the July Subcommittee hearings. It will cover the effect on competition of the acquisition of many coal companies by gas and oil companies and the relationship of these mergers and acquisitions to fuel shortages and price increases. The four major problem areas in the energy field, to be covered by the study, each of which has a direct bearing upon the degree of present concentration in the field, are: 

1. A careful investigation and definition of competitive market boundaries within the energy sector, focusing largely on inter-fuel substitutability within the various demand markets. 2. An empirical analysis of concentration and concentration trends within the energy sector, utilizing production and sales data, and examining the extent to which the use of regional geographic market dimensions are appropriate. 

3. A careful examination of the relationships between concentration levels within the energy sector and industry profitability, as a prime empirical indicator of the existence and application of market power. 

4. An inquiry into affects of structural change on new investment and research and development within the energy sector. . . 

None of the above studies will include an analysis of patterns of reserve ownership of fuels for in most cases the required data are not

available. However, in a parallel study the FTC is examining the question of reporting of gas reserve data. This is being done to determine whether there has been collusion or other unlawful activity in reserve data reporting. The reliability of this data was discussed extensively by witnesses at the hearings since the gas price rises allowed by the FPC were predicated on the existence of gas shortages. 

Witnesses representing FTC and the Department of Justice were queried extensively on the relationship of those two orgainzations in the field of antitrust and monopoly. The Committee was assured that there was extensive cooperation and that, through continual contacts, duplication of effort was avoided. There appears, however, to be no general rule as to which organization will be responsible for any given problem as it arises.

The Department of Justice witnesses explained their previous action in permitting Consolidation Coal Company and Continental 

Oil Co. to merge. They said:

We did not attack the acquisition of the Consolidation Coal Co. by the Continental Oil Co. for the following reasons. Only insignificant amounts of Continental's gasoline are sold in Consolidation's market area. Although natural gas and residual fuel oil-which Continental produces may sometimes compete with coal, Continental sold these products for use only in areas of the country in which Consolidation does not operate and Consolidation did not sell in areas in which 

Continental's products were sold.

Moreover, Consolidation and Continental are not significant potential competitors. Heavy residual fuel oil requires water transportation if it is to be shipped far from where it is produced. It is thus unlikely that Continental could expand its residual fuel oil sales into Consolidation's markets. Similarly transportation costs made it unlikely that Continental's natural gas would be sold in Consolidation's markets or that Consolidation's coal would be sold in Continental markets. 

We were, of course, fully aware that Consolidation was trying to develop a process for making gasoline out of coal. Even if it develops such a process, however, it is unlikely that it would have competed significantly with Continental. Consolidation operates primarily in Pennsylvania, West Virginia and Kentucky and presumably would sell gasoline in that area.

The more direct competition between coal and oil in specific markets, the existence of new technology for conversion of coal to oil or gas, and the heavy reliance of the electric utility industry on a single supplier if petroleum companies become "energy companies," would cause the Department of Justice to regard the merger of these two companies differently in 1971.

In response to questions raised by other witnesses during testimony as to why the Department had not called a Grand Jury to investigate the alleged price fixing in the coal industry during 1970 the witness said:

The complainants furnished little in the way of hard facts to support a price conspiracy charge beyond (1) an alleged statement by a National Coal Association vice president to the effect that "we raised the price of coal and you have to pay it"; and (2) the fact of the price increase itself. The violation of the law by coal producers was deduced from the essential and erroneous premise that in fact there had been no coal shortage in 1970 and that therefore the price rises could only have been the result of collusion. This premise was bolstered by an economic analysis prepared for the complainants, with which on examination we disagreed completely.

It was concluded that the 1970 price rises could be attributed to this interaction of supply and demand rather than to conspiracy. In the absence of any more persuasive specific evidence of conspiratorial behavior by coal producers, it was accordingly determined that no adequate foundation had been presented at this time on which to request authority for a grand jury investigation.

The representatives of the Public Power Associations raised a num ber of questions concerning the reasons for the precipitious rise in coal prices during 1970. Their studies supported the position that a 

Grand Jury should be impaneled to consider price fixing in the coal industry. In the face of the evidence presented to the Antitrust 

Division, the inaction by the Justice Department was said by one witness to strongly suggest that at least for the basic energy industries "antitrust is charade.

It was claimed that, along with price rises, a substantial change in bidding patterns occurred in 1970 with coal companies refraining from responding to invitations to bid. When they did bid, unlike past practice where companies had bid on the entire amount offered in the invitation to bid, they would only make partial bids.

A witness appearing for the Center for Study of Responsive Law contended that neither government antitrust activities nor regulation had posed a significant threat to the petroleum industry but that the increasing substitutability of competing fuels has caused problems. He claimed that the oil industry sought to meet the threat of competition from synthetic oil products made by independent coal companies by the acquisition of leading coal producers. He was critical of the FTC for dropping what he considered to be two of the most important aspects of any inquiry into concentration in the fuel industries the structure of reserve holdings and the long term supply situation, particularly as it is effected by numerous anticompetitive government interventions.

Witnesses representing the oil industry contended that the industry is not monolithic and that even the largest companies are not necessarily the leaders in all phases of the industry, i.e. production refining, transportation, and marketing. It was claimed that there was nothing "sinister" about oil companies acquiring coal reserves. All of the most respectable estimates of future liquid and gas demands predict that a large share will be supplied from synthetic products made from coal. 

In view of this, it was contended that it was natural for the oil and gas companies to obtain the energy resources that will be used in the future. There were differences of opinion expressed about whether the oil companies would have sufficient motivation to push the development of synthetic fuel technology since coal could then compete more effectively with oil.

Mr. Hardesty, formerly with Consolidation Coal Co. and now with 

Continental Oil Co., pointed out that since Continental had taken over Consolidation, production had increased over 25%, a much greater increase than for the industry as a whole, and that this rise in production has had a salutary effect on coal supplies and has helped to keep prices down. He made a distinction between the sharp price rises noted by other witnesses since that coal represented spot prices and only a small portion of the total coal tonnage sold. Long term contracts at lower prices kept the actual average price rise between 1969 and 1970 at 22%, a much lower figure than the 50 to 100% claimed by some coal purchasers. He gave us reasons for the price increase, general inflationary trends, the Coal Mine Health and Safety Act, new environmental demands, labor unrest, and the adverse economic effects that freight car shortages cause. The alleged change in bidding patterns was explained by the greatly increased demand and the shortage of supplies so that the companies could not respond to new bid invitations as they had in the past.

The hearings also covered many other issues associated with mergers and the growth of energy companies. An Atomic Energy Commission witness testified that it has a statutory responsibility to report to the Department of Justice any information on antitrust activities that it becomes aware of. The AEC has reported several such activities to the 

Department and these have resulted in both action and surveillance by the Department. The FPC witness warned that in examing concentration by large companies one must remember that the resulting economies of scale, and the superior management and know-how of these companies may assure greater supplies of energy and more competition in the market place. In addition, at least in the short run, the mergers of coal companies with oil companies had provided the coal industry with access to a very significant source of needed additional capital.

A series of questions were raised by Subcommittee members with respect to the size of energy reserves held by oil companies. Humble 

Oil Co. owns about 1% of the total recoverable coal reserves of the 

U.S. nearly 20 to 25% of which are on Federal lands. They also have extensive uranium reserves. The AEC stated that at the beginning of 1970 about one-half of all uranium raw material resources were held by companies whose major revenues were derived from oil. Based on current drilling activities this percentage can be expected to grow. 

However the AEC did not consider that this represented a serious problem because of both worldwide and local competition in the nuclear fuel business.

Natural gas reserves received the greatest attention because of the ongoing FTC study, the FPC study of natural gas, and the price rises that the FPC granted. The latter was done on the basis that natural gas was in short supply and that the price increases would be expected to result in bringing in new supplies. The decision to increase prices was based on reserve data supplied basically by the oil and gas. industry. The accuracy of this data was challenged by several witnesses and defended by the FPC. All of the FPC commissioners agreed that the reserve data were accurate enough to have been able to make a judicious decision about price. The American Public Gas Association 

(APGA) alleged that the shortage of gas is based on unverified reports of the American Gas Association, that the FPC decision to rely on AGA data and abandon fact finding on their own was a fatal error, and that staff documents of the FPC confirm the APGA contention concerning the unreliability of the AGA data. The APGA witness complained that the FPC has refused to reopen the hearing to reconsider the evidence. APGA contended that during the years when the industry was arguing most strongly for price increases, reserve additions took a sharp nosedive. More importantly APGA said the precipitous drop bears no relation to the drilling effort and pointed to this as evidence that the industry was withholding from market new gas discoveries.

A number of witnesses said that the shortage of natural gas had been caused by the low field prices set by the FPC. The low prices discourage investment in drilling and encourage greater use of gas.

Expanded gas usage was also brought on by the need to meet new environmental standards. The FPC pointed out that the price of natural gas is not only effected by FPC actions but by a number of other governmental policies. These include leasing policies of the Interior

Department, oil import quotas, and State prorationing of oil production.

Concern was expressed by some Committee members that the practice of joint ventures by major oil companies in bidding on Federal lands may restrict competition and that government policies and bidding procedures tended to keep out small producers. The bonus bids that are required presented a high barrier for initial entry of the small companies. Also discussed was the effect that the withholding from the market of coal and uranium reserves by the major oil companies would have on the price of fuels.

The importance of R & D in the determination of the real definition of "concentration and monopoly" was of great interest to Subcommittee members. With new technology currently being developed, it will be possible for vigorous cross-fuel competition to occur. Questions were raised, therefore, as to whether the oil companies had any incentive to bring this new technology to the point of commercial application. (This also involves the Federal coal research program since much of coal research has been federally financed.) The results of the Department of Interior's ten year program was examined and there were wide differences of opinion as to how successful the program had been. 

The Department said that, in view of the small funding that has been received, excellent progress had been made. Some members of the committee were concerned that the Department had not contracted for research with any independent coal company in view of the possible reluctance of oil companies to press vigorously for new conversion technology. This issue was raised because several of the Department's large contracts were with a company with both coal and oil interests. 

The Subcommittee issued a report (House Report No. 92-719) on

December 8, 1971 in which its findings were summarized and recommendations made for future action. The subcommittee concluded that there has been a growing trend toward concentration by competing fuel suppliers and that this may have contributed to the recent increases in fuel prices. This growing concentration could result in dwindling fuel supplies, maintainence of artificially high price levels, and a reduction in the number of competitors by merger, acquisition and bankruptcy. Should this happen the substitutability that once allowed the consumer to choose among fuels would be foreclosed in the highly concentrated market structure that was created. The Subcommittee observed that "the formulation and implementation of a national energy policy is imperative to the greatest utilization and conservation of our energy resources at reasonable price while giving full attention to environmental protection."

The full recommendations of the Subcommittee report are contained in Appendix I. The major thrust of these recommendations is that the responsible agencies should make an indepth study of the anticompetitive aspects of the energy industries with a view to preventing further concentration, reassessing the research and development programs underway, revising present leasing policies to encourage small producers, developing an equitable balance between environmental protection and energy resources development, and creating a central depository for energy information that would contain reliable data on which informed judgments could be made in the energy field. 

It also recommended that consumer-oriented organizations be given an opportunity to participate in government sponsored energy studies..

(B) Gasoline Marketing Practices 

It is estimated that 35% of the retailers in the gasoline field go out of business each year. Hearings held in past few years have shown that while the consumer may benefit by price wars in the short term any savings are ultimately lost in the long term. The structure of the oil industry is generally recognized to be the cause of this problem. Two solutions have been offered: The first would free the brand name dealer from the requirement that he obtain his supply from the parent company. The second suggestion is to divorce oil companies from ownership of retail outlets. 

On July 20, 21, 22, and 23 the Subcommittee on Antitrust and

Monopoly of the Senate Committee on the Judiciary conducted further hearings on gasoline marketing practices. The objective was to ascertain if the two suggestions cited above would lead to more competition and more independence for the retailer.

A witness from the Defense Fuel Supply Center of the Department of 

Defense opened the hearings by giving factual information on methods used by that agency in purchasing large volumes of gasoline for gov ernment use. The Defense Fuel Supply Center does not set specifica tions, but instead purchases to specifications set by others. Two levels of performance for gasoline are recognized and these are based on octane rating. A special-type purchase is made for the post office where the pattern of stop and go driving and idling causes excessive carbon deposits. In this case the gasoline is blended with a special detergent type additive. Samples are taken at various points in the supply system and tested for adherence to specifications. Very few complaints have been received from users that the gasolines bought in this fashion had not performed satisfactorily. All government purchases are based on specifications and if a gasoline offered by a bidder meets these specifications it is bought on a low bid basis. The use of additives is not required if specifications are met. The gasoline purchased for the post office with the detergent type additive is not priced higher than other gasolines. In response to a committee question, the witness said that the cost of all additives used in gasoline is a very small part of the total cost. 

Oil industry witnesses represented three major oil companies operating in national markets, one major company with a relatively limited sales area and one large independent refiner. Another industry witness represented a chemical company with a refinery at which the petrochemicals are removed from crude oil primarily to supply a feed for their chemical plant.

The major oil companies operating in national markets took very similar positions on the issues raised by the hearings. They maintained that gasoline marketing is highly competitive and has been for many years. The results of this competition have been of great benefit to the consumer. This competition has resulted in a downward pressure on prices so that gasoline prices have increased only 22.3% between 

1950 and 1970, while other commodities have increased 61.3% over the same period.

With respect to whether gasoline is a fungible product (one that can be mixed without any change in quality) the three majors agreed that it was not. They declared that there were real and significant differences among brands. These consisted of different performance

characteristics: volatility, specific gravity, effect on the type and amount of deposits formed, amounts of pollution emitted, and degree of vapor locking tendencies. The use of additives further enhanced, they contended, the differences between marketed gasolines and there are a wide number of additives used to overcome special problems. A large number of different blends can be made so that the manufacturing of gasoline is a choice of compromises. This is what makes each brand different from the others. Because of these differences the witnesses agreed that the proposal that each dealer be able to buy from any refiner that met certain minimum standards would not work effectively.

The three companies agreed that they would not permit a dealer to buy in this fashion and use their brand name. The major reason given for this view was that it would be impossible to maintain product quality. The use of additive compounds complicates the problem of treating gasoline as a fungible product. Each company has its own additive package that differs from the others. A suggestion that the additives be added by the retailer so that he could buy the basic feed stock from any supplier was rejected by the witnesses as being impractical because of the difficulties of adding the additives properly and the absence of appropriate means to maintain quality control. 

In view of this position a large number of questions were raised by Subcommittee members concerning the exchanges of gasoline among oil companies that is a common occurrence in the industry. The witnesses defended this practice as being only a small part of the total marketed; it is done for the consumers' benefit since it reduces transportation costs and thereby increases competition in any given area; and, it is considered "non-deceptive" in that the exchanges are made on the basis of specifications which are established by the buyer. 

Because of the sophisticated quality controls that large oil companies. maintain, exchanges among companies was not thought to be a sufficient precedent for allowing individual retailers to buy from any refiner that can meet a predetermined specification. 

The other major issue covered in testimony by the three major oil company witnesses was divorcement of the refiners from retail marketing. While each witness did not advance the same arguments against divorcement, the following points were made. Divorcement would have an adverse effect on the consumer because it would break the line of responsibility from the refiner to the consumer; raise problems for the consumer as to how to choose a reputable brand of gasoline; reduce the opportunity to construct facilities with high standards of quality; raise the prices to buyers, and reduce the incentive for refiners to make continual improvements in gasoline quality. 

It was said that the status quo was advantageous to the dealer since the major companies provided financial assistance, sophisticated training, advertising, and corollary assistance such as credit cards.

In addition, with the present prices for acquiring service stations only a few retailers would be able to invest in stations and this would put many independent dealers out of business. The witness from Humble 

Oil Co. said that divorcement would remove a strong competitive element, result in utter confusion in the industry, be a step backward in the trend of providing the consumer with improved products at reasonable prices, and decrease the incentive for product and services improvement.

In summarizing their testimony, the Humble witness said that the present competitive market system had served the consumer well. 

If the system is restructured to (1) treat gasoline as a fungible product, (2) require divorcement, (3) limit advertising, and (4) eliminate the benefits of exchange, it would be detrimental to both to the consumer and the small businessmen involved in gasoline marketing. 

Standard Oil Co. of Ohio has been a regional marketer-refiner. 

Although it is necessary for the company to make extensive exchanges in some areas it was contended that there is no deception of the public. Except for unplanned exchanges in emergency periods, all exchanges are to Sohio's specifications and they provide their recipients with gasoline that meets their specifications. As with the other oil company witnesses, the Sohio representative opposed divorcement on the grounds that it would have an adverse effect upon the company and the witness expressed bewilderment as to how it could be accomplished. Moreover, unless the company could be certain of assurances of outlets for its product it could not justify the construction of refineries costing between $100 and $200 million. The other reasons given by other witnesses for continuation of the present gasoline marketing methods were also supported by the representative of Sohio. 

The witness from Ashland Oil Co., a large independent refiner which sells largely to the private brand marketers, testified that independent refiners faced different problems than those of an integrated company. 

The independent company suffers severe hard ships when crude prices rise without any corresponding increase in product price. On the other hand, the integrated company is able to enjoy the benefits resulting from the increased price of the crude. This inequality occurs because of governmental actions such as allowing prorationing of crude oil production, Federal tax treatment of oil production, and the mandatory oil import. The handicaps that these policies impose on the independent refiner give the large integrated company enormous competitive advantages. For example, the independent refiner must purchase foreign crude at major oil company prices under conditions satisfactory to the international majors. To help save the independent refiner the Ashland Oil Co. recommended that: (1) Consideration be given to a fundamental revision in the present method of allocating offshore crude oil imports among refiners. (2) Elimination of quantitative restriction on imports of Canadian crude. (3) Strict adherence to the governing national security purposes of the oil import program. (4) Strict enforcement of the antitrust laws. 

The witness from Ashland Oil Co. supported the position of the major companies with respect to the advantages of exchange arrangements among companies and in opposing divorcement of refining from retailing. 

The Dow Chemical Co., 84% of whose sales of gasoline go to independent outlets using their brand name, agreed with the contention of the other witnesses that a brand name is important and that they would insist that anyone selling under their trademark meet the quality standards set by them. Under the arrangements that Dow has with its dealers, they are independent and in a position to change to another supplier. In contrast to difficulties that other witnesses said their companies would have in finding retail owners who could afford

independent stations, Dow said that many professional people invest in these stations and lease them to distributors on long term leases. 

A representative of the Williams Co., which owns pipelines transporting a wide range of refined petroleum products, described how a common carrier pipeline operates. Their pipeline is the largest one in the U.S. that carries common specification gasolines. The gasolines produced by different refineries are not separated as they are pipelined but are commingled, so that at the discharge end it is impossible to distinguish the source of the material. The pipeline company does require that gasolines that are shipped in it meet certain basic specifications before it will be accepted. Nevertheless this type of pipeline would lead one to conclude that there are little differences between the basic gasoline stocks, at least before the additive packages are added. 

A representative of the Center for Science in the Public Interest testified that based on their studies they agreed with the statement of a witness who had appeared in hearings held last year: "gasoline is treated commercially as a fungible product within the petroleum refining and transport network and then, by a grand hocus pocus, becomes a nonfungible product when it reaches the consumer." The witness contended that as things now stand the consumer is unable to make an intelligent judgment about the quality of his gasoline. It was said that the consumer would like to see some immediate changes for his own protection. These would include a systematic elimination of antiknock and other detrimental additives, minimum standards to protect independent dealers and consumers, and insistence that all claims in gasoline advertisements be substantiated. 

(c) Combination Gas and Electric Companies 

On May 11, 12 and 13, and June 15, 16 and 17, hearings were held on S. 403, a bill which requires that all combination gas and electric companies, except those owned by cooperatives, municipalities or the Federal government, be required to divest themselves of one of the two utility services. There are approximately 78 combination companies in the U.S. which market about 43% of all the electric power sold. The bill was described by its sponsors as being in the national interest, in the interest of the consumers of gas and electricity, and in the interest of national defense in that it would help to maintain a strong, competitive energy industry. It was also stated that monopoly in the energy industry was increasing and that efforts at the State level to increase competition had not been successful. 

The National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissions 

(NARUC) opposed the bill on the grounds that it was a simplistic approach to a very complex problem. The bill would substitute "a Federal meat axe solution to local regulatory judgment". The

State regulatory commissions were said to be in a better position to protect consumer interests since individual judgments are required on a case by case basis of whether a straight or combination company will be in the public interest.

The arguments in favor of retaining combination companies were: 

1) Economies of scale achieved through joint operations; 2) Single administrative services such as meter readers, joint accounting, etc., providing savings to the consumers; 3) Better financial stability through the diversification; 4) Quality service through providing unbiased information so that consumers can select the best fuel for

their purposes; 5) Greater efficiency through advocating the use of energy so as to smooth out the peak demands for the two fuels; 6) 

Generation of electricity by the use of gas, thus keeping the load factor high on the gas pipeline with accompanying savings; and 7) 

Financial strength of a combination company which enables it to finance pipelines and other facilities that a straight gas company could not.

Dissolving combination companies was also said to have negative aspects such as creating two weaker companies, higher rates for consumers, and situations where higher financing costs can be expected. 

Moreover, divestiture could cause disruption in utility service and effect the national economy, and create the need for vast adjustments in utility structure and capital markets, with an adverse effect on the economy. The difficulties of dividing up the assets of a combined company, along with the many issues that would have to be adjudicated so that the divested company would receive its fair share of the long term low interest rate debt, were said to be persuasive reasons for not requiring the divestiture of the large combined companies. 

The large costs associated with widespread divestiture were also cited as a reason for not making divestiture mandatory. 

Witnesses favoring retention of combined companies were NARUC, several representatives of companies operating both gas and electric systems, an economics firm representing 29 utilities, and the Federal Power Commission. The FPC opposed the bill because it would preempt the regulatory authority of the States and held that, in the absence of compelling evidence that combination companies were against the public interest, they should not be forced to divest. 

The arguments advanced by those who favored the bill were that: 

1) several combined companies that had been made to divest under the terms of the Public Utility Holding Company Act of 1935 had done so with great success; 2) as competition between gas and electricity intensifies, a combined company finds itself facing a conflict of interest; 3) competition rather than regulation acts more favorably for the consumer; 4) consumers are at a disadvantage where there are combined companies; 5) where combined companies exist there is a loss of competitive stimulus; 6) combined companies have no incentive to innovate and pass the benefits on to the consumer; 7) combination companies are in a better position to discriminate between consumer groups, with greater possibilities for cross-subsidization; and 

8) straight companies give the consumer greater freedom of choice.

Montana Power Company, a combination company which opposed the bill, gave the following reasons for divestiture's being inappropriate in its area: 1) The small size of the company and the sparse population resulted in significant savings when the services were offered by a combined company; 2) Financing costs would be appreciably increased if the two services were separated; 3) It was in the public interest to have a strong, healthy utility; and 4) Sales were regulated so as to protect the public.

Proponents of the legislation were representatives of a labor organization, of straight natural gas companies, a former Commissioner and a former staff member of the FPC, a Virginia State Senator and a consultant to the Antitrust Division of the Department of Justice. 

They argued that the only alternative to competition is regulation and that this has proved to be imperfect. It has resulted in combination

companies charging higher rates than straight companies and having higher distribution costs. This despite the apparently greater opportunities for the combination companies to achieve theoretical savings. 

Instead of being more efficient, as the combination companies claim to be, they are actually less efficient, based on studies made by several independent consultants.

There was general agreement among the witnesses that the bill, as drafted, needed amendments to make it acceptable to those who either opposed or were in favor of it. For example, some combination companies serve gas in one area and electricity in another. While they are a combined company they would not inhibit competition in the areas they serve since both the gas and electric segments of the company could be in competition with another utility. Another exception to divestiture that was suggested was for companies that were predominently either gas or electricity and where it was obviously in the interest of the consumer for the combined company to continue to operate.

The bill was also thought to be deficient in that it did not provide standards that would be used for divestiture, that the deadline was both too short and too arbitrary, and that the blanket prohibition of combination companies, without provision for a smooth transition to straight companies, could cause chaos in the industry. 

Other suggestions to improve the Bill were to offer economic incentives for companies to divest and to make it mandatory that companies seeking exemptions from the divestiture assume the burden of proof that it would be in the public interest to allow them to continue to operate as a combined company. 

(D) TVA Rate Increases 

On October 28 and 29 the Subcommittee on Small Business Problems of the Select Committee on Small Business of the House of Representatives conducted hearings at Oak Ridge and Nashville, Tennessee to determine the cause of the rapid rise in electricity rates in the TVA system area. In opening the hearings the Chairman noted that the 

U.S., with 6% of the world's population consumes about 33% of the world's energy and that the electricity growth rate has been about 7% per year over the past ten years. In the past two years the TVA had reversed the long term downward trend in rates that had characterized the system, with rate increases of 23%. The purpose of the inquiry was to determine the factors that caused this reversal. The Chairman noted that the four largest coal companies now produce one-third of all coal and that the major oil companies owned 85% of the refining capacity and 72% of natural gas production. The question of whether there was a relationship between higher fuel prices and concentration of ownership was one that was to be explored.

Mr. Wagner of TVA described the terms under which TVA must set their rates so that they can meet their statutory requirements for repayment of debt.
