The rapid increase in fuel prices along with greatly increased interest rates were said to be responsible for the recent rate increases. Coal costs had risen 26% between 1970 and 1971 and were 

55% higher than in 1965. New coal contracts written during 1971 were at prices double that paid one year to eighteen months ago. 

Previous to late 1969 TVA had always had ample bidders for all coal contracts offered. From that time until now it has been necessary to

negotiate contracts since not enough coal was offered. Moreover, there had been difficulties with deliveries as a result of labor troubles, freight car shortages, and competition from the export market. Negotiated contracts have been at higher prices, with coal prices rising from $4.50 per ton in 1968 to $8.70 in the fall of 1970. Prices in the latter part of 1971 had declined to about $8.30 per ton. The average cost for new coal was reported to be: 19671969 1970 

1971. 

[Cents per million B.t.u.'s] 19 23 25. 35 In addition there was some escalation in the costs even for long term contracts as allowances were made for strip mine reclamation and for the increased costs caused by the Coal Mine Health and Safety Act of 1969. 

Mr. Wagner did not anticipate any further increase in electricity rates for the next year unless there are changes in fuel prices and interest rates. However, it will not be possible to determine if there has been a return to competitive bidding on coal contracts until the next invitations are offered.

TVA attempts to spread the contracts around to increase competition and to help small business. In 1970, 93% of all new contracts and 

55% of all payments for fuel were to small businesses. In normal times 

95% of their purchases are on a competitive bid basis. With respect to honoring long term contracts the witness said that deliveries had averaged about 86% compared to the normal 90%. 

A representative of the TVA Public Power Association substantiated the data on fuel price rises and on electricity rates presented by the previous witness. In addition, he observed that the usual market forces had not seemed to be operating in the fuel supply market and that he could see no justification for the sharp price increase. He felt that the antitrust laws should be applied more vigorously and that the trend toward "energy companies" would reduce competition. With the large ownership by the Federal Government of energy resources it should be possible for the government to insure that monopolies do not develop even if it means the government must enter the market in a competitive way.

The representative of the Federal Trade Commission reported that it is investigating the concentration in the energy industries, the mergers of oil and coal companies, and the published natural gas reserve estimates. A University of Vanderbilt witness who had prepared a study on the need for antitrust action in connection with the energy crisis supplied data to indicate the growing concentration in the coal industry. Between 1949 and 1969 there had been a decrease of 42% in the number of companies producing over 1 million tons per year. Methods to maintain competition in the energy industry that do not appear to work well are application of the Sherman and Clayton Acts and regulation of the industries. The disadvantages of these methods were outlined and the witness suggested that the competition between the various fuel forms, the only good method of preventing monoply pricing, was being destroyed as the result of mergers.

At the hearings on October 29 a representative of the AEC uranium enrichment plant testified as to the importance of the price of electricity in the cost of the separative work of the AEC. He pointed out that prices of coal had been relatively stable until about 1966. Following a series of mergers, starting with the Continental Oil Company acquisition of Consolidation Coal Company, the price of coal had increased sharply.

A broker for small mines who also trucks coal for small mines noted that in the TVA area prices have recently dropped back sharply from their highs and that spot coal can now be purchased in the area for $5.75 to $6.00. This he attributed to a decrease in demand as a result of the wet summer (more production from hydroelectric sites), a warm winter, and the general business slowdown. He did however observe that the size of what is now considered a "small mine" has increased about 4 to 5 times over what it was in the past and that many of the small mines that he had represented had been unable to stay in business.

representative of the Tennessee Land and Mining Co. described the coal mining industry in the State. Most of the producers are small with 65% of their production from surface mines. He noted that there was a sharp drop in the number of underground mines in the State between 1970 and 1971 and attributed much of this to the new Coal 

Mine Health and Safety Act, particularly that provision which requires the use of "permissible equipment" in all mines after a certain date. He urged that the Congress review that requirement of the law that forces all mines to use this more expensive equipment even under geologic conditions where explosive gas would not be expected to be found.

III. Atomic Energy 

Nuclear power is beginning to supply an increasing part of the nation's electrical power supply but there have been delays in building and licensing new nuclear plants. In addition, nuclear power is strongly criticized as posing too much risk for the public and the environment. Several Congressional critics have challenged the safety of certain features of nuclear power plants and of AEC plans for the long term storage of radioactive wastes. Congressional attention during the 

92nd Congress (centered in the Joint Committee on Atomic Energy) has focused on such issues as: increasing the supply of enriched uranium; licensing procedures and problems; uranium mill tailings and the future of the fast breeder program. The Senate Interior and Insular Affairs Committee, in pursuing the fuels and energy study called for by S. Res. 45, conducted hearings on the "Calvert Cliffs" 

Court (See p. 34) decision relating to the procedures necessary for the

AEC to satisfy the requirements of the National Environment Policy 

Act. 

(A) Uranium Enrichment Pricing 

In addition to authorization hearings for FY 1972, in the early part of the first session of the 92nd Congress, hearings were held on criteria for uranium enrichment pricing. Enrichment of customersupplied uranium for use in light water reactors now operating in the U.S. is a government monopoly which, by law, must be operated to recover the government's costs for providing this service. The Uranium Enrichment Service Criteria are revised periodically to reflect total costs of carrying out this service. Prices were raised from $26.50 per Separative Work Unit (SWU) to $28.70 per SWU on Feb. 22, 1971 and proposals were made to increase this to $32.00 per SWU. 

Hearings on the proposed criteria amendment and price increases were held on Feb. 25, 1971. The increase was supported by the Administration. The General Accounting Office reported that the price of $32.00 would allow the government to recover appropriate government costs over a reasonable period of time. 

AEC Commissioner Ramey objected to the $32.00 price as being so large as to have a disruptive effect on nuclear costs. He argued that, as a matter of good business, revisions of price should be limited to once per year and that the increase to $32.00 represented two price changes in a short time that amounted to a 23% increase in prices. 

The problem of uranium enrichment prices can be expected to become more important as more nuclear plants are installed in the United States and abroad. As enrichment facility capacity designed initially for military purposes is exceeded by the demand for nuclear electricity generating plants, the matter of how to recover the costs of the added facilities needed to supply this demand will become a matter of more concern. 

(B) Licensing Procedures 

Another major issue discussed in the Congress with respect to the use of nuclear energy for generation of electricity related to proposed (54)

changes in AEC Licensing Procedures, particularly those caused by carrying out the provisions of the National Environmental Policy Act and the transfer of certain functions from the AEC. The bills under consideration concerned two types of changes: those that would permit the States to impose standards for radioactive effluent (H.R. 997, 1743, 3683, 6933, and 7539) and those that would transfer AEC regulatory functions to other agencies (H.R. 1197, 1742 and 6310). In addition, H.R. 9285 and S. 2151 provided for discretionary review by the 

Advisory Committee on Reactor Safeguards and H.R. 9286 and

S. 2152 provided for mandatory early site hearings. 

The licensing procedures used by the AEC have not fully reflected the requirements of the National Environmental Protection Act. New provisions in the Water Quality Improvement Act of 1970 and the recent transfer to the Environmental Protection Agency of the responsibilities previously held by the Federal Radiation Council with regard to radiation protection standards setting directly affect AEC regulation of nuclear power. 17 A landmark decision "7 by a Federal circuit court required that AEC licensing procedures take into account environmental effects of nuclear power plants in addition to radiological effects. The "Calvert 

Cliffs" decision (See p. 34) resulted in AEC review of all licenses it has issued since January 1970 for construction or operation of nuclear power plants.

Hearings on how the AEC licensing procedures must be amended to reflect their new responsibilites were held by the Joint Committee on 

Atomic Energy on June 22, 23, and July 13 and 14, 1971.

Previous hearings on licensing had been held in 1967 but since that time the number of applications to construct and operate nuclear plants had increased greatly, the plants had grown much larger and more complex, requiring more sophisticated safety evaluations and the new concern about environmental values has added more complexity to licensing considerations.

At the time of the first hearings a new concern about the safety of nuclear generating plants was raised with respect to the adequacy of devices used for emergency cooling of the nuclear core. As a result of some tests in a small scale model of a reactor core, it was questioned (based on an extrapolation of the data) whether emergency cooling procedures would be adequate for full scale plants. 

As a result the AEC placed an interim limitation on the maximum temperature at which a core could be operated. 

In addition to the question of transferring AEC functions, the hearings were largely concerned with the matter of safety and the development of ways to reduce the excessive time required in obtaining licenses, particularly with respect to new requirements relating to the National Environment Policy Act. The Advisory Committee on 

Reactor Safeguards reported that for advanced designs or reactors located at difficult sites the average time for construction permits was 13 months and 20 months for operations permits. Several witnesses believed that a fundamental change in the procedure would be required in the existing licensing approvals including a reappraisal of public participation in the decision making process. Among the suggestions proposed for improving the licensing procedures were, early site 

17 Calvert Cliffs' Coordinating Committee v. Atomic Energy Commission. 449 F.2d 1109 (D.C. Cir. 1971). 

71-900-72- -5

selection under terms of provisions of legislation proposed for power plant siting, early evaluation of design concepts, a relaxation of the mandatory requirement for review of each plant by the Advisory Committee on Reactor Safeguards, advance planning for power plant siting, and developing a more cooperative relationship between the AEC and EPA with respect to thermal pollution and radiation hazards.

The Advisory Committee on Reactor Safeguards (ACRS) was in favor of the non-mandatory review of licenses and in development of standard reactor types, as useful steps to speed up the licensing process.

The privately owned utility industry, represented by the Edison Electric Institute, expressed interest in reforms in reactor licensing and recommended: 1) eliminating mandatory ACRS reviews of licenses, 2) providing for a method of early site authorization, 3) combining site and construction authorization, 4) that intervenors in proceedings be able to show cause for their action, and 5) that some legislation be enacted that would clarify the procedural relationships between the various environment requirements with respect to nuclear power plants.

This industry position was supported by witnesses representing a number of utilities which had encountered delays in obtaining approval for nuclear generating plants. Witnesses representing conservation groups, however, argued that the changes proposed in licensing procedures was merely an attempt to "get on with the business of building nuclear reactors" with the "least possible interference from the public". Concern was also expressed with regard to nuclear safety (radiation, accidents), whether the hearings were "industry protective" in providing a way to speed up licensing for the benefit of industry, and to the reduction in the role of the ACRS in review of applications in view of the rapid growth of the nuclear industry. 

Opposition to the new legislation under consideration was expressed by several of these witnesses.

The consumer and conservationist witnesses stressed the need for early operating license hearings, methods to improve hearing procedures, and changes in rulemaking hearings. A panel discussion of the various issues did not succeed in obtaining agreement on how licensing procedures should be modified. The testimony of representatives of the public interest groups called for a method of providing a voice for the citizens to insure that the AEC made the best possible decisions with respect to licensing of nuclear plants. They claimed that previous 

AEC testimony indicated that the AEC was insulated from public intervention.

Modification of the proposed legislation was suggested by both the industry and conservation witnesses. Some of the problems that were thought to need further discussion were: 

1. Consideration of the role of the States. 

2. New procedures for contested issues. 

3. Redefinition of the role of parties in contested proceedings. 

4. Elimination of the dual responsibility of the AEC. 

5. Providing knowledgeable public intervenors in public hearings. 

6. Modification of the rule making procedures through the use of public hearings.

Testimony of other witnesses from individual utility companies revealed that the provisions of the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) were thought to be outside of the purview of the AEČ during hearings on reactor safety and on siting. This view was finally found to be contrary to the requirements of section 102 of NEPA and resulted in the "Calvert Cliffs" decision by the courts. 

(c) Uranium Mill Tailings 

The tailings, or rejects from the mills at which uranium ores are upgraded have been used (starting in 1952 and continuing until 1966) for construction fill purposes and in some cases for the mortar mix in public and private buildings in the Grand Junction, Colorado area. 

The tailings contain radium from which both radiation and radon (and its daughters) are emitted with possible danger to the health of the inhabitants of the buildings.

The purposes of the hearings held on October 29 and 30 by the Joint Committee on Atomic Energy were to determine whether a health problem actually existed, to define the scope of the problem, and to ascertain what remedial actions should be taken. In addition, methods were to be explored to achieve protection for the public in the future.

The Atomic Energy Commission stated that control of the tailings from uranium ore dressing plants was beyond the responsibility of the AEC. If the uranium and thorium content were below a given level the 

Commission had no authority over its disposition. Moreover, the 

Commission has no authority over radium which is the cause of the difficulties associated with the use of tailings.

The Public Health Service, as a result of requests from the State of Colorado and others has issued guidelines to be followed in determining the danger that might be involved at several exposure levels, but stated that it had no legal responsibilities with respect to uranium tailings or in implementing the guidelines that it had recommended.

The Public Health Service and the Environmental Protection 

Agency both described extensive tests currently under way designed to (1) determine the severity and extent of the public health problems from uranium tailings and (2) the feasibility and cost of control by removing the tailings, by increased ventilation, and by sealing_off the tailings so that radon could not escape into the structure. The costs of surveys, their accuracy, and the relationship of radiation levels to radon daughter levels were discussed. 

The State of Colorado witnesses, however, expressed the view that the AEC should have recognized the long term hazards and provided the needed protection for the public. Without regard to past responsibility, the State is conducting a house by house survey and will recommend corrective action on an individual basis. The problem of who will be responsible for corrective costs remains unanswered, but State witnesses urged that the AEC provide immediate financial relief so those residing or using the buildings will not continue to be subject to further exposure.

The Mayor of Grand Junction testified that as a result of a series of newspaper stories the attitude of the local residents had changed from one of no concern to one of doubt about the health dangers involved and this was having a significant impact on real estate values and sales. Moreover, he stated that economic burden would

be too great for the Grand Junction community to bear the cost of corrective action.

The representative of the AMAX company that operated the plant from which most of the tailings had come, stated the tailings were removed from the pile at no cost to the user after the AEC indicated that it had no jurisdiction over the material. 

The hearings concluded with the Committee waiting for the extensive new data on exposure rates and clean-up costs to be developed as a basis for determining whether it will be necessary to remove the tailings, whether no action should be taken, or what other means should be used to correct any danger that might exist. 

(D) Controlled Thermonuclear Research (Fusion) 

On November 10 and 11, the Joint Committee on Atomic Energy held hearings on the status of research and development on controlled thermonuclear reactions. These reactions offer the potential of virtually inexhaustable supplies of energy with minimum environmental impacts. Research in this field has been supported by the 

AEC for over 20 years with a total expenditure of about $450 million. 

Even with this expenditure the feasibility of producing electric power by controlled thermonuclear reactors is still to be proved. With the very large potential pay-out if the research is successful, there is strong incentive to continue to support the R & D efforts. 

Significant improvements have been made in the three areas which must be further developed relating to confinement of the plasma, the high temperatures that must be produced, and the densities that must be achieved. Although progress has been encouraging, major improvements in these variables are still needed.

The hearings resulted in an up-to-date report on the progress of the major research groups that are conducting experiments in the field. 

The degree of success to date and the unresolved problems still to be studied were described. The current level of research is at a yearly rate of about $33 million per year. The major questions raised at the hearing were concerned with whether it would be desirable to try to speed up the development in view of the limited fossil fuel resources and, more importantly, whether the expenditure of additional funds in this area would actually result in closing the time gap when fusion reactions could be commercially used. There was general agreement among those engaged in this research that a 20 to 30% increase could be used effectively by the existing experimental organizations. Also additional funds could be used by the universities conducting supporting research. While admittedly a guess, it was thought that the date for possible commercialization could be advanced from the year 2000 to as early as 1990 if the added funds were appropriated. 

There was general agreement among the witnesses that the three principal steps that must be pursued are: demonstration of technical feasibility; construction and operation of a prototype reactor; and the design, construction, and operation of a demonstration reactor. 

At this stage of development it was recommended that all possible routes to demonstrate feasibility should be pursued. The best likelihood that feasibility would be demonstrated was by experimenting with all possible methods since the final process selected might be a combination of several of the different approaches that are currently being studied. Once feasibility has been proven, the additional costs

for the second and third stages would be so high that only one route would probably be selected for further testing. 

Progress on feasibility research is accomplished in discrete steps, since the test results from one apparatus must be translated into the design, construction, and testing of the next device. This cycle takes about 5 years. With the greater amcunt of cooperation and coordination among the various groups than had prevailed in the past, along with increased international cooperation, it is believed that progress in the field will be accelerated.

A panel discussion of the state of the art reached general agreement that the research had now reached the point where the physical factors were in the range that was of real interest with respect to temperature, density, and confinement. In the past, theoretical and experimental workers in the field had worked independently. Now the two groups are working closely so that the experimental results obtained in the laboratory are subjected to theoretical analysis and the new theories developed are then tested experimentally. At this stage of development all parallel efforts on plasmas, materials, superconductivity, etc. should be strengthened. All the options must be retained but must be monitored closely so that at the proper time a selection of which type of device to develop to a larger size can be made.

IV. Oil (A) Interstate Compact to Conserve Oil and Gas 

The Interstate Compact to Conserve Oil and Gas, was approved by Congress in 1935 for a two year period. Since that time, the Compact has been renewed for succeeding periods of two or four years. S.J. Res. 

72 if passed would grant approval of open ended extension of the compact. Congress would retain power to withdraw its approval at any time.

The compact was created in response to oil production problems which arose with the discoveries of enormous oil fields in the twenties and thirties. The purpose of the compact was "to conserve oil and gas by the prevention of physical waste thereof from any cause. 

18 The 

Interstate Oil Compact Commission was created by Congress and consists of the Governors or their representatives of the member states; there are presently 29 members. The Commission is empowered to make recommendations to the member states for regulating their oil and gas industries. 

Hearings on S.J. Res. 72 were held on June 17, 1971, before the

Subcommittee on Minerals, Materials, and Fuels of the Senate Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs. Letters were received from the Department of the Interior, the Office of Management and Budget, and the Department of Justice, all of which endorsed the resolution. 

The Justice Department letter recommended that the compact be amended so that the department could survey the activities of the Commission and make reports from time to time rather than make annual reports as presently required. 

Witnesses representing the Interstate Oil Compact Commission and the Office of Oil and Gas spoke in favor of the resolution. They stated that an open-ended extension would avoid the expenditure of time and effort necessary to renew the Compact. These witnesses did not object to ending the requirement for yearly Justice Department reports.

During the hearings, it was noted that the Committee had undertaken an energy policy study (pursuant to S. Res. 45) and it might be better to grant indefinite extension after the study was completed. 

It was also noted that the last time the compact had been renewed there had been considerable opposition because of the feeling that industry had undue influence on the compact. Both Administration witnesses stated that they did not feel this to be the case. As one witness pointed out, there are industry people on committees of the compact but policy is set by the representatives of the States. These witnesses felt that the Compact had been beneficial to consumers. 

The Senate passed the Resolution extending the Pact for 3 years instead of indefinitely. The House has not acted. 

18 The purpose was not to "limit the production of oil or gas for the purpose of stabilizing or fixing the price thereof, or create or perpetuate monopoly, or to promote regimentation." (60)

(B) Environmental Effects of the Alaskan Pipeline 

The proposed Trans Alaskan Pipeline would transport oil from the 

North Slope Fields to the port of Valdez on Prince William Sound, about 120 miles east of Anchorage. The oil would then be transferred to tankers for shipment to refineries on the continental West Coast. 

On November 17, 1971, the Subcommittee on Fisheries and Wildlife

Conservation of the House Committee on Merchant Marine and 

Fisheries heard testimony from representatives of the fishery industry of the Prince William Sound region. The fishing industry was fearful that oil pollution from oil tankers might damage or destroy their fishing grounds. They also expressed concern that the environmental impact on the marine aspects of the development of the oil fields was not receiving adequate consideration. Oil could be introduced into the environment either from ballast water and other normal activities or by major spills resulting from accidents to the tankers. These problems would be aggravated by the great volume of traffic projected for the port, by the dangerous channels that would have to be used, and the often severe weather conditions in the area. 

The witnesses representing the fisheries of Cordova pointed out that fishing represents a two million dollar per year industry and that the town of Cordova is effectively totally dependent upon this source of income. The pink salmon which represent 75% of the catch is a rather intolerant species and because of the nature of its life cycle there is reason to fear that oil pollution could be very destructive. Additionally the herring fisheries and the harvesting of the kelp might be adversely effected by oil spills.

The witnesses stated that there was very strong political pressure in 

Alaska to support the pipeline. However, the Fisheries Union has instituted suits against Federal agencies in regard to the pipeline. 

Although various Federal agencies have been cooperative, the fishing group said that they have had difficulty in convincing them of the importance of and the need for funding of a study of the problem. 

The witnesses stated that the development of the oil fields would be good for Alaska, but, they want to prevent serious disruption of their industry. They pointed out that recent advances in safer tanker construction were not being employed in vessels planned to service the port and also suggested that serious consideration be given to an entirely overland route through Canada.

Representatives of the industry requested a one year moratorium on the pipeline so that adequate studies of this problem could be made. 

(c) Marine Sanctuaries On November 1 and 4 the Senate Interior and Insular Affairs 

Committee held hearings on seven bills, each of which would have created a marine sanctuary at different locations off the California coast. The State of California has already created sanctuaries in offshore water areas that are under State control. If drilling were permitted on the Federal lands seaward of these sanctuaries the purposes of the State laws would be thwarted. The hearing was to determine if leasing should be suspended on the Federal lands and to establish a Congressional policy on these lands. 

Similar legislation had been introduced in the 91st Congress but was opposed on the grounds that, as written, it would lead to an erosion of Federal authority. A State could decide how the Federal

land would be administered, there was no provision for terminating the withdrawal during periods of national emergency, and it too severely restricted scientific exploration on the withdrawn areas. 

The bills introduced in the 92nd Congress sought to overcome those objections by identifying the specific areas to be withdrawn. They also provided that for future withdrawals the Secretary of Interior would recommend appropriate action to the Congress and the Congress would then consider each area separately. The national defense and exploration provisions were also changed in the new bills. 

Senators Cranston and Tunney of California argued that the State, knowing that there was oil in these areas, had decided to forego the revenues and jobs that the resources would have created in order to protect the coastline. Since oil spills were inevitable the leasing of the Federal portion of the Outer Continental Shelf seaward of the sanctuaries would make the California law meaningless. They suggested that at some time in the future, when improved technology had been developed, it might be possible to develop these resources without defacing the seascape and in a manner that would safeguard the environment. Representatives of a number of counties in California where State sanctuaries had been established testified in support of this position. One witness noted that there are six general hazards to be considered: blowouts, oil spills, oil associated land installations, aesthetic objections, navigational hazards, and geologic hazards. 

Representatives of the American Petroleum Institute and the 

Western Oil and Gas Association said that the bills had to be considered in a broader context. It was not simply a matter of a few sanctuaries, although these seven represented about one-half of the California coastline, but the future development of all of the resources of the Outer Continental Shelf (OCS) that was involved. The most promising new oil resources were in the OCS and the country is in need of new oil and gas reserves to meet future energy requirements. 

Alternatives to these resources are inadequate. The safety record of offshore petroleum operations have been excellent. Only 3 wells out of 14,000 had produced any important pollution threats. It was also argued that the passage of these bills would set a precedent and other areas would soon be closed to leasing, thus creating a monumental lockup of potential OCS resources. The Committee was urged not to take a fragmented view of the energy issue and to wait until the Senate developed a comprehensive energy policy. Then it would be possible to decide what should be done in the context of a broader understanding of the need for energy resources. 

The representative of the Western Oil and Gas Association pointed out that although the bills did not prevent exploration in the areas, it would not actually take place because even if an area looked attractive for drilling none would be undertaken. He also described the many new innovations that had been developed by the oil industry to insure that another Santa Barbara type incident would not occur. He outlined the numerous new techniques available for rapid containment and cleanup. The bills were said to violate the concept of the multiple use of lands and to eliminate the incentive for the development of the OCS. The governing policies on how these lands should be treated was already covered in the OCS Act, the recommendations of the Public 

Land Law Review Commission and the Minerals Policy Act of 1970.

The witness for the Administration described the great difficulties that arise when a practical and desirable balance is sought between recreational uses and the development of resources on Federal lands. He stated that national needs sometimes were in conflict with the narrower concerns and local preferences and that the national requirements must prevail. He opposed these bills since under the OCS Act the Secretary of Interior already has the discretionary authority to withhold or advance the use of these lands and the bills under consideration would remove this discretion from him. Moreover, other States would also see similar legislation and the Federal Government would lose control of the OCS lands. In response to a question, the administration witness noted that an alternative to developing these deposits of oil was increased imports from abroad. This would not only endanger national security but the pollution hazards from the increased number of tankers needed to bring the oil to U.S. ports might be a greater potential pollution hazard then offshore drilling.

(A) Fuel Gas Price Controls V. Natural gas 

The overall gas supply situation and particularly the subject of potential shortages were investigated in hearings on S. 1846, a bill which would establish a Coal Gasification Development Corporation. 

(See p. 76)

The major reason advanced by industry for the decline of gas reserves is the insufficiency of economic incentives to drill for new supplies. This, in turn, is said to have resulted from the Supreme 

Court decision of 1954 which placed the regulation of field prices of natural gas under the Federal Power Commission. Prior to that time gas producers selling gas to interstate pipelines were able to negotiate prices for their gas without any control of prices. From 

1954 to date, the methods of administering these controls have been changed but gas prices at the well head have remained under the regulatory authority of the FPC.

Whatever the reasons, the absolute reserves of natural gas have declined for the past three years. There is now general agreement that means to reverse this trend are desperately needed. This has provided a climate for proposals to change the type and nature of regulations now being applied by the FPC. The gas industry has suggested that, if some limitation were placed on the FPC's regulatory authority, more exploratory drilling would result.

Hearings were held on HR 2513, a bill to amend the Natural Gas Act and which according to Congressman Murphy, sponsor of the bill, would not deregulate gas prices or in itself increase gas prices but would make firm those prices once set by the FPC. In his view, this procedure would encourage gas producers to find and develop new reserves.

A major provision of the bill would require that all new gas contracts between a gas producer and an interstate pipeline transmission company (except contracts of small producers) would be negotiated between the producer and the buyer but would require that they continue to be submitted for approval by the FPC. Prices in existing contracts that have been approved could not be reduced. 

The bill received the endorsement of the American Gas Association. 

AGA believes that the bill will produce a practical way to regulate prices and would assure that contract prices could be relied upon. 

The price reliability provided in HR 2513 was viewed as a means of achieving price stability.

The Independent Natural Gas Association of America argued that 

HR 2513 would provide for "sanctity of contracts," giving stability to producer prices. Others supporting the legislation thought that it would assist interstate purchasers to compete more effectively for gas with uncontrolled intrastate purchasers.

Also supporting the bill were representatives of the coal industry which expressed the view that while it did not permit the market place (64)

6.to set gas prices it was a move in that direction. The coal industry has historically opposed field gas price regulation since it believes that it has kept gas prices artificially low and resulted in unfair competition in some of the coal industry's markets. 

The Gas Supply Committee of the AGA supported HR 2513 

expressing the view that it would:

A. Establish functional criteria for use by the Federal Power 

Commission in regulation of producers. 

B. Simplify administrative procedures and reduce regulatory delay.

C. Eliminate huge buildup of potential refunds. 

D. Improve the competitive position of the interstate markets vs. the intrastate market. 

E. Restore the confidence of the producers.

Opponents of the legislation claimed that it would permit short-term gas contracts and in time of gas shortage this would result in priceescalating bidding among purchasers for the limited supply. In the opinion of Mr. Swidler a former FPC chairman and currently chairman of the N.Y. State Utility Commission, it would not operate to expand gas supply nor would it permit interstate purchasers to compete effectively with intrastate gas buyers. He also disputed the claim that the new bill would speed up regulation, since it required that not only the initial gas price but also future price escalations in the contract be approved. This, in Mr. Swidler's opinion, would result in even longer delays than are now the rule.

Other opponents argued that exemption of small producers from contract approval would mean that a substantial portion of gas now under regulation would be deregulated. Objections were also raised with respect to the provision of the bill that would permit unregulated sales during the time that approval was being sought for new gas contracts.

The bill was opposed by the present Chairman of the FPC on the grounds that it would make regulatory procedures too complex and lead to protracted litigation in the courts. In addition, by creating an uncertain regulatory climate, it would delay rather than speed up the time when the natural gas supply would be increased. The FPC 

Chairman also warned that higher gas prices could result if HR 2513 were enacted and that the time permitted for the FPC to act on applications was unrealistic. 

An Administration bill, offered as a substitute for HR 2513, would limit the "sanctity of contract" provisions only to new gas and would permit the FPC to consider "rate base and cost of service" evidence in determining reasonable contract prices. This would have been prohibited under provisions of HR 2513. The FPC Chairman said that the Administration bill would accomplish the same objective of developing new gas supplies but would avoid discarding procedures that had been developed over nearly two decades of regulation. 

Phillips Petroleum Co. testified that exempting small producers from regulation would cause problems for the producer-processer of gas unless the latter could charge a higher price for that portion of the gas which had been purchased (at higher prices) from the unregulated small producers.

Provisions of other related bills on gas supplies and prices and on which hearings have not been held are:

H.R. 3668 which authorizes and directs the FPC to conduct studies of production, gathering, storage, transport, distrubution and sale of natural or artificial gas and report the results of such studies to Congress.

H.R. 10333 and S. 2405 which authorize similar studies but in addition direct the FPC to make an annual independent evaluation of proven natural gas reserve, identifying the amount in production and that not being produced and the reasons for the non-production. 

Any company that fails to provide FPC with résumé data would be ineligible to bid on any leases on Federal lands. 

S. 637 which would remove the authority of the FPC to set field prices of natural gas in order to establish a free market price for natural gas. Senator Tower, the bill's sponsor, predicted that gas prices would rise but said that gas was underpriced and the increase was necessary to encourage the exploration which would develop the new reserves that are needed. The degree that gas was underpriced compared to other fuels was illustrated by the fact that the petroleum industry sells more heat units as gas than as oil but only one-fourth of its revenues are generated by gas sales. The inhibiting effect of present gas regulatory practices was shown by comparing the price of unregulated intrastate gas of 30¢ per 1,000 cubic feet to the average price of 16¢ per 1,000 cubic feet for regulated interstate gas. It was said that it is only through these economic incentives, which deregulation of gas prices would offer, that the gas supply shortage can be overcome. 

(B) Pipeline Safety The Natural Gas Pipeline Safety Act, enacted in 1968, established a joint Federal-State program to strengthen pipeline safety programs. 

A State which adopts at least the minimum Federal standards and has machinery adequate to enforce these standards is authorized to administer the Act with respect to interstate pipelines. The State may also impose more strict standards than the Federal standards for those lines but may not do so for interstate pipelines. 

Since the passage of the Act the Office of Pipeline Safety has devoted most of its efforts to the development of regulations. These were issued in August 1970. Other responsibilities including the provision of supplying technical information to the States that had not established pipeline safety programs and the enforcement of the new Federal regulations have received little attention from the office. 

Hearings were held by the House Committee on Interstate and 

Foreign Commerce on H.R. 5065 on March 9, 1971. This bill would have amended the Natural Gas Pipeline Safety Act to authorize (for an indefinite period) the appropriation of funds to carry out the purposes of the Act. It would also have extended until August 1971 the period during which states may qualify to administer the Act. 

The witnesses appearing for the State regulatory commissions and NARUC criticized the standards adopted by the Department of Transportation as being weaker than the standards which had been in effect in several States and pointed to the failure of the Federal Government to make available grant-in-aid funds authorized by the Act. They also objected to those provisions of the act which prevent the States from establishing for interstate transmission lines standards which are more stringent than those established by the Federal

Government. Furthermore, these witnesses urged that the act be amended to authorize the Department of Transportation and the States to assess interstate pipelines for the cost of inspecting such lines in enforcing the Federal standards.

The Department of Transportation generally supported the bill and responded to the criticism of the regulatory commissions by arguing that it was not the intent of Congress, under the 1968 Act, to enable the States to impose more rigid standards on interstate pipelines. 

However, the Department would not oppose an amendment that would permit the States to impose stricter standards than those issued by the 

Federal agency.

The House of Representatives passed an amended bill on August 6, 1971; the bill provided authorizations for three years and extended the deadline for the States to qualify to enforce the Federal standards from August 1971 to August 1972.

Hearings were held by the Senate Commerce Committee on 

November 9 on H.R. 5065 and two Senate bills S. 980 and S. 1910. The purposes of the hearing were to determine the effectiveness of the 1968 Act and to determine what amendments were needed to achieve its original objectives of increasing pipeline safety.

A witness of the National Transportation Safety Board testified with respect to the Board's activities under the Act and presented statistical information about the occurrence of accidents and death as a result of pipeline failures. The Board reported good cooperation from the Office of Pipeline Safety, from the utilities, and from the 

States involved.

The witness for NARUC repeated the concern expressed at the House hearings with respect to the prohibition to the States of establishing standards that are stricter than the Federal ones for interstate pipelines. The NARUC witness was in favor of the provision in S. 1910 to allow the States to use the judicial process for imposing injunctions and monetary sanctions for non-compliance. 

Since the States has stopped receiving Federal funds for inspection of interstate pipelines, there is no effective safety program now in existence. S. 1910 would share these costs on a 50-50 basis between the Federal and State authorities, but NARUC preferred that the States be given the right to assess the pipelines to pay for the inspection.

The Department of Transportation opposed many of the provisions in S. 1910 since it did not provide the flexibility of enforcement contained in H.R. 5065. The Department also opposed as premature the provision of Federal funds to the National Organization of State 

Commission. The activities of the Office of Pipeline Safety were reviewed and plans for monitoring the States to insure compliance were discussed. The Department was criticized for not providing a mechanism for consumer groups to participate actively in helping to establish improved standards.

The Senate did not report out a bill during the first session of the 

92nd Congress. 

(c) Natural Gas For The Pacific Northwest 

On four separate occasions from 1964 to 1969 the Supreme Court has ruled that El Paso Natural Gas Co. should divest itself of its

Pacific Northwest Division. El Paso had acquired 99.8% of the 

Pacific Northwest Pipeline Corporation's outstanding stocks in 1957.

At that time El Paso was the sole out-of-state supplier of natural gas in California (50% of the gas sold in the State). Prior to the acquisition, Pacific Northwest and El Paso had exchange arrangements for gas from the San Juan Basin and from Canadian supplies available to Pacific Northwest. And prior to these agreements Pacific 

Northwest had tried to enter the California market using Canadian gas supplies. Negotiations were entered into with Southern California 

Edison Co. (Edison) to replace with "firm" gas the "interruptable" supplies that Edison had been receiving with El Paso. Edison finally contracted with El Paso for "firm" gas at a price that was reduced from 40 to 30 cents per MCF during the negotiations. 

The first Supreme Court decision ruled that El Paso's acquisition of Pacific Northwest had been in violation of the Clayton Antitrust Act in that, by the acquisition, competition of natural gas sales in California was substantially lessened. Several subsequent decisions revolved around the rights of certain appellants to intervene in the case. In the most recent decision the Court ruled that divestiture should be carried out. However many parties to the case had earlier agreed that lower court decisions, not requiring divestitute, were in the public interest.

Hearings were held on April 10 and 20, 1971 before the Senate Commerce Committee to receive the opinions of those affected by the decision of the Supreme Court. The Governors of the Pacific 

Northwest States and other public officials has requested to be heard since they believed that the integrity of gas service in the area would be threatened. The hearings were designed to consider this issue and to determine if legislation to preserve the merger would help to assure the Pacific Northwest adequate supplies at lowest costs.

The Governor of Idaho, the entire Idaho congressional delegation, and the Idaho Utilities Commission supported the view that the merger should be preserved. It was argued that FPC had found the merger was in the public interest because it would improve the gas supply and utilization of reserves, assure a financially strong gas supplier, expand and improve gas services to the public and provide lower rates to gas consumers. Moreover, it was claimed that subsequent experience since the merger had proved that these desirable goals had been achieved.

The very long litigation that had developed over El Paso's divestiture plans and the uncertainty as to what would occur were said to be resulting in a lack of interest to assure a long term supply of natural gas for the area. It was also claimed that the court terms for organizing a successor company could only result in higher rates to consumers because of changes in the cost of capital. The need for additional natural gas to meet environmental standards was stressed. 

These "public interest" arguments for preserving the merger were supported by the Governor of Washington, the Chairman of the Public Utilities Commission of Washington, a representative of the Governor of the State of Oregon, and the Wyoming Public Service Commission. 

Additional arguments to support this view were (1) that if the divestiture was the proper course at one time, it was not longer timely, (2) that the termination of long, expensive, fruitless litigation with

respect to the divestiture plants would be in the public interest, and, (3) that El Paso is an experienced gas company and their performance is such that they could be expected to find additional reserves to alleviate the gas shortage in the area served. 

Objections were also raised, by the Washington Natural Gas Co. to a recent decision that requires a cash sale and the allocation of San Juan reserves by the new company to provide for immediate service to California. It was claimed that both of these conditions would impose great dangers on any new company in that the gas reserve requirement could not be met and a cash sale would be disadvantageous to the new entity because of the present high cost of capital. 

The Washington Water Power Company, the Washington State Superintendent of Public Instruction, the Washington Public Utilities District's Association, the Association of Washington Business, Washington State Hospital Association, Apartment Operators Associ ation, the Industrial Energy Users Committee, the former Governor of the State of California, the University of Puget Sound, the Idaho State University, and the Associated Oregon industries all supported, the right of El Paso to supply gas to Pacific Northwest. 

The testimony of the El Paso witness covered the history of the company's acquisition of the Pacific Northwest Co.; the events in the intervening years which the Company thought demonstrated the anticipated benefits of the merger, why it believed that divestiture in 

1971 is contrary to the interests of consumers; and why Congress should enact legislation to preserve the merger. The reasons given for new legislation were: (1) the legal conflicts in the history of the litigation that have arisen as a result of conflict in the fundamental policies of two Acts of Congress (Natural Gas Act and Clayton Act); (2) because of this conflict the courts never considered the broad aspects of public interest involved in the merger; and, (3) only the 

Congress can fashion a solution to this problem that will assure that consumers in the area will enjoy the benefits of competition. In this connection El Paso said that the competitive test should be whether a divestiture will result in lower prices, better service, more economical use of resources and the greater likelihood that new gas supplies will be made available. The lengthly court case had permitted conditions of gas supply and gas markets to change so radically that, even if the initial divestiture decision had been made correctly, divestiture would no longer meet the test of providing the best competitive climate for gas customers in the area.

Opponents of the legislation, however, claimed that it was not possible to judge at this time whether divestiture would be desirable. since the courts had not yet decided on a plan or named an applicant. 

As a result the practical application would be speculative. Secondly, legislation by Congress to overrule a Supreme Court decision presents a constitutional confrontation that requires that action be taken only if compelling cause can be found. This would raise questions about the propriety of Congress legitimizing an action that the courts had held illegal and the possible impact on antitrust laws. It therefore would compel those who wish Congress to enact legislation to convince it that there could be no serious doubt about the issue. 

Concern was expressed with respect to the public concluding that large corporations were "beyond the powers of the Court" and that there was a continuing concentration of powers into the hands of a few.

A representative of the Washington State Labor Council AFL-CIO 

opposed the legislation, arguing that judgments about divestiture could not be made since the conditions of divestiture had not yet been settled. He pointed out that El Paso was receiving consideration of its case largely because it had the resources to delay implementation of the Supreme Court decisions. This tactic is not available to the ordinary citizen and the law should apply equally to all. Moreover, the consideration should not be whether gas prices will go up for a short time, but rather whether long term low cost gas supplies will be available to the consumer. In this regard it was claimed that the issues were not clear and the consumer would benefit if no action were taken until better information is available. He concluded that any harm to the consumer would be small and that greater damage would be done by allowing "corporate giants to flaunt the law, then have 

Congress bless their illegal activities."

Other opponents of legislation pointed out that El Paso had continuously reduced the amount of low cost U.S. gas to the Northwest and increased the amount of high cost Canadian gas, to the benefit of consumers in the South. It was also claimed that El Paso, knowing since 1964 that it must divest itself of the Pacific Northwest market, had further increased the amount of Canadian gas. 

Representatives of the Department of Justice recommended against enactment of any legislation in the El Paso case. This position was based on the belief that it would result in a very detrimental effect on antitrust enforcement and would be viewed by other potential violators as a precedent for obtaining "forgiveness legislation".

However, because of the conflict between the Natural Gas Act and the Clayton Act they proposed that new procedures be incorporated by law into the National Gas Act which would give the antitrust division of the Department of Justice an opportunity to review promptly decisions having potential antitrust involvement. If the 

Department objected, no merger could occur until the court had clarified the issue and thus no divestiture would need to occur. If approved by the Department, the action would be immunized from subsequent attack under the antitrust laws. 

A study prepared for the Commerce Committee suggested three courses of action open to it: (1) Immunize the merger, (2) Conditional immunity, or (3) "Wait and see" what actions the court takes on the divestiture conditions. Immunity would secure the merger benefits and end time-consuming legislation. However, the report suggested that the merger's net benefits were not important enough to warrant a special legislative exception. Conditional immunity (by a new act) would be used to avoid a harmful divestiture decree by providing that El Paso divest itself under terms and conditions provided by the new act. Because a "wait and see" plan would at most require the loss of a few additional months this action was proposed. If the divestiture plan the courts decide upon is not reasonable Congress could then act to pass a bill with conditional immunity. 

On Oct. 21 and 22 hearings were again held on the El Paso case. 

By this time S. 2404 had been introduced. This measure would allow 

El Paso to preserve the merger. At the October hearings the arguments previously offered for preserving the merger and for divestiture were repeated by the various witnesses. New witnesses included

Senator Harris, Representative Adams of the State of Washington, the Attorney General of California, newspaper publisher in Salt Lake 

City, The Pacific Lighting Co., the Continental Bank and Trust Co. 

of Salt Lake, and a representative of Ralph Nader. In addition the antitrust division of the Department of Justice testified as did the 

President's Special Assistant for Consumer Affairs. The Husky Oil Co., APCO Oil Co., Western Sunset Transmission Co., COPACO 

Inc., and Banister Continental Corporation, all applicants to operate the new company, also appeared as witnesses.

The witness from the State of California supported the bill but only if it were amended to protect the benefits that were due California consumers. If the bill were not amended to insure this protection the 

State would oppose the legislation. This view was also supported by the Pacific Lighting Co. The Utah bank witness opposed the bill since he believed it would have an adverse effect on the economy of his State and would reduce free, vigorous, and healthy competition. 

The Justice Department repeated its opposition to S. 2404 on the basis that only El Paso would benefit by the passage of the bill. A 

University of Utah witness claimed that passage of the bill would be a corruption of the judicial process since El Paso had left no stone unturned to frustrate and delay the decisions of the Supreme Court. 

The witness also suggested that the introduction of S. 2404 was simply another example of El Paso trying to get a favorable decision in another arena before the final divestiture method was decided by the Court.

In the course of the testimony, an accusation was made that there had been improper conduct in trying to gain support for S. 2404. A special hearing was held on Nov. 23 that did not involve the merits of the bill, but was to serve only to remove the cloud which had cloaked the bill as a result of this accusation. The Chairman at the conclusion of the hearings said that the cloud had been removed with respect to the bill and that the Department of Justice would be asked to examine the testimony to determine if any action would be necessary on their part with respect to conflicts in the statements of witnesses. 71-900-72– -6

(A) Surface Mining VI. Coal 

Both the House and Senate Interior and Insular Affairs Committees conducted extensive hearings on surface mining legislation. The House hearings were held on September 20 and 21, October 21 and 26, and 

November 29 and 30, the Senate hearings on November 16 and 17 and December 2. Twenty-two organizations testified at both hearings and several other witnesses appeared before only one Committee. 

Twelve Congressmen testified before the House Committee while four Senators testified before the Senate Committee. 

Legislation related to surface mining has been introduced in Congress repeatedly over the last thirty years. 

Strip mining permits recovery of coal that cannot be mined by underground methods, is safer, allows higher recovery of the resource and is usually a lower-cost method of producing coal. Vast deposits of coal are still susceptible to strip mining methods, and, with expected improvements in technology, even larger reserves should be recoverable in this manner. In 1970 surface mining accounted for 94% of all domestic production of mineral and non-metallic ores; approximately 

44% of all coal was strip-mined in 1970, a sharp increase from 38% in 1969.

Attempts to limit strip mining have grown out of the adverse environmental effects that it creates. These include destruction of the vegetative cover, the adverse effects of overburden on adjacent lands, the destruction of esthetic values, the stream pollution created by silt and acid mine drainage, and the losses of fish and wildlife. For some types of strip mining, reclamation of the affected lands is not too difficult, although there are sharp differences of opinion as to the effectiveness of even the best reclamation methods. In other cases, such as contour mining on steep slopes, the problems of reclamation are much greater and methods of restoring the land effectively are yet to be developed.

Recently, the growing concern for environmental quality has caused a shift in emphasis from the question of whether or not strip mining should be permitted to one of determining the best means of administering a mined lands reclamation program. The major issue is whether the Federal or State government should establish and operate such a program. The mining industry favors State control and argues that the unique local characteristics of restoration techniques would be best managed by State personnel. The industry points to the need to use different types of trees and grasses for the various quality soils that are to be restored and to the need to understand the meterological conditions that vary from region to region, if effective restoration is to be accomplished.

Those in favor of Federal legislation point to the inadequacy of many State statutes and lack of enforcement of those that do exist in some States. They say, in effect, that the coal mine operator would move to the State in which poorest overall reclamation is practiced ((72)

so that his production costs would be lower, thus gaining a competitive advantage over operators in other States.

The health and safety advantages of strip mining over underground mining is clearly revealed in both accident and fatality rates. Underground mining fatality rates in 1970 were 1.17 per million man hours, compared with .64 per million man hours in strip mines: non-fatal injury rates were 22 and 48 million man hours of exposure in strip and underground mines, respectively. 

While annual data are not collected on recovery rates at coal mines, the most recent study of underground mining gives an average recovery rate of over 50%. In strip mines average recovery is usually assumed to be 80%.

In 1969 the average f.o.b. mine price for strip coal in the U.S. was $3.98, compared with an average price of $5.62 for underground coal. 

Thus, in that year, underground coal was priced 40% higher than strip coal. The lower cost of strip mining results from the lower capital investment cost per ton of coal mined, the shorter time required to get into production, and higher productivity per man hour. In 1969 average strip mine productivity was 29.4 tons per man per day, compared with 14.9 tons for underground mines. 

A large number of bills on surface mining were introduced in the 92nd Congress. Appendix J contains the tabulation of a selected list of these bills and compares their major features about which there is still widespread disagreement. The provisions of the bills differ principally as to whether public or public-and-private lands are included; whether all minerals (or only coal) are to be regulated; and whether previously mined or only newly mined surface lands are covered. They also differ with respect to the designation of the Federal agency proposed as the lead agency; the various sources suggested for funding reclamation efforts; and, most important, in the manner of administering regulations that would be established. 

Methods of payment for restoration of land that has not yet been mined do not present any great difficulties. There are a variety of devices which can be used which, in effect, have the cost of restoration included in the price of coal by requiring the coal operator to restore the land to a condition meeting the standards set out in the law. Performance bonds can be required which permit the administering body to do the reclamation if the operator defaults on his performance. Restoration of previously mined lands is more difficult to handle. The coal operator may have only leased the land, or he may have acquired only the mineral rights and not the surface rights. In many instances the coal company that had mined the land may no longer exist, and the present owner would rather abandon the property than pay for the cost of reclamation. Moreover, in order to reclaim a given area, it is often necessary that all of the land be treated as a whole; otherwise the benefits of restoration cannot be permanently achieved. 

The two methods that have been suggested for funding the treatment of previously damaged lands are through annual appropriations and the establishment of a reclamation fund. The latter could take several forms but would provide for the establishment of a revolving fund composed of collections from fines and for feitures. This fund would be used to restore land that had been newly disturbed but not reclaimed by the operator, as well as to help restore land previously damaged.

In the bill proposed by the Administration, 2 years would be allowed for States to develop regulations for strip and underground mining, in accordance with guidelines provided by the Secretary of Interior. The Secretary would be required to maintain a continual review of State regulations and advise the States when such regulations are inadequate; if corrections are not made, the Secretary would be authorized to withdraw his approval. In the event a State did not issue satisfactory regulations within 2 years, or approval is withdrawn, the Secretary would issue Federal regulations and enforce them, recovering the full cost of administration from the State. The proposal includes penalties for noncompliance, authorization for conducting research, and grants to States for the purpose of developing, administering, and enforcing environmental regulations. 

Other bills introduced in the 92d Congress contained provisions ranging from an absolute ban on all strip mining of coal 6 months after enactment to a simple requirement that the Secretary designate those public lands on which prospectors would not be allowed to explore with bulldozers or other mechanical equipment. If exploration using such equipment was intended, the individual or organization would be required to file a statement with the Secretary of his intent to explore in a given area and post a performance bond to assure reclamation of the area.

Representatives of the mining industry testified that strip mining is more in accord with the National policy of conserving natural resources, that it was unrealistic to expect strip mining could be replaced by underground mining in a short time, that the power grids of the Nation were dependent on surface-mined coal, and that its sudden removal would create unacceptable economic and social consequences. 

One coal company witness stated that his strip-mine operation will recover over 14 million tons from a deposit in which only 4 million tons could be recovered by underground mining. 

Witnesses opposed to strip mining emphasized the many adverse environmental costs associated with this type of mineral extraction. 

In his testimony, Congressman Hechler said:

Watertables are destroyed, depriving the earth of its channel of nourishment. 

The delicate surface fabric of life-supporting earth is cast to the bottom. Deep strata of rock and shale are pulverized and exposed to the elements, where they will leech acids and toxic minerals into the surrounding streams for generations. 

Mountains, now unstable, crack, slip and slide. Rains wash mud, sand and toxic substances down into the streams and rivers, filing their channels and poisoning their waters.

Representatives of conservationist organizations questioned the existence of an energy crisis of such magnitude that strip mining of coal was indispensible to meet energy demands. They pointed out that underground coal reserves are large enough to supply all of our demand for many years, and it is not necessary to resort to stripping. They also questioned whether technology is available to restore the land to a satisfactory condition following strip mining. A representative of the 

Wilderness Society said: 

. . reclamation is at best a myth and at worst a hoax if we delude ourselves into believing that we can re-establish anything but a shaky monoculture on strip-mined areas. 

Administration witnesses discussed at some length methods that can be used to prevent or correct the adverse effects of both underground

and strip mining. Reclamation was said not only to reduce pollution but to return the land to productive use. As proof, the experience of the government in the management of the mineral deposits that have been surface mined on federal lands was cited. Administration witnesses also noted that underground mining, which would have to be resorted to if strip mining were banned, also causes difficult-to-restore environmental problems from subsidence and acid mine damage.

Industry witnesses urged that the use of surface-mined land after mining should be left to the decision of the mine operator. 

A number of conservationist, industry and regulatory officials suggested that there were many problems peculiar to each of the different minerals that are surface-mined, and that any law which tries to treat the surface mining industry as though it were homogeneous would not be satisfactory or produce the results desired. Examples of these differences were quarries which must always be located near urban areas but for which there is no material left for land fill when the operation is stopped; the inactive mines and stockpiles of iron ore which represent a resource that can be used in case of a national emergency; and the development, over time, of new techniques to recover lean ores that are currently treated as waste. 

A number of witnesses offered suggestions as to how to treat lands that had been previously mined but not restored. The Conservation 

Foundation offered a plan under which a joint Federal-State plan would be developed for these areas which would provide for selective rehabilitation. Windfall profits to private owners would be prevented by placing liens on the land in amounts equalling their increased value as a result of the reclamation work. A revolving fund to purchase disturbed land and resell the reclaimed land would be established. On the other hand, the Administration said that prevention of future damage was more important than restoring affected lands and that the cost of restoring land damaged by previous mining could not be passed on to its despoilers but was too high for the government to assume. Senator 

Baker and other witnesses suggested that a per-ton tax be assessed on all minerals, such funds to be devoted exclusively to restoring land damaged by strip mining; one suggestion was that the tax be $2.50 per ton of coal, said to be the difference between the cost of strip and underground mining.

The effective date of any law that would be enacted raised numerous comments from witnesses. The administration bill would allow two years, while some bills would make the law effective in six months. 

Senator Cooper proposed that an 18-month period would be necessary in order for guidelines to be established, and for States to develop their plans and receive Federal approval. During the interim Senator 

Cooper would have surface mining conducted only under Federal authority. Conservation group witnesses were also in favor of a compressed timetable for the effective date of the legislation. 

Industry witnesses made numerous other suggestions for modifying the bills under consideration. These included deleting underground mines, including the right of judicial review, the removal of criminal sanctions, the requiring of public notice and hearings on any Federal guidelines, and providing guidelines for administration of any act consonant with the Mining and Minerals Policy Act of 1970. This latter Act established a public policy to encourage strong domestic

mining, minerals, and metallurgical industries and the sound development of mineral resources. 

The major issue raised at the hearings in both Houses was concerned with how the law would be administered. The industry view generally was that it would best if the Federal government established guidelines with which State governments would be required to comply. Unless the 

States met these guidelines, the Federal government would have to do the job itself. Those representing conservationist interests were no longer satisfied that State programs would work, and they favored a Federally-administered program; they claim that State enforcement programs have been feeble even when State laws have been adequate. 

The existing situation was criticized by one administration witness: 

Many of the State statutes are inadequate and ambiguous; some do not admit of equitable enforcement. State enforcement has been hampered by lack of funds and personnel. In addition, most of the State laws are too limited in coverage to provide a comprehensive remedy for the problem. 

Regulatory officials from the State of Pennsylvania argued that their experience indicated there should be an over-all Federal program, but that responsibility for enforcement should be with the individual States. Industry spokesmen noted that blanket Federal rules for reclamation would conflict with the historic role of State and local government in providing rational growth and land development. 

The other issue which raised sharply conflicting views concerned which Federal agency would take the lead role. The Administration bill would designate the Secretary of Interior, but with the requirement that the Secretary use an advisory committee consisting of representatives of Agriculture, Commerce, the Environmental Pro tection Agency, TVA, and the Appalachian Regional Commission to assist in preparing guidelines. The Secretary would also solicit the view of Federal agencies when approving State regulations and utilize the Department of Agriculture in the reclamation of areas affected by surface mining.

The proposal that the Department of Interior take the lead role was strongly supported by industry witnesses and by the representative of the United Mine Workers. They gave as reasons the accumulated knowledge that the Department had in both mining and reclamation matters. In general, the conservation groups favored giving the lead authority to the Environmental Protection Agency, since it would divorce enforcement duties from the Department of Interior's development and management functions with respect to mineral resources. This view was supported by both Senators Cooper and 

Baker. The National Association of Conservation Districts favored the Department of Agriculture as the lead agency since that Department is the recognized authority for dealing with erosion, land reclamation, and land conservation. Its nearly forty years of experience and large network of conservation districts would provide an excellent base on which to build the new strip mine reclamation enforcement responsibilities. 

(B) Coal Gasification 

As early as 1969 those concerned with gas supplies had warned that, if normal growth in demand and discovery rates continued, a gas shortage was inevitable. Moreover, its acceptability within the context of growing concern for environmental quality will result in

an accelerated demand, and this would further compound the supply problem. For the first time since records had been kept, new supplies of natural gas discovered for each of the three years, 1968, 1969 and 1970, were less than the gas consumed in each of those years. 

A staff report of the Federal Power Commission, released in 1969, concluded that, if natural gas demand were to be met in the decade of the 1970's and beyond, it would be necessary to (1) speed up exploration to increase gas resource base, and (2) develop supplementary gas sources. To implement these recommendations would require exploration incentives, improved Federal leasing policies, firm policies for the import of natural and liquified gas, and priorities for gas use when there was short supply. In addition, greatly increased research efforts would be required to develop economic methods for the production of synthetic gas from coal. Unless such supplemental gas supplies were found, regional deficiencies would occur and no further growth would be possible after 1974. 

In an effort to reduce anticipated shortages, on June 15th, 1971, Secretary Morton announced an accelerated schedule of Federal leases on the Outer Continental Shelf covering a 5-year period. It called for sales of leases each year through 1975. Disclosing a leasing schedule this far in advance would allow ample time for an evaluation of the environmental impact of the leases and would permit industry to plan for bids in a more orderly and systematic manner. 

Öther methods to reduce the potential gas shortage have also been proposed. Price incentives (see page 64), the development of a North 

American energy policy, the importation of liquified natural gas from 

North Africa and Venezuela, and the transportation of Alaskan gas to the lower 48 States are among the methods that have received the attention of the Congress or the Executive Branch, or both. 

The vast coal resources of the United States, from which gas was produced for many years before natural gas became widely available, will be needed to produce synthetic gas in commercial quantities by 1980. If this timetable is to be met, immediate action is required, for under the best of circumstances it is anticipated that from 6 to 10 years will be required to bring such a development into commercial operation. 

S. 1846 was introduced on May 12, 1971, with the objective of finding a method to meet this accelerated timetable for producing synthetic natural gas from coal in commercial quantities. On introducing this bill Senator Jackson said:

The development of a technology for coal gasification presents a financing problem that is becoming increasingly common in the energy field. Stated simply, research and development must be carried on at such large scale and at such enormous costs that it is impossible for any single company, even large energy or utility companies, to assume the risks involved within the limitations upon management practices and financial capabilities.

Another serious problem with such highly complex and expensive research and development is that it is becoming more difficult for those who invest in the research to reap its benefits. This is because of antitrust laws, nonpatentability of processes, and the difficulty of maintaining exclusive positions and proprietary knowledge. 

S. 1846 was designed to: (1) Create a Government-industry, jointly supported and operated corporation with the objective of demonstrating on a commercial scale at least two alternative methods for the production of gas from coal;
