(2) The corporation would have a life limited to this single objective and would be abolished when the objective was attained; (3) Financing would be shared by Government and industry thus assuring a deep commitment by industry to the successful outcome of the effort; and (4) The corporation would be managed by an entity responsible to a Board of Directors made up of representatives of industry and Government. The ratio of industry to Government financial support could be used as the basis for division of membership on the board. 

Hearings on S. 1846 were held on July 27th and 28th, 1971. All of the witnesses who testified or submitted letters for the record were in agreement with the need for an accelerated coal gasification program as well as with Section 1 of the bill, which outlines the need for such a bill.

The Administration opposed passage of the bill on the ground that the President's program on energy gave a new initiative to coal gasification research and this should have an opportunity to go forward before any consideration was given to an independent corporation to carry out the provisions of S. 1846. The administration program included the design, construction and operation of pilot plants using 7 different gasification processes. The pilot program was expected to require 4 years, after which one or more of the processes would be selected for demonstration; the second phase of the program was expected to take 5 years.

The President's program was jointly sponsored by industry, represented by the American Gas Association, and the Department of the Interior. Expenditures during the first four years would be at the rate of $30 million per year, the costs to be shared by government and industry, with industry supplying one-third of the total expenditures. 

Dr. Chilton, Chairman of the National Academy of Engineering 

Committee investigating the state of coal gasification technology for the Department of Interior, expressed concern about the timing and financial commitments which would be needed under S. 1846. Similar views were also given by witnesses from the University of Wyoming and the National Coal Association. 

Several witnesses suggested that both the Department of the Interior American Gas Association program and the program called for by S. 1846 should be pursued.

Gas industry witnesses generally endorsed the objectives of S. 1846 and hoped that the bill would "serve as the vehicle for the final move from the pilot plant stage into the demonstration plant stage, and then to full commercialization. This would seem to be an effective and a most appropriate way to pursue the prime objective to wit, commercialization of coal gasification with the least possible delay.

The Administration also objected to the creation of a new organization to carry out the research, since it would appear to be incompatible with the President's proposed reorganization which would group all energy functions in a Department of Natural Resources. 

Witnesses favoring the bill stated that administration by an existing Department would blunt the urgency the program requires. 

Endorsement of the proposal for an accelerated program by a Coal 

Gasification Development Corporation came from Congressional witnesses, the National Association of Regulatory Utilities Commis-

sioners, the Consolidated Edison Co. of New York, Pullman, Inc.,

Burns and Roe, and others. They expressed the view that, while some risk was involved, technology was sufficiently advanced to build a demonstration plant; the great urgency connected with the need for this program justifies such risks. 

The National Wildlife Federation called attention to the provision for selection of the final process based on consideration of only technical and economic factors. The Federation witness urged that environmental factors also be considered, and that opportunity be given for citizen participation in carrying the program forward. 

Subsequent to the hearings, a number of newspaper stories appeared reporting that several companies were planning to proceed with construction of fuel-scale gasification plants; some were reported to be planning to use coal, and others, petroleum products as feed stocks.

Hearings were conducted on November 19, 1971 to obtain detailed information on the plans of various companies with respect to these commercial coal gasification plants, including size, location, timing, availability of resources (coal and water) and provision for environmental protection. Also to be determined were the types of processes to be used, their state of development, process limitations (if any) and, to the extent possible, anticipated costs of the synthetic gas to be produced.

In the case of administration witnesses the hearings were designed to develop answers to the following questions: (1) Has the additional Federal funding called for in the President's energy message been requested? What stage of the budget process has it reached? (2) Have industry commitments for their share of the funds. been received? Have the funds actually been made available to the Department? If not, when will they be? (3) What are the terms of the government-industry cooperative agreement? (4) Does the announcement of commercial-sized development change Interior research plans? If so, how? If not, why not?

Representatives of the Department of the Interior testified that numerous announcements about commercial-sized synthetic gas plants did not alter the industry-government coal gasification program described in the President's energy message. First, most of the announced plants would use some petroleum fraction as a feedstock, and not coal. Secondly, the coal-based plants which were to be constructed would use a proven technology not applicable to many U.S. coals, and was a relatively high cost process. The joint industry-government program would be coal-based, since the use of petroleum feedstocks did not represent a long-term solution to the gas shortage problem. 

Moreover, the processes under study were expected to be more efficient and capable of using any coal seam in the U.S. Therefore, the fouryear experimental program originally announced would proceed to the point where a 5,000-ton-per-day plant producing 80,000 million cubic feet per day would be constructed and operated. This would use one full-scale gasifier, but in a plant which would be one-third the size of a full-scale plant.

Administration witnesses reported that the Congress had been asked for an additional $10,000,000 in a supplemental appropriation.19 

19 The appropriation later passed was for $5,000,000.

The Gas Association had firm committments for its share of the funds, and copies of the terms of the agreement between industry and the Department of Interior were provided for the record. 

Representatives of El Paso, which had announced a 250-millioncubic-feet-per-day plant using coal as the raw material, testified with respect to their plans. El Paso supplies about 11% of the total gas demand of the U.S., but has been unable, since 1966, to acquire new gas supplies anywhere except in Canada. While the company urges that other alternatives be explored for alleviating the natural gas shortage (incentives to explore, importation of liquified natural gas, Alaskan gas, etc.) they believe that coal gasification is the best alternative for ultimately relieving the natural gas shortage. 

El Paso has 40,000 acres under lease with a coal reserve of 600 million tons under less than 150 feet of overburden. The company chose the Lurgi process since it was the only proven gasification process and was suitable for the coals that they have under lease. Although the Lurgi process produces only a 400 BTU gas (without methanation) even if the methanation step should not prove to be feasible, this gas could be successfully blended into the natural gas in the pipeline without adversely affecting its use by consumers. This would permit El 

Paso to help supply some gas to potential consumers they would otherwise be unable to serve if they waited for new technology to be developed before going ahead with a coal gasification plant. There also would be an opportunity to train operators and technicians to improve the process through operating experience gained, and to work on components of the process that would be of value to the R & D program being undertaken by government and industry. 

The consortium formed by Texas Eastern Transmission Co. (a pipeline transmission company), Utah International (a coal mining company), and Pacific Light Service Co. (a gas distributor) testified that they have an economic and technical feasibility study underway which should be completed by mid-1972. If the results are favorable, they would expect to have a coal gasification plant, using San Juan Basin coal, in operation by 1975. Both water and coal are available for several gas plants. The coal reserves cover 31,000 acres and contain 1.1 billion tons of recoverable coal. With the very large anticipated market for gas in Southern California, and with the inability to obtain new gas reserves in recent years, the gas distribution company felt that the potential for coal gasification should be examined now. 

The price of the gas that would be produced is currently estimated at about $1 per 1000 cubic feet.

The FMC Corporation said that they were attempting to interest a number of firms in their COGAS process; this has as its product both oil and gas. Oil is first removed from the coal and the residual char is used to produce a high BTU gas by a new proprietary process. 

If the oil can be sold for $4 a barrel, the gas could be produced for about 75 to 90 cents per million BTU. In the view of FMC, the government could best assist the coal gasification development by creating a climate in which private companies could use the technology that they have developed. If the government would secure financing on a reasonable basis, it could materially reduce costs of the gas. For example, if average interest rates are 11% on plant capital investment, the final gas price would be 82 cents per 1000 cubic feet; if the interest

rate were lowered to 7% the gas cost would be reduced to 70 cents per 1000 cubic feet. On questioning, the FMC witness stated that the pilot plant unit to test the COGAS product has not yet been operated exclusively.

A representative of a Coal Gasification Group formed at the request of the Illinois Commerce Commission explained that the objective of the group was to decide how the high sulfur coal resources of Illinois could best be used to provide a clean fuel for consumers in the area. 

Because of its sulfur content, the coal can no longer be used directly for generation of electricity, at least until methods are found to extract the sulfur from the coal or the sulfur oxide from the flue gases. This has resulted in market dislocation for both producers and users. Moreover, natural gas is in short supply in the region, and economic ways to convert the coal to gas could be helpful in simultaneously overcoming both problems. At present the interest is in studying processes and in investigating the local environmental and legal restraints, identifying possible plant sites, and investigating alternative methods of organizing the various interests to meet the common goal of using Illinois resources.

The American Gas Association witness testified that, while they continued to support S 1846 as they had done previously, the joint research program should be undertaken first, and the Coal Development Corporation would be used in the final step of commercial demonstration. The Association was greatly encouraged by the decision of some of its members to go ahead with the one proven process (Lurgi), since it insured that results of the R & D program would be applied quickly. 

Although the Lurgi process has been proven, the AGA feels that processes still to be tested offer more promise in the long run and that process development is still urgently needed for the full scale commercial developments that are anticipated.

The Senate Interior Committee has taken no action with respect to 

S 1846. 

(c) Freight Car Shortages 

For many years the U.S. has been faced with shortages of freight cars, a situation which is aggravated during periods of the year when there are high seasonable demands for their use. Although the utilization of cars has improved in recent years, the weak financial condition of railroads has not permitted them to purchase new cars that are needed. Senate hearings were held on May 19 and 21, June 3, 16, 19 and 29 on Senate Bills S 1415, S 1729, S 1730 and S 1731, each of which offered a different method to correct this problem. Additional hearings were held in Portland, Oregon, on September 20 to gain a better understanding of local problems associated with freight car shortages. The fuels industry is involved, since it is the largest user of hopper cars, which it employs for transporting coal from mine to market.

The railroad industry generally favored S 1730 and S 1731. S 1730 would create a non-profit, Federally-incorporated company that would purchase and make available to the railroads a pool of freight cars, while S 1731 would provide for loans to railroads, insured by the government, for the purchase of additional freight cars. There was no serious opposition to S 1415 which would permit the Department of 

Defense to purchase 10,000 cars to help relieve shortages.

The Administration generally opposed the bills at this time because it considered that this problem must be legislated in the context of an overall rail transport program which has not yet been formulated. 

However, the Department of Transportation did endorse the loan bill (S. 1731) with some amendments.

The coal industry testified in support of any bill that would overcome the persistent freight car shortages. More than 70% of all the coal mined is moved by rail; this represents about 11% of the total income of all railroads. With anticipated demand for additional coal that will be needed for the generation of electricity in the decade of the seventies, some solution to the chronic freight car shortages is required. It was stated that shortages have a greater financial effect on the coal industry now than they have had in the past. As the coal industry mechanized it became more capital-intensive. Any short circuiting of the smooth flow of coal from mine to market now has a more important effect on coal profitability. The severity of the freight car shortage problem on one railroad system was demonstrated by its inability to move to market over 1 million tons of coal which had been purchased in the last half of 1969 and the first half of 1970. 

Both representatives of the United Mine Workers and the National Coal Association took no position on which of the bills would be best for the country as a whole, but supported conceptually any legislation that would provide a quick but permanent solution to their critical need for additional freight cars. The Coal Association also suggested that the five-year amortization provision granted to freight cars owned by the railroads be extended to those cars owned and leased by utilities or other private owners.

Field hearings were held by the House Subcommittee on Investigation of the Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, on freight car shortages for the first time in the history of the Subcommittee. 

These investigative (rather than legislative) hearings were held on 

July 16 at Great Falls, Montana, on July 17, at Fargo, North Dakota, and October 1 at Houston, Texas. Their purpose was to develop the basic facts with respect to shortages, costs of transportation, whether there had been unncessary delays or inefficient marketing practices, and to identify possible remedies for the problems uncovered. The hearings dealt largely with shortages relating to agricultural commodities and to timber. The indirect effect that these shortages produce on the availability of freight cars for coal use and the possible impact that proposed solutions might have on the availability of coal freight cars is of interest to the fuels industry. 

(D) Black Lung Benefits A major provision of the Federal Coal Mine Health and Safety Act of 1969 was the establishment of a program authorizing benefit pay- ments to miners totally disabled by the disease "pneumoconiosis" or "black lung". This program was the subject of a great deal of discussion, and several proposed amendments were introduced. On May 10, 1971, hearings on four such amendments-H.R. 18, H.R. 42, H.R. 43, and H.R. 5702-were held by the Subcommittee on General Labor of the Committee on Education and Labor. 

H.R. 18 proposed the extension of Title IV of the Health and Safety 

Act. This would extend until the end of 1973, rather than the end of 

1972, the deadline to file for benefits for existing cases under the

program. The reasons behind this proposal was the feeling that because of problems associated with initiation of State programs, as well as those indicated by the other three bills, additional time would be necessary for receiving applications. The program is designed to be replaced by State programs. 

Title IV as written allows for benefits to miners or their widows; this is considered to have been an oversight in the original law. H.R. 

42 would extend benefits to orphans whose fathers died of black lung. 

There was considerable discussion of the wording of this amendment, as it would seem to allow payments to adults who were orphaned when they were children.

As presently administered, recipients of black lung benefits often have Social Security benefits reduced, sometimes to the point of not receiving any gain in benefits. H.R. 43 would specifiy that black lung benefits are not part of workmen's compensation programs, so that there would not be offsetting reductions in Social Security benefits. 

This is an issue that apparently had come up during consideration of the 1969 Act. It was stated that reporting of the 1969 Act had been delayed over the issue of deduction of disability insurance. Backers of the black lung benefits program turned down a compromise provision for a 50% deduction, and reported the bill without provision for deduction. An Administrative procedure has effectively created the deduction even though it was not written into the law. H.R. 43 would end this practice by spelling out the original intent of the Congress. 

A letter from the insurance industry opposed H.R. 43 because it would allow the disabled worker more money for staying home than for going back to work; even with the offsetting reduction the miner is receiving the equivalent of his full salary. The reduction was also thought to be justified because the Black Lung benefits program was passed because of inadequacy of State workmens compensation programs.

H.R. 5702 would define disability as the inability to work as a miner, and prohibit denial of claims solely on the basis of a chest x-ray. There was considerable concern expressed respecting the difficulty of proving eligibility for the program; numerous constituent letters were introduced to point out this problem. In Kentucky, for example, 72% of applicants are denied benefits as compared with 

56% for West Virginia and 31% for Pennsylvania. Complaints were made that large numbers of people were being run through x-ray exams and then denied eligibility. Discussion with a doctor from a coal mining area indicated the difficulty of diagnosing black lung. The administration of the program was complimented, but problems resulting from inflexible policies were pointed out, as was the lack of understanding of procedures by the claimants. Another insurance industry letter requested reconsideration of the proposed definition of disability, which allows compensation to workers who can perform other jobs, perhaps at higher pay. The letter suggested this would cause reluctance to increase benefits. As part of the Coal Mine Health and Safety Act, this definition would be incorporated into the State programs when established.

Witnesses included seven Congressmen from coal mining states, a representative of the United Mine Workers-who endorsed all four bills, and a citizen group representative.

The Committee reported out H.R. 9212, a clean bill, based on H.R. 

18, H.R. 42, H.R. 43, and H.R. 5702. H.R. 9212 calls for a two-year extension rather than the year called for in H.R. 18. It is expected that state programs could take over after that time, even though there has been very little progress at that level so far. Designation of double orphans eligible for benefits (as in H.R. 42) is carefully spelled out, with benefits ending upon reaching adulthood. H.R. 9212 contains no definition of total disability (as in H.R. 5702) but prohibits using x-rays as the sole basis for denial of claims. Specification of distinctness of black lung benefits from workmens compensation programs (as in 

H.R. 43) is also maintained.

On October 18, 1971 motion was made in the House floor to pass H.R. 9212 under suspension of the rules, but the necessary two-thirds majority was not achieved. On November 10, 1973, H.R. 9212 with minor amendments was passed by the House of Representatives; the bill was the subject of considerable floor defate on both dates. The most discussed issue was that of the use of x-rays for diagnosis, but the cost of the program and the offset in Social Security benefits also received attention. 

On December 1 and 2, 1971 the Subcommittee on Labor of the Committee on Labor and Public Welfare of the United States Senate held hearings on proposed amendments to Title IV of the Federal Coal Mine Health and Safety Act of 1969. These included H.R. 9212 as passed by the House of Representatives, S. 2289, and S. 2675. S. 2289 proposes a definition of total disability as the inability to do work similar to that which the claimant had performed throughout most of his career. (Definition of total disability was excluded from H.R. 

9212). S. 2675 contains several of the provision of H.R. 9212; it differs most importantly in that it includes not only black lung but all respiratory and pulmonary impairments.

The witness list was similar to that for the hearings held by the 

House of Representatives. Dr. Rasmussen of the Appalachian Re gional Hospital was a primary witness. A panel of doctors that had investigated the issue also gave testimony. Among new topics discussed were other occupational hazards of coal mining and whether the original bill would have been passed had the States' workmen's compensation programs dealt with black lung. The question was raised as to whether, with passage of the amendment, it would be necessary to show that impairment was the result of coal mining. It was proposed that legislation include language to the effect that impairment would be assumed to be the result of coal mining, and that surface mines would be included. An amendment has been offered to the latter effect. Field hearings are intended. (E) Hyden, Kentucky Mine Disaster

The Federal Coal Mine Health and Safety Act of 1969 was signed into law on December 30, 1969. Exactly one year later, an explosion in a coal mine at Hyden, Kentucky took the lives of 38 workers. 

The Committee on Education and Labor of the House of Representa tives held hearings on March 9, 10, 11, 1971 in Washington and March 

12 and 13, 1971, in Hazard, Kentucky. These hearings concerned

H.R. 5680, which would require filing of reports on mining accidents, and other proposed amendments to the Federal Coal Mine Health and Safety Act. However, the actual subject of these hearings was the disaster at Hyden.

These hearings essentially became an investigation of responsibility for the disaster. The Committee's majority report placed a "heavy burden of responsibility for the Hyden tragedy" upon the Bureau of Mines for not enforcing the Health and Safety Act vigorously or recognizing the danger in this mine and closing it down. The minority report did not feel that the Bureau of Mines should be considered responsible and criticized the majority report both for its conclusions and for its handling of procedural questions. 

A report on the investigation of the Hyden disaster was issued in June of 1971. The minority report states that no members of the issuing subcommittee were able to review the document, and that no approval of it was obtained. After this protest, approval was obtained in July and another print containing minority views was issued. 

Congressman Landgrebe concurred with the minority report, but added concern for the inflexibility of the standards of the Coal Mine Health and Safety Act of 1969, and suggested the Committee investigate means of achieving standards more appropriate to physical differences among various mines. 

The Hyden mine had accumulated a long list of violations, had been cited three times for conditions of imminent danger, and had reported two accidents. There had been citations for inadequate rock dusting and for coal dust levels ten times the permissible levels. There had also been a fatal accident resulting from failure to replace faulty equipment that had been cited for imminent danger, and reported evidence of drinking by the mine manager. The majority report stated that the Bureau should have been cognizant of these conditions and more aggressive in inspection, issuance of citations, and follow-up on violations. It concluded that this might have prevented the disaster. 

Although there had been eight inspections in the nine months of the mine's operation, only one had been complete. The Bureau's explanation was that the infrequent complete inspection resulted from a shortage of inspectors. This was not considered justifiable in the majority report, since there had been no shortage of funds. The minority report contended that the Bureau could be as aggressive as possible in inspecting and levying penalties, but that this would still not guarantee that the mine operator would obey the law. As for the shortage of inspectors, this was considered understandable because of a shortage of qualified applicants.

Investigation of the disaster revealed that the explosion could be directly attributed to (1) use of nonpermissable explosives, (2) firing of an illegal number of charges at one time, and (3) excessive accumulations of coal dust. The minority report indicated that the Bureau had no knowledge of, nor reason to expect the use of illegal explosives or the setting off of too many charges. The majority report said that the use of the illegal explosive was widely known in the community. 

Certain violations were due to be abated by December 22 and others by December 28-two days before the explosion. Inspectors did not return, as the law required, to determine whether or not these corrections had been carried out. The minority report pointed out though that on December 22 there was no follow up because inspections are made only when the mine is operating, and the inspector was told that the mine would be closed for a holiday. Also, none of the violations that were to be abated contributed to the disaster.

The mine had also had a non-fatal accident which had not been reported. At the time of the report there had been no citation for this. 

The majority felt that lack of penalty for failure to report accidents is very serious, and this is the subject of H.R. 5680, the official subject of the March hearings.

Involved in the explosion were two adjoining interconnected mines that could be effectively considered as one mine. The majority report held that the Bureau's treatment of the operation as two distinct mines might have contributed to continuation of unsafe conditions. 

If the records for the two mines had been kept as one, the resulting total number of violations might have been sufficient to close the mine for "unwarranted failure" to correct violations. The minority report pointed out that a closure order given for a section of one mine did not require abandoning the rest of that mine. It concluded, therefore that considering them as one more should not have required closing both.

The majority report was sharply critical of the procedural aspects of the Bureau of Mines post-disaster investigation, referring to the public hearing held as a "sham," and saying that it may have impeded the purpose of the investigation. Criticism was also made that widows were not advised of the right of autopsy which might have made them eligible for black lung benefits. The minority countered that the object of the hearing, which was to obtain additional information, was accomplished, and that the success of the hearings was borne out by the indictments issued against the mine on June 23, 1971, which were based on the hearing and on the Bureau's investigations. The minority report said that performing autopsies to check for black lung is

HEW's responsibility, and that the issue was irrelevant to the investigation.

In February of 1971, the Bureau of Mines made an assessment against the mine for violations at the time of the disaster. These were protested, and lengthy court action could result. In the minority's view, several suggestions for further study by the Committee were made. These included safety training programs, Federal-State coordination, appropriateness of standards to small mines, more frequent investigations of mines with bad records, and stricter safety regulations for individual miners. (F) United Mine Workers' Election

The United Mine Workers' Union is one of the major unions in this country and, because of its involvement in coal and other fuel industries, it is relevant to the energy problem. Under the leadership of 

John L. Lewis, the UMW was for many years vigorously and tightly directed. Strong conflicts developed within the Union after Mr. 

Lewis's death. In the 1969 elections, Joseph Yablonski unsuccessfully challenged Mr. Boyle for Presidency of the Union. (Mr. Boyle had taken over upon Mr. Lewis' death.) Shortly thereafter the murder of 

Mr. Yablonski, his wife, and his daughter became a major national news item. Opposition within the Union to Mr. Boyle then organized as the Miners for Democracy, and ran candidates in the 1970 Union elections. They claimed severe repression and numerous irregularities by the incumbent Union leadership during the campaign and at the polls.

Shortly after the murder of Mr. Yablonski, the Subcommittee on 

Labor of the Committee on Labor and Public Welfare of the United States Senate had begun inquiry into charges of misuse of funds in the 1969 election. On July 12 and 13, 1971, the Subcommittee held hearings to take testimony from Miners for Democracy about alleged repression and irregularities in District 5 in the 1970 elections. Among the charges were: (1) that the incumbent's campaign was financed by illegally-used Union funds and by forced contributions, (2) that members and supporters of the opposition were threatened with expulsion from the Union for being dual unionists, (3) that some locals were not legally constituted and the boundaries of others were manipulated, and (4) that the votes from three locals were thrown out, changing the election results. The UMW is different from most unions in that retired workers remain in the Union and can vote. They constitute about 50% of the membership. A substantial increase in pension payments by Mr. Boyle before the election is claimed to have been a political move that has bankrupted the pension fund.

Among reasons for government involvement are: (1) the fact that 

1964 court cases regarding Union trusteeships have not yet come to trial, (2) charges that the Department of Labor was indifferent to the complaints of Miners for Democracy, and (3) charges that the 

Labor-Management Reporting and Disclosure Act of 1959 (LMRDA), or the Landrum-Griffin Act, is not being enforced.

Senator Griffin testified, expressing his views on the administration of the LMRDA, both in regard to this case and in general. 

Litigation seeking to set aside the 1969 election is presently in court. Reports of this committee which were submitted to the Department of Justice are being used in the trial. The Committee is contin- 'ng its investigation and may schedule additional hearings. 71-900-72—-7

VII. Electricity (A) Power Plant Reliability and Power Plant Siting 

The "Northeast Blackout" of November 1965, during which power was interrupted for periods of a few minutes to over a half a day in an area encompassing 80,500 square miles affecting an estimated 30 million people, resulted in a large number of bills being introduced in the 89th Congress aimed at strengthening the reliability of electric power supply.

In that and succeeding Congresses, problems with respect to electric power supply and reliability began to receive attention. These focused on the environmental effects of power generation, methods to insure bulk power supply, and the many difficulties that had arisen with respect to power plant siting. Numerous bills were introduced in the 

89th, 90th, 91st, and 92nd Congresses aimed at providing relief for one or more of these problems.

Electric power generation using existing technology is inefficient; only 40 percent of the energy in the original fuel is turned into electricity in even the most advanced plants. The balance of the fuel energy is injected into the environment in the form of heat to the air and to cooling water used in condensers. In addition fossil fuel plants emit significant quantities of air pollutants and, when coal is used, pose an additional problem in the disposal of the ash that is collected. Nuclear plants, while essentially free of air pollutants, give rise to radioactive waste disposal difficulties and, because they are currently less efficient than fossil fuel plants, create more thermal pollution per kilowatt generated.

The difficulties of assessing the overall environmental impact of fossil fuel plants as compared with nuclear plants led to a series of detailed hearings conducted by the Joint Committee on Atomic Energy in 

October and November 1969 and January and February 1970. The 

Committee's interest was in obtaining full disclosure of the environmental effects of all types of power stations-fossil fuels, hydroelectirc and nuclear. Presentations by witnesses at hearings included discussion of the quantities of pollutants-air, water, solid wastes, radioactive waste and the impact on aesthetic values-associated with the generation of electricity by the various methods currently in use. The costs and state of abatement technology for each of the pollutants were discussed. The possible tradeoff of pollution by shifts in fuel and by type of abatement process was also examined.

In August of 1970 the Energy Policy Staff of the Office of Science and Technology issued a new report on "Electric Power and the Environment." The report recommended that long range planning encompassing large regions be carried out on a continuing basis. The planning horizon for regional plans should be at least 10 years and preferably 15 years in advance of construction. Institutions should be established to insure that all parties, including those concerned with environmental effects, should become involved in the process at the earliest pos- (88)

sible date. The nine existing Electric Reliability Councils formed in cooperation with the Federal Power Commission would be a satisfactory vehicle for this comprehensive planning. 

During the 91st Congress hearings were held on S. 2752 which was designed to speed up the siting process. It would have established 

Federal criteria for powerplant siting and provide a regional mechanism to coordinate planning of power development. All three levels of 

Government-Federal, State and local-would be involved, but final approval of standards and sites would rest with a Federal agency. 

Most of the witnesses considered the objectives of the bill commendable, but believed that it would simply provide for an added layer of Government approval rather than a consolidation of existing layers. 

These and previous hearings showed that the problems of siting new plants were becoming more complex each year and that the growing concern about protecting the environment would surely further aggravate the problem at all three governmental regulatory levels.

A large number of bills designed to solve the siting problem were introduced in the 92d Congress, and hearings were held by the House Subcommittee on Communication and Power of the Committee on 

Interstate and Foreign Commerce. Eight bills, each of which varied somewhat from the others, were considered. 

A comparative analysis of the major provisions of the bills is given in Appendix K. Appendix L comprises a list of organizations represented at the hearings and is an indication of the intense interest in this problem by diverse groups. 

All of the bills covered large facilities, and required the establishment of regional planning councils (but the organizational rules and membership were not always identical); seven required that regional plans be established, but the method to be used and the length of the planning period varied; and judicial review was provided in five. 

The most important differences among the bills were in the provisions for certification of new plant sites. The administration bill (H.R. 5277) proposed that each State designate a certifying agency in accordance with guidelines prescribed by the President. It contained procedures for selecting the best of the alternative sites. When the state agency met Federal standards it would receive a certificate of qualification; if no state certifying agency were named plants would be certified by a Federal agency. All federal plants would receive federal certification by a Federal Certifying Agency (FCA). If the

FPC found that bulk power facilities were being impaired by delays caused in state certification, the FCA would assume jurisdiction.

The judgment of the certifying body would be conclusive on all environmental matters, but all applicable Federal standards would have to be met. 

In H.R. 6970, a bill introduced by Cong. MacDonald, each utility would submit detailed construction proposals 2 years in advance for generating plants. These would be consistent with regional plans and describe the steps that had been taken to get approval by various public agencies. In the case of delays due to disputes over environmental matters, the FPC would convene a Special Panel made up of one person named by FPC and one by the CEQ. The Panel, after a demonstration that construction plans have met other criteria, would

select the plan that "best assures reliable power consistent with environmental considerations."

H.R. 1079 contained no provision with respect to certification procedures. H.R. 7045 was similar to the administration proposal, but proposed a State Joint Board instead of the FCA; it did not provide 

Presidential guidelines for certifying bodies.

H.R. 3838 proposed that each utility obtain FPC approval (in addition to State approval) at least six months in advance of proposed construction. If there were no objection to the proposal (or acceptable modifications made by FPC) the proposal would be adopted. An 

Electric Power Environmental Council (EPEC) would review each proposal to which objection was raised to determine if construction were consistent with environmental considerations. If not, EPEC would file an objection which would have the effect of a suspension order. H. R. 5486 is similar, except that it relates only to transmission lines. 

H.R. 6971 would permit any utility to request the FPC to issue a certificate if it met requirements set out in the bill. A State joint board would be convened (State and Federal representatives) which would establish its own operating rules. In case of disagreement in the Board, the Commissioners of the FPC would review and could allow new proceedings to be conducted. 

H.R. 6972 would provide that proposals for facilities be released 2 years in advance of construction, accompanied by certification that all applicable Federal_and_environmental standards had been met. FPC and the Advisory Panel on the Environment would review proposals, and if their comments were not accepted, the reasons must be given. If standards are claimed not to have been met, the FPC would submit a protest to the agency, and its response would be conclusive. 

Administration witnesses at the hearings indicated their preference for H.R. 5277 over the other bills because of its provisions relating to long range planning, public hearings, application for certificates well in advance of construction, a single decision making body, and a set of 

Federal guidelines for certifying agencies with respect to applicable environmental standards. H.R. 5277 also proposes vesting the siting responsibility with the States, while retaining a supporting role for the 

Federal Government.

The administration argued that H.R. 5277 would provide for a more permanent body which would operate more efficiently than "ad hoc" bodies proposed by other bills. Moreover, it would provide a higher degree of public participation in the planning and site approval process, and would more nearly provide for "one stop" determination of environmental and power supply issues. It was also said to be consistent with the land-use planning bill proposed by the administration. 

In later testimony the Antitrust Division of the Department of Justice contended that unless the administration bill added several "savings" provisions, the principal provisions would cloud the applicability of the antitrust laws to the power industry. 

The AEC supported the administration bill, but attempted to clarify a number of its provisions so that equal weight would be given to electrical requirements and environmental protection, undue burdens would not be placed on the utilities by two hearings-one 5 years, and one 2 years before construction, and the State certifying powers

would be conclusive. The AEC also suggested that the arbitration board not have jurisdiction over radiation matters. 

T.V.A. witnesses were concerned that none of the bills would give a "one stop" service that would prevent long drawn-out legal battles. 

In commenting on H.R. 6970, TVA said that the responsibility for reliable electric power should remain with the individual supplier of electricity and not with the regional councils. 

The Chairman of the National Academy of Engineering (NAE) 

committee_addressing the problem of resolving the conflict between power production and preserving the environment suggested that solutions could be found by the application of good engineering principles undertaken early in process. The NAE committee expressed the view that legislation should provide for a "one stop" procedure, and for clarification the role of the Regional Reliability Councils, but should not use the arbitration method or include projects already started. 

The Chairman of the FPC, while supporting the administration bill thought that it "was too predominately oriented with environmental emphasis". He stated that the 5-year advance hearings on siting inventories, combined with a 2-year advance application would require 2 hearings-one of which he thought unnecessary. Further, he considered that the "one stop" provisions were inadequate, and that there should be preemptive emergency certification procedures.

Finally, he said that the FPC should have the fundamental responsibility for reliability of power considerations.

A matter that received considerable discussion throughout hearings was the arbitration procedure provided in H.R. 6970, but not included in the administration bill. However, the question of whether a decision by the Arbitration Board would be any better than an administrative decision was unanswered. The Chairman of the FPC felt that the arbitration provisions of H.R. 6970 were valid, but the same function could be accomplished by utilizing one agency (FPC). 

Commissioner O'Connor of the FPC expressed the view that the arbitration provisions of H.R. 6970 would probably be more advantageous to environmentalists than the Environmental Quality Council provision of H.R. 6872. There was disagreement among witnesses with respect to the limitations placed on judicial review. Commissioner 

Carver of FPC testified that one of his main objections to the bill was its proposed transfer of Federal powers to a non-Federal instrumentality.

The Edison Electric Institute (EEI), representing the privately owned utility industries, indicated that no additional legislation was needed either in the field of long-range planning or in the area of reliability of bulk power supply. In its view the bills being considered would only add "another layer of regulation on top of existing layers", but it was in favor of the arbitration provisions of H. R. 6079. Criticism of H.R. 5277 centered on its failure to focus on the problem of multiple federal agency jurisdiction. The proposed Federal guidelines were viewed as unduly restrictive and the EEI proposed that in place of the legislation under consideration, a bill which would provide "a federal forum for overcoming impasses in obtaining regulatory approvals" would be welcome.

Representatives of the American Public Power Association (APPA) 

representing 1400 local (mostly municipally owned) power systems

submitted a list of principles which they considered a good bill should · contain, but stated that they could support either H.R. 5277 or H.R. 

6970 if they were amended to include provisions that would satisfy these principles.

The Atomic Industrial Forum supported the objectives of H.R. 

6970, but raised two question with respect to the arbitration panel. 

These were: Would the workload be so great as to create staffing problems, and; would not the provision that the panel recommend alternative solutions to a problem it was considering open a new dimension to the panel's authority that it could not handle? 

Representative of the National Rural Electric Cooperative Asso ciation (NRECA) supported the need for federal legislation to help utilities in resolving siting problems. NRECA strongly endorsed the provisions of H.R. 6970, which would permit the State to make the initial determination of siting decisions, as well as a mechanism for 

Federal intervention when the State procedures fail. NRECA also supported the inclusion of all utilities, including publicly-owned ones, so that they might obtain the benefits of bulk supply. NRECA representatives expressed some concern about the basis on which a judicial review could overturn a decision of the Arbitration Board. 

The National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissions 

(NARUC) testified in favor of the bill (H.R. 7045) introduced on their behalf, and argued that it was preferable to the administration bill in that a State Joint Board rather than a Federal agency would act in the absence of a qualified State or regional regulatory body. On the other hand, NARUC supported the arbitration procedure contained in H.R. 6970 with modification; it was in favor of the "substantial evidence" rather than "arbitrary or capricious" role as the basis for judicial review.

The National Electric Reliability Council (NERC) did not take a position on any of the bills, but was concerned that any regulations with respect to siting not require mandatory review so that plant sites about which no opposition had been raised could be sited without requiring further review. NERC approved of the arbitration approach to resolving disputes, but thought that the authority should rest with a permanent organization rather than the ad hoc procedure proposed in H.R. 6970. The witnesses opposed the concept of making the ERC's statutory creatures, and wanted to keep them in their present voluntary form. 

Along with NERC, several regional Electric Reliability Councils also testified on the provisions of various bills and their relative im portance and usefulness. The Texas Electric Reliability Council believed all matters concerning the environmental aspects of siting should be settled at the local level, and that Federal siting legislation "would hinder rather than improve either the protection of the public interest or the ability to meet the growing electrical requirements." 

The regional council of the Wisconsin-Illinois-Minnesota-Iowa-Upper 

Michigan area was mainly concerned with the need for an effective one-stop certification at the Federal level. 

Comments received from the Administrative Conference of the 

United States on H.R. 6970 related largely to clarification of language to insure that there be no delays in setting up the panel, that procedures for operation of the panel be strengthened, and that methods be de-

vised to avoid the long delays occasioned by the judicial review procedure in the bill.

Individual utility companies had different views on the adequacy of the various bills, some supporting the administration bill, with or without modification, and others supporting H.R. 6970. Pacific Gas and Electric Co., for example, felt that H.R. 7045, the bill supported by NARUC, was superior to the administration bill because it eliminated the guidelines concept while retaining most of the control by the States. However, the addition of a "one stop" control agency for meeting Federal requirements would be necessary if the objectives of speeding up power plant siting were to be achieved. 

Public Service of Indiana felt the administration bill would cause "such delays that the planning, construction, and operating of electric generating and transmission facilities would practically be impossible." 

The President of the New England Electric System supported those provisions of H.R. 6970 that recognized the need for a balance between power requirements and environmental factors, provided arbitration features, and set limitations on judicial review. Suggestions were made for improving the language of the bill to insure improved State representation, a speedier arbitration process and the establishment of a one-stop agency at the State level. 

A representative of the American Electric Power Service Corpora tion testified that he believed H.R. 6970 came closest to resolving the conflict on power plant siting between environment and electric power supply, but suggested certain modifications to clarify and strengthen the arbitration claims and the judicial review procedure.

Conservation interests were represented by a number of groups. 

The representative of the Sierra Club opposed the enactment of any of the bills under consideration because they did not meet the basic principles of power plant siting which the conservation organization thought important. They suggested that a new bill containing the following provisions should be introduced: (1) basic licensing for facilities rest with the FPC; (2) plans for system extension 20 years in advance must be filed; (3) hearings on plans by Regional Boards; and (4) establishment of new agencies to propose energy policy, administer a program for electric reliability and safety, administer an electric R & D program, and administer a land use program.

The National Wildlife Federation, on the other hand, supported the administration bill, but suggested amendments that would broaden the planning process to include more than just plant sites, and establish a National Energy Agency to coordinate and assist the utilities in new approaches to locating plants. These witnesses raised objection to the arbitration procedure, by stating that the vote of the one environmentalist on the 3-man board would be made in a crisis situation, and that planning and research were superior to arbitration in solving the power plant siting problem. 

Representatives of the National Audubon Society supported the administration bill and stressed the need for a national power policy. 

The Society opposed the arbitration concept contained in H.R. 

6970 as "a tendency to undercut agency responsibility", and that "arbitration will become a sort of ad hoc review board and you would have almost continuous arbitration process on your hands."

The Joint Scientific Staff, Massachusetts and National Audubon 

Societies, testified that environmental assessment institutes funded by environmental protection agencies should be created. These would provide a third-party objective assessment of the environmental impact of a new power plant and should result in better protection of the environment.

The Lake Michigan Federation, representing conservation interests in that area testified that any legislation should: 

1. recognize the failure of existing industry and governmental institutions; 

2. provide for public participation in the planning processes; 

3. provide for thorough review of all environmental questions; 

4. insure adequate protection of publicly-owned resources; and 

5. allow for more participation by environmentally-oriented government agencies.

The representative of the Citizens Committee on Natural Resources, a conservation lobby group, agreed with most, but not all witnesses, that either H.R. 5277 or H.R. 6970 would be preferable to no action.

If H.R. 6970 were to be passed, the witness suggested that it should ensure extensive public involvement in the administrative process in order to reduce subsequent judicial delays. 

On October 8, 1971, the Subcommittee on Communications and 

Power reported a new bill, H.R. 11C66 to the full committee. The report stated that:

The general purpose of the legislation is to achieve a publicly acceptable balance between two competing objectives: (1) to provide an adequate, reliable, and economical bulk power supply to meet reasonable power needs, and (2) in meeting such needs, to protect the environment, to conserve natural resources and to plan the proper use of available lands. (Sec. 401) In order to achieve such balance, the legislation provides for long-range planning by individual power systems and coordination of individual system plans through the medium of regional councils (Sec. 

402, 403); timely disclosure of the coordinated plans (Sec. 404); the establishment of State siting agencies (Sec 405); interstate cooperation (Sec. 406); coordination of Federal proceedings with the proceedings of State siting agencies for the purpose of achieving to the maximum extent possible one-stop proceedings (Sec. 407); establishment of evaluation criteria by Secretary of the Interior to secure greater uniformity in deciding issues relating to reasonable power needs and reasonable environmental protection (Sec. 408); opportunities for bulk power suppliers and environmental groups to secure a special three-man panel for the purpose of deciding unresolved disputes (Sec. 409, 410, 411); authorizing the panels to use formal or informal (arbitration or mediation) procedures in resolving such disputes (Sec. 412); providing a judicial review standard of "arbitrary and capricious' with regard to panel decisions (Sec. 413); and authorizing the use of Federal lands for bulk power facilities where a panel has approved a particular project (Sec. 414). 

No further action was taken on H.R. 11066 during the first session. 

(B) Four Corners Power Plants 

The Southwest and its metropolitan areas, such as Los Angeles. and Phoenix, have undergone phenomenal growth in recent years. 

The region is not, however, very well endowed with the natural resources needed to support a large population. An area within the 

Southwest that has the potential for supplying the energy demand for the region is the Upper Colorado River Basin. The Colorado 

River already supplies substantial amounts of water and hydroelectric power. In recent years additional hydroelectric projects have been rejected largely to preserve the natural beauty of the area. 

As one alternative, steam electric plants using vast coal deposits have been built and others are planned. The conflict between those seeking

to utilize this coal to satisfy the energy demands of the Southwest and those who wish to prevent such use to preserve aesthetic and other values, has become known as the "Four Corners" controversy. 

On May 24-28th, 1971 a series of field hearings on "Problems of Electrical Power Production in the Southwest" were held before the Senate Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs to determine the impact of thermal electric power plant developments on the desert region of the Southwest. 

Testimony was taken at Albuquerque, New Mexico; Las Vegas, Nevada; Salt Lake City, Utah; Durango, Colorado; and Page,

Arizona. Numerous witnesses were heard, including the Governors and other elected officials from the States involved. 

In 1964, 23 public and private utilities representing electric power concerns in California, Nevada, New Mexico, Arizona, Utah and

Colorado formed a consortium to develop coal-fired electric power plants sufficient to meet long-range needs of their respective states. 

The consortium, the Western Energy Supply and Transmission 

Association (WEST), projected a doubling of electric power demands every decade. In response, they planned the construction of six power plants across the Southwest, to be built near the Four Corners area and fueled by low-rank coal found near the surface in New Mexico and Arizona. Most of it will be strip mined and slurried or railed to the plants. Transmission lines will supply markets in Phoenix, Las 

Vegas, Tucson, Albuquerque and Los Angeles. 

The Four Corners Plant at Farmington, New Mexico, in operation since 1963, has been the source of most of the controversy. It uses strip-mined coal from the nearby Navajo Mine. While it is producing a great deal of power in the opinion of some, it also produces a great deal of air pollution. A 1966 photo from a satellite showed the smoke trail from this plant to be the only indication of human activity that could be observed from that height. The Mohave plant at the southern tip of Nevada is also in operation, using coal shipped 273 miles by pipeline from the Black Mesa mine in the Four Corners region. 

This mine will also supply coal to the Navajo Plant under construction in Arizona. The Huntington Plant in Utah and the San Juan Plant in New Mexico are also under construction. Initially, little attention was paid to the installation of air pollution abatement equipment and there is considerable disagreement as to the effectiveness of that which is currently planned for installation.

The unique nature of the Four Corners area is the source of much of the controversy. On the one hand it is a very sparsely populated area in a huge airshed that is not subject to adverse weather conditions such as those which compound air quality problems in the Los Angeles Basin. On the other hand, the region is known for having the purest air in the country. While generally regarded as not having much value, the desert ecosystem is not well understood. Also, most of this land is part of the Hopi or Navajo Reservations, and questions have been raised as to whether the Indians who leased the coal were adequately represented when the lease terms were drawn. 

The main benefits to be derived from the projects would be the supplying of greatly increasing demand for electric power; economic stimulus to the region; and economic benefits to the Indians in the form of royalties and employment. Principal disadvantages would be

air pollution, possible land destruction by strip mining, and pollution problems resulting from the use of water for the coal slurry pipeline and for cooling. Additionally, the Black Mesa area is of religious significance to the Indians. As a result, the dispute is between those who perceive economic benefit to their area (power companies and some elected officials) and those who are concerned by the adverse environmental effects of the plants (environmentalists and other elected officials).

The hearings disclosed the following information on the opposing views of those concerned:

Air Pollution.-Critics have cited the volume of stack emissions from the existing and proposed plants, drawing parallels with the total emissions of cities such as New York and Los Angeles. Opponents also noted that California regulations would not permit similar coal-fired plants in the Los Angeles Basin, maintaining that Los Angeles, in effect, was exporting its air polltuion. Proponents of the project, on the other hand, maintain that both stack emission restrictions and ambient air quality standards are included in the Clean Air Act. They have also announced the proposed installation of several pollution control facilities and that an area-wide monitoring program to determine the nature and extent of atmospheric pollution from the plants would be initiated.

Water Pollution.-Opponents of the project contended that leaks had occurred in the coal slurry pipeline, polluting nearby drainages, and in the future slurry water would be disposed of by running it into the nearby rivers. Another complaint was that the removal of water from the Colorado River would result in an increase in salinity of the remaining water. This was seen as a problem in fulfilling treaty requirements for delivery of usable Colorado River water to Mexico. 

Company officials acknowledged that pipeline leaks or discharges had occurred but that steps were now being taken to prevent their recurrence.

Strip Mining.-Critics maintained that the strip mining techniques used to procure coal for the Farmington plant produced detrimental effects comparable to those of strip mining in Appalachia. Proponents countered that the comparsion is unjustified. They pointed out that rainfall in Farmington is about 5 inches annually, compared to nearly 10 times that amount for Appalachia; whereas the Farmington area is sparsely vegetated, Appalachia is heavily forested; and, finally, the topography of the Southwest consists of table-like mesas and interspersed lowlands, while Appalachia consists of steeply sloping hills. 

Mercury Contamination. This problem was raised with regard to both air pollution and strip mining. Contradictory figures were presented by State and private witnesses. 

Indian Affairs.-Two problems associated with Indian control over their land holdings were disclosed at the hearings. The first involved the contracts signed by the Indians with the various companies for use of tribal lands. The primary issue involved how the contract terms compare with other non-Indian contracts for similar services. The second problem involved a preservation-versus-development conflict within the tribes. "Traditional" segments of the tribes oppose the mining operations and other development activity. "Progressives" however, promoted the power plants, viewing them as a source of economic development which could aid the welfare of the tribe.

On May 7, 1971, Secretary of Interior Morton announced initiation of a study of the power plant developments, and during the week of the hearings announced a moratorium on construction not yet begun. This affected only the Kaiparowits Plant in Utah, two of the six plants already being completed, and three others are under construction. The 

Kaiparowits Plant is by far the largest of the six plants. 

On November 10, 1971 an additional day of hearing was held in

Washington to obtain administration testimony. Witnesses represented the Department of the Interior, the Federal Power Commission, and the Environmental Protection Agency. The effects on Indian peoples and on public lands were pointed out as being among the concerns of the Committee.

Under Secretary Pecora of the Department of the Interior discussed the Federal Interagency Task Force which is carrying out the South west Energy Study announced by Secretary Morton on May 7. Dr. 

Pecora stated that his Department was concerned about the parks in the region and that drafts of environmental impact statements for the Navajo and Huntington plants had been prepared. He discussed the economic benefits of the plants to the Indians, and stated that he endorsed economic development of the region, but not at the expense of the quality of the environment. Among issues brought up during questioning were: effect on the water table of water used for the coal slurry pipelines; possibility of slurry water reentering the Colorado; revegetation after strip mining; environmental safeguard provisions in contracts; the possibility of independent studies; and, public involvement. In response to the question whether the Indians had been fairly represented, he replied that the Department must deal with their elected bodies on the assumption that they represent the Indians in the same manner as the Congress represents the Nation as a whole. 

The various standards of performance for air pollution abatement equipment, the ways of expressing them, and the equipment at the various plants both existent and proposed, were considered. The 

Mojave plant in Nevada is in a county whose standards are more rigorous than expected Federal standards. Senator Montoya compared the effluent of the plants to that of Los Angeles and expressed strong dissatisfaction with the quality of abatement equipment at the plants. 

Senator Allott of Colorado reiterated his State's dissatisfaction in being subject to the ill effects of the plants without having the benefits of a plant located in the State, or of using the power generated by them. 

Chairman Nassikas of the Federal Power Commission spoke about energy issues on the whole, and indicated that the development of these plants was accelerated by the rejection of hydro-power projects on the Colorado River. He expressed the view that there would be environmental impacts on the area in the vicinity of the plants, and that the power generated would be used elsewhere, but that this would be the case for any project. He also asserted that failure to fully develop these plants could seriously affect power supply in the area, and that the time involved in completing the studies being conducted by the Department of Interior would not be a serious delay. 

Assistant Administrator Greenfield of the Environmental Protection Agency limited his presentation to air pollution aspects and discussed EPA's role and the mechanisms for air quality enforcement. 

EPA does not now have monitoring equipment in the vicinity. Dr. 

Greenfield said that standards would be changed with new research

and that the issue of whether standards should apply retroactively has not been resolved.

The Department of Interior noted that many of their contracts had retroactive clauses.

(A) Oil Shale Leasing VIII. Other Energy Issues On Nov. 15 the Senate Interior and Insular Affairs Committee conducted hearings on S. 2510 and on plans by the Department of 

Interior to lease Federal oil shale lands in the States of Colorado, Utah, and Wyoming. S. 2510 would create a development corporation similar to that proposed for coal gasification in S. 1846. (See p.76.) It was designed to speed up the development of the oil shale and other energy resources that have not yet reached a commercial stage, but could be important in helping meet the anticipated deficit in energy supplies. The Department's proposed leasing program for the 

Federal oil shale lands was a part of the action program contained in the President's energy message offered to insure that the Nation had an ample supply of clean fuels to meet the demands of an expanding economy.

The shale deposits in the States of Colorado, Utah and Wyoming contain vast quantities of shale oil that can be refined into products suitable for the same uses as petroleum. Deposits in these three States contain over 1.8 trillion barrels of oil that can be classed as "proved reserves" -more than 60 times our proved reserves of crude oil in the 48 States. The Federal government's interest in these deposits is great since the Federally owned share contains about 80% of all the shale oil, and most of the very thick and very rich deposits are on Federal lands. The efficient management of these publicly owned resources dictates that they be used wisely to assure the nation an adequate, dependable, and diversely based energy supply at the lowest cost consistent with other national goals, such as protection of environmental quality.

A number of methods to produce a satisfactory liquid fuel from oil shale deposits have been known for many years, but complex economic, technical, and legal problems have stood in the way of their development. In Dec. 1968, the Department of the Interior offered three oil shale leases in Colorado, but no acceptable bids were made. As a result, the Department undertook to study the problem further in an attempt to get an oil shale industry started. In June, during the hearings on the President's energy message, a new leasing program was announced. The hearings on Nov. 15 were called to determine what response to the program had been received from industry, and whether S. 2510 would be needed to complement or act as a substitute for the Department's program for encouraging development of an oil shale industry. 

The Departmental witness described the activities that occurred. after the 1968 lease offering. These consisted largely of increasd cooperation with the States in designing a program that would minimize adverse environmental effects. Each of the States submitted environmental reports to the Department early in 1971, and on the basis of this and other information, the Department decided to pro- ((99)

ceed with its leasing plan. Industry was asked to nominate tracts on on which they might bid and permission to drill on these tracts was granted to provide potential bidders with the geologic information needed to decide on the size of the bonus bid. At the time of the hearings, the Department had 22 application for drilling at several locations; nineteen were in Colorado and 3 in Utah. About $2 million was expected to be expended for core drilling by applicants. 

Although significant progress has been made in clearing many unpatented claims on the oil shale lands, title to much of it is still clouded. Two significant decisions which may help to increase industry interest in this resource have been made by the Department. Tracts that have a clouded title, but which have been nominated for lease, will be offered, and any funds collected in this connection will be placed in escrow until title problems have been resolved. This will permit the Department to offer a wider choice of leases. In addition, the Soliciter has ruled that the Mineral Leasing Act is to be interpreted as meaning that a corporation or person may hold only one lease at a time, but may bid on other leases when the first one is relinquished. The previous interpretation had been that one lease was all that could ever be held by a person or corporation. This should also increase the interest in bidding on new leases. 

The administration witness at the Nov. 15 hearings opposed 

S. 2510 on the grounds that it would short-circuit the efforts of private enterprise which was already moving forward in the development of oil shale; it would result in a fragmentation of executive department responsibility in energy matters and thus would be incompatible with the President's reorganization plans; it ran counter to the policies of the recently enacted Mining and Minerals Policy Act; and, it had provisions with respect to patents that were inconsistent with administration policy.

The 1968 leasing program was believed by the Department to have failed to elicit satisfactory bids because of the very short time that had been allowed for industry to study and understand the lease offer and to drill the deposits to obtain geologic information. 

In respone to questions, the Departmental witness said that an oil shale plant capable of producing 100,000 barrels per day would require capital costs of about $425 million, and that it was estimated that shale oil could be produced for about $3.75 per barrel. In veiw of this, no satisfactory explanation was given as to why industry had not gone forward in oil shale development on privately-held lands. The reason given as to why industry would submit reasonable bids for the leases when the Department's own calculations showed that on the first plant the owner could expect to lose 40 cents per barrel on a capital investment of $425 million was that oil prices can be expected to rise. 

Witnesses from the Tosco company and from Atlantic Richfield, which is now a part-owner in the development, described their efforts in developing a technology different from that which had been studied by others interested in oil shale development. They concurred with the reasons given by the Departmental witness as to why the 1968 lease offering had failed, and thought that the present one would be successful. In addition to giving industry time to discover the nature of the resources being offered, the energy supply situation and the economic environment will have changed significantly between

1968 and 1972 when the first new lease will be offered. Tosco expects that there will be a commercial plant in operation by 1975 or 1976, since the required technology is now fully at hand. As a result, it concluded there is no need for a bill such as S. 2510 to bring the government into the development. 

The witness from Cameron Engineers, a firm which has been heavily involved in the oil shale resource and its development, said that the new leasing program had a resonable chance of success, and that his firm had contracts for 6 informational drilling programs involving 12 companies. As a result, he felt that there was no need for the type of legislation contained in S. 2510 and, that instead, any legislation affecting oil shale should be directed toward amending those provisions of the Mineral Leasing Act-such as acreage limitationsthat are seriously reducing interest in oil shale development. In view of the country's need for developing secure supplies of liquid fuels, the witness suggested that the Government give some assistance to the pioneering plants which will be making very large, high-risk investments. While he favored any plan that would give industry an incentive to go ahead and make the estimated one billion dollar investment needed to get the industry started, one method that was suggested involved the government buying the products from the first plants at a fixed price. He cited as precedents for this type of government assistance the help that the railroads received in their early days, and the way that industry was encouraged to build uranium stockpiles for the Nation. 

(B) Amendments to The Mining and Minerals Policy Act of 1970 

Demand for minerals and fuels is rapidly increasing. At the same time, it is becoming necessary to utilize resources which are more expensive to extract. Moreover, interest has developed in the environmental problems associated with mineral extraction, and the health and safety of mine workers. These activities indicate a need for technological advancements and, in turn, for research and development and the training of adequate personnel. An example of the latter problem is the decreased number of students entering and graduating in mining research and engineering programs; enrollment in these programs has dropped significantly in recent years and several schools have had to close departments dealing with these subjects. 

The Mining and Minerals Policy Act of 1970 declared a Federal intent to encourage strong domestic mining, minerals and metallurgical industries; the sound development of mineral resources; and research into the environmental aspects mineral development to further the goals by promoting improved mining technology. S. 635 was introduced to achieve this objective through a program of matching grants to States and the establishment of research and training institutions for mineral resources to be administered by the Secretary of the Interior. It called for total authorizations of $12.1 million for 

1972 increasing to $23.75 million by 1976. Guidelines for selecting institutions within the States to receive grants were detailed, and provisions for cooperation among States were made. Grants to each 

State would be in the amount of $100,000 for 1972, increasing to $250,000 for 1975, and each year thereafter. It would authorize appropriations of $5 million per year distributed on a 75-25 basis for the purchase of equipment, and $1 million per year for printing and

publishing. An additional $1 million per year, increasing to $5 million per year by the fifth year, would be allocated for "special mineral resource research projects which would not otherwise be undertaken." 

Hearings on S. 635 were held before the Subcommittee on Minerals, Materials, and Fuels of the Senate Committee on Interior and Insular 

Affairs on April 28, 1971. Witnesses for the Administration did not testify; they requested a postponement until their position on the bill could be detailed. Even without an Administration spokesman, there was a full slate of witnesses; approximately one-quarter represented industry and three-quarters were from universities. Testimony citing the need for this measure drew a parallel between it and the support the Federal Government has given agricultural research over the past century. Several instances were also cited of support foreign countries have given their mining and minerals industries. 

S. 635 passed the Senate on July 19, 1971. 

On November 11, 1971, the Subcommittee on Mines and Mining of the Interior and Insular Affairs Committee of the House took up consideration of identical bills, H.R. 6788 and H.R. 10950, to establish mineral resources research centers, and S. 635. Congressman 

Aspinall noted that the situation was complicated by passage the previous week of H.R. 7248, the Higher Education Act of 1971, Title 

XI of which is intended to establish mineral resource research centers. 

Both H.R. 6788 and H.R. 10950 include provisions for: (1) a program of matching grants of $500,000 to each of the States for establishing institutes; (2) $5,000,000 per year for five years for grants for special projects to the institutes; and (3) $10,000,000, increasing to $18,000,- 

000 per year, for five years for grants to educational, private, and governmental institutions for carrying out appropriate research. 

The program would be administered by the Secretary of the Interior S. 635 differs somewhat in amounts of appropriations and designated uses, in not terminating after five years, and in that it takes the form of an amendment to the Mining and Mineral Policy Act of 1970. 

Title XI of the higher Education Act of 1971 has the fundamental differences of being administered by the Commissioner of Education rather than the Secretary of the Interior and in establishing only 10 institutions across the country, rather than one in each State. 

Mr. Dole stated his Department's support for either H.R. 6788 or 

H.R. 10905. He referred to the rapid depletion of mineral resource supplies; to increased concern for the quality of the environment; to the declined advancement of mineral technology in this country; to a decrease in the number of graduates in mineral engineering; and to the analagous support of agricultural education.
