He suggested amendments which would limit grants to a certain number of institutions to be chosen by the Secretary of the Interior, and which would delete the provisions for research grants. 

The rationale for limiting the number of institutions was that large budget implications were involved; a crash program was not necessarily needed for all 50 states; and it would be better not to diffuse the funds and efforts. Concern was raised about the provisions of 

Title XI of H.R. 7248 and that its recommendation that having only one school per region would create political problems. 

The reasons for asking that the provisions for research grants be deleted were that the Department already had such authority and that it would be redundant.

In regard to the other bills, Mr. Dole stated that he preferred those of this Committee, modified by the suggested amendments. In regard to the Higher Education Act, he said that his Department was responsible for this program area, and that the program should be within the Department and under the surveillance of the Interior Committee. 

A basic rationale behind these various bills is that by supporting minerals engineering and research, more students will be attracted. 

Dr. Osborn, Director of the Bureau of Mines, stated that mineral engineering had been largely bypassed by the research funding which has taken place since World War II, and this had put University departments in a poor competitive position with other technical fields. 

He pointed out, however, that there also were no large enrollments in undergraduate courses in mineral resources, such as in other fields and this tended to decrease department strength; agriculture is similarly handicapped, but has been aided greatly by Federal programs. 

The mineral resource research institute programs proposed by these bills are to some extent modeled after the agricultural education programs.

It was also pointed out that an increased number of graduating mineral engineers was not the only reason for seeking to strengthen university departments, This bill would also provide centers of technical expertise and facilities for carrying out needed research. 

The decrease in numbers of minerals engineering students was not the result of decreased demand. Some present graduation levels were insufficient even to replace present levels. It was very difficult to get qualified people to carry out the Coal Mine Health and Safety program and people from other fields were having to be trained specially for the positions that needed to be filled. 

(c) Utility Consumers' Counsel and Information Act 

The regulation of utilities and the cost of utility services to consumers has received considerable congressional attention over the years. Senate Bill 607 is designed to protect consumers' interests in dealing with the utilities. Title I calls for the creation of an Office of 

Utility Consumers' Counsel. The Counsel, who would be appointed for a five year term, would represent consumer interests at investigations and non-criminal court cases, provide information useful for consumer protection, and make grants to State and local governments and to bodies responsible for utility regulation for consumer protection programs. Title II of the bill requires certain information to be made public by utility companies. 

On October 28 and 29, 1971, the Subcommittee on Intergovernmental Relations of the Committee on Government Operations of the Senate held hearings on S. 607. Testimony in favor of the bill was heard from three witnesses. The first represented Colorado Project 

Common Cause, which is financed largely by the public interest group, Common Cause. He criticized strongly utility treatment of 

Colorado consumers, referring to regulation of utilities in Colorado as scandalous. Colorado has a combined gas and electric utility which was said to wield a great deal of influence on Colorado affairs; many of its officers also sat on various government boards, banks, etc. The 

Colorado Public Utilities Commission was said to be understaffed, and also to be connected with the industry in a variety of ways. 71-900-72-8

The second witness was an attorney and former chairman of the Arlington Public Utilities Commission.20 He indicated especially strong support for that part of the bill which provides for assistance in representing consumer interests before State regulatory agencies. This is because of the high cost of legal assistance to consumer groups and the large competent legal staffs usually available to utility companies. 

The witness also suggested that the bill be amended to insure its applicability to appearances before the Price Board under the Wage and Price Control Program. 

The third witness represented the Midland Community Action Agency of South Carolina. The web of influence of utility companies was again pointed out, this time involving members of the State legislature. The question of the proper level for administering a program such as that proposed by S. 607 was discussed by both the second and third witnesses. The State level was felt by these witnesses to be inadequate because in States where the utilities have especially strong influence, it was felt that such programs could never get underway. Moreover, since the utilities are able to turn to resources available through national affiliations, State programs might still leave a situation in which the consumer remained at a severe disadvantage. 

(D) Minerals Resource Briefing 

The Subcommittee on Mines and Mining of the Committee on 

Interior and Insular Affairs of the House of Representatives held hearings on May 17, 1971. Hollis Dole, Assistant Secretary for Mineral

Resources of the Department of the Interior assessed the major mineral resource issues facing the country. A similar briefing had been held two years before.

The continuing fundamental problem is that domestic mineral raw material production is not increasing as rapidly as domestic demand. Current production of $37.6 billion accounts for slightly less than 78% of domestic demand in contrast to 91% in 1950. Projections to the year 2000 predict that only 38% would be produced domestically. Metals are the most serious problem, with domestic production meeting 58% of demand presently, but declining to 20% in the year 2000. Fuel production is presently 85%, but in 2000 is expected to be only 48% of demand. Domestic mines currently produce 

97% of demand for non-metals and are projected to decline to 60% by the year 2000.

A challenge is posed by these projected material shortages, by the decreasing grades of domestic ores, by the increasing competition for high grade foreign deposits, and by conflicts between the mining industry and environmental concerns. Secretary Dole proposed that the answer to this challenge is a "massive revitalization and rededication of mineral science and technology." It will be necessary to re-assert the need for techological advancement, and for new manpower in the minerals industries. For example, the number of mining schools in the United States has dropped from 37 to 15 in a little over ten years. The Mining and Mineral Policy Act of 1970 should be an important step toward meeting these ends. 

The need for a national energy policy to help solve the problems of fuels and shortages was emphasized. The energy gap was said not 

20 Arlington County along with another Virginia county have often taken on the role of advocate for the citizenry of the entire state before utility regulatory bodies.

to result from immediate shortages of fuels, but from the need for technological advances and a coherent comprehensive long-range energy policy. Another factor that would be helpful would be the creation of a Department of Natural Resources. 

Coal gasification, offshore leasing, the Trans-Alaska Pipeline, coal mine health and safety, the helium program, mined land reclamation, recycling of sold wastes, reorganization of the executive branch, and preemption of mining lands by wilderness area designations were also discussed. These topics were also the subject of several legislative proposals: H.R. 6959 and S. 1341 in regard to a proposed Department of Natural Resources; H.R. 7991 and S. 1853, in regard to offshore leasing off Santa Barbara; and H.R. 5689, and S. 993, to mined land reclamation.

Major programs that were discussed during questioning were the helium program and the Coal Mine Health and Safety Act. Due to changed conditions, the helium stockpile is in excess of desired levels, but there are legal difficulties being encountered in terminating contracts. The failure of the Department to carry out the Coal Mine 

Health and Safety in the way intended by the legislation was said to be due to the short time span allowed by the legislation, and to the difficulty of obtaining manpower.

SYNOPSIS OF SELECTED MAJOR REPORTS ON ENERGY AND FUELS 

Controversy of national and international importance concerning the supply of fuels and energy has generated a large number of studies and reports. This section attempts to synthesize the pertinent conclusions and recommendations contained in those studies of major importance which were issued in 1971. Such an approach was thought to be more conducive to an understanding of the policy questions involved than a detailed summary and analysis of each, due to their large number, as well as to similarities among many of their findings. 

A selected list of the energy-related reports issued in 1971 are listed in Appendix E, organized under broad subject area headings. 

(A) Energy Policy Reports 

The United States now produces and consumes greater quantities of energy than ever before. With less than six percent of the world's population, the United States now uses about one-third of all world energy. Americans also consume nearly eight times more energy than the world average. The basis of the U.S. energy economy is a triad of petroleum, natural gas and coal. This triad is built on supplies largely from indigenous sources, with imports accounting for only seven percent of total energy supplies in the U.S. 

The past decade has seen a rapid growth in energy demand. Total domestic energy demand in the United States grew at a rate of five percent per year between 1965 and 1970. This represents a sharp increase from past trends. The rapid increase in demand for energy has exerted sharp pressures on many economic institutions. Moreover, the development of new national goals, as well as emerging technological problems, has severely limited the ability of present-day policies to resolve present and future energy problems. 

The difficulties developing from increased demand, changing institutions and technological problems were the central focus of a report. prepared for the National Energy Forum by Arthur D. Little, Inc.

This report, Energy Policy Issues for the United States During the Seventies, sought to provide background to the major energy issues facing the public and private energy sectors in the decade ahead. 

The study focused on the major energy issues that were developing from environmental, as well as other, sources:

A new dimension has been added to this evolving pattern of energy demand and supply by growing public concern over environmental quality 

As a result the energy industries are at the very heart of the national controversy about the environment and energy planning must now consider ecological_concerns as well as resource adequacy, import dependency, and economics. Faced with conflicting pressures and choices, the private sector of the energy industries must turn for policy guidance to government if it is to operate effectively. Here it finds the absence of stable, coordinated Federal policies on energy, together with conflicting jurisdiction and goals among the Federal, state and local levels. 

The Advisory Committee on Energy to the Secretary of the Interior addressed the reasons for a lack of policy formulation at the (106)

Federal level in their report, U.S. Energy-A General Review. The 

Committee observed that:

Historically, the Federal Government has not considered coordinated national energy policies necessary because the United States has enjoyed a fortunate position in energy. Until recently, energy supplies have been abundant and cheap, with declining real prices measured in dollars of constant purchasing power. Interfuel competition modified only by state conservation regulations to prevent waste and to protect the interests of small operators served consumers well for decades as the nation shifted from reliance primarily on coal to oil and gas.

The Advisory Committee summarized the problems, position, outlook and recommendations for each source and for the total energy economy. Their outlook for the provision of energy resources could best be described as one of cautious optimism: (1) The U.S. has sufficient potential energy resources from most sources to rely on continuing expansion of production as required to meet growing needs and to remain largely self-sufficient in energy provided it chooses to do so and follows policies designed to achieve that objective. (2) To meet the needs of the nation for progress toward solution of economic, social, and environmental problems, it will be necessary to provide ample supplies of energy from conventional fuels and new sources of energy when needed by consumers. ... It appears that we will need more fossil fuels plus all the energy that can be expected economically from new sources in order to meet total needs for energy in the next 10-15 years. Unfortunately, there are no easy panaceas for securing most supplies of energy in desirable forms at low cost without unwanted side effects. (3) Because of past mistakes in governmental energy policies and the long lead time required for major expansion in capacity, the U.S. cannot escape experiencing some shortages of energy, as is now the case in the shortages in supplies of natural gas.

The outlook for energy resources was also the subject of a report by the National Petroleum Council (NPC) entitled: U.S. Energy Outlook, 

An Initial Appraisal, 1971–1985. Basing its analysis on several important assumptions, the report concluded that U.S. energy consumption could be expected to grow at an average rate of 4.2 percent per year during the 1971-1985 period. The recommendations for individual fuels for this relatively short time frame, pointed out the possibility of future difficulties:

Petroleum Liquids In order to meet growing demands for petroleum liquids, imports would have to increase more than fourfold by 1985, reaching a rate of 14.8 million B/D in that year. Assuming the availability of foreign supply, oil imports would then account for 57 percent of total petroleum supplies and would represent 25 percent of total energy consumption. Most of the imports would have to originate in the Eastern Hemisphere because of the limited potential for increased imports from Western Hemisphere sources. 

Natural gas.

Taking all of these sources into account, 1985 supplies would total only 21.49 TCF, or Ĭ.25 TCF less than 1970 supplies of 22.74 TCF. Dependency on imports would rise from 4 percent of gas supplies in 1970 to more than 28 percent in 1985, assuming the availability of foreign supply. The shortfall in energy supply between potential gas demand and available gas supplies would have to be made up from increased supplies of other fuels. 

Coal... Coal reserves were judged ample and could support a faster growth rate in production. Potential constraints, however, were seen as being the availability of manpower and transportation facilities, health and safety regulations, and the need to develop a commercially proven technology for control of sulfur dioxide emissions.

Nuclear No shortage of domestic fuels was foreseen, assuming prices for 

U3Og up to $10 per pound.

Both the Advisory Committee and the NPC report expressed concern respecting the availability of investment capital to meet increased energy needs. The Advisory Committee, for example, observed that a

number of developments had worked to discourage the expansion of new investments to develop new energy supplies:

These developments include (1) Federal regulation of the price of natural gas at artificially low levels which caused demand to exceed supply; (2) added taxes on petroleum imposed by the Tax Reform Act of 1969; (3) the effect of the Federal 

Mine Health and Safety Act on the cost and availability of coal; (4) uncertainty as to import policies dealing with energy which affect coal as well as oil and gas; (5) delay in the leasing of Federal acreage on the Continental Shelf; (6) indecision as to Federal policy with respect to leasing of shale lands for extraction of oil and gas; (7) attacks on price increases for energy even though the increases still left real prices of fuels lower than a decade ago; (8) uncertainty as to the effect of changing environmental standards on the long-run value of prompt investments designed to improve the quality of air and water; (9) the delay and prospect of further delay in bringing oil from the North Slope into the U.S. economy; and (10) construction delays and licensing problems of nuclear plants and hydroelectric plants. 

For the period 1971-1985, the NPC observed that the:

Annual new investment required to finance development of natural resources and construction of new facilities would greatly exceed the levels of recent years. 

Funds provided from operations of energy industries at present price levels would fall far short of meeting these capital requirements. Environmental regulations affecting the supply, transportation and consumption of all fuels would further increase investment costs. All these things indicate increasing energy costs. 

The National Petroleum Council report also foresaw difficulties in providing the physical facilities needed to meet future energy requirements. Because energy requirements were expected to double by 1985, significant additions to facilities will be required which may not be easily forthcoming given existing political, social and economic conditions.

In petroleum, the importation of an incremental 10-11 million B/D of overseas crude oil and products above the 1970 level would require more than 

350 tankers, each of 250,000 deadweight tonnage (DWT). No U.S. ports are presently equipped to receive such tankers, so new terminals would have to be developed in coastal areas. Similarly, the increase in refined products requirements would necessitate net additions of about 10 million B/D to domestic refining capacity over the 15-year period. This would involve construction at about 2.5 times the rate of the past decade. In gas, the importation of 4 TCF of LNG annually by 1985 would require the building of 120 tankers each having a maximum capacity equivalent of approximately 790,000 barrels. In addition, such operations would require the building of liquefaction plants at the loading terminals and the building of unloading terminals, regasification plants and storage and transportation facilities at points of delivery. In coal, the doubling of mine output would involve the development of Western coal reserves with associated transportation to markets as well as expanded development of underground mines in the East and Midwest. 

In nuclear power, the pace of construction of new plants would have to risevery sharply from recent levels, reaching a capability of bringing thirty 1,000-megawatt plants on line each year from 1980 through 1985. 

The report by Arthur D. Little, Inc. raised two other problems which will face energy industries in the next decade; the issues surrounding environmental quality and national security. The report noted that: energy planning and policy making have been virtually overwhelmed by the combination of basic structural uncertainties when overlaid by an endless series of case-by-case environmental issues which are thrown at industry and government without overall coordination. If all environ-mental problems were simple-that is, if they could be corrected by a reasonable mixture of money and diligent effort-such a case-by-case approach would be highly productive. However, most of the environmental issues are complex.

This is not an argument for inaction or delay. It is an argument for understanding of the basic implications of individual moves to make sure that they are consistent with public policy, and seek an early reconciliation of conflicting alternatives where these appear.

A group of problems highlighted in each report related to national security. These problems can impinge on energy policies in two areas: 

First is the gradual declassification of confidential information about nuclear fission and fusion pointing toward commercial utilization for nuclear power purposes; the other concerns the oil import quota. The basis for existing oil import policies has been the need: -to guarantee supplies sufficient to meet the need of U.S. military forces and defense industries; -for sufficient supply of crude oil and its derivatives to meet essential civilian demands and sustain economic growth; and -to foster exploration and development so as to insure against a depletion of reserves to an extent which would jeopardize the capability of the petroleum industry to meet future demands without undue reliance on foreign sources of questionable reliability.

The increasing reliance on foreign energy sources (especially petroleum) forecasted by the National Petroleum Council indicates the need for a re-assessment of existing criteria for the oil import quota program. Figure 1 shows why a re-assessment of present policies will be necessary. The large projected increase in imported petroleum could endanger reliability of supplies and the competitive position of the domestic petroleum industry.

FIGURE 1. U.S. Petroleum Required Supply [Million Barrels per Day]

Source: National Petroleum Council, U.S. Energy Outlook, p. 27.

All three reports indicated the need for a re-examination of present energy policies at the Federal level. The National Petroleum Council noted, for example, that:

Continuation of present government policies, particularly in respect to leasing of federal lands, environmental controls, health and safety, tax rates, research funding, natural gas price regulation, and import policies, clearly will result in a sharp rise in national dependence on imported energy sources, particularly petroleum liquids. This will require careful assessment, in respect to both national security aspects and the impact on the U.S. balance of payments. Furthermore, the United States cannot expect indefinitely to be able to increase imports of foreign oil. Towards the end of the century, foreign oil supplies may prove insufficient to meet all potential demands. 

Continuation of present government policies will also result in available gas supplies being equal to only about one-half of market requirements in 1985. 

The report of the Advisory Committee was more detailed about the basic requirements for new energy policies: 

1. The interrelation of all forms of energy means that effective U.S. energy policies must be formulated broadly and on a long-run basis in order that consumers and investors may make intelligent decisions about the use and production of various competing fuels. 

2. Because the complexity of energy problems in terms of technology, economics, and environment requires a coordinated, analytical examination on a continuing basis, primary governmental responsibility for analysis of energy policies should be placed in the hands of one entity knowledgeable about all energy problems, such as the proposed Department of Natural Resources. 

3. Policy with respect to reliance on domestic sources of energy versus dependence on imports should be defined clearly for the long run to promote increased development of supplies of domestic oil, gas, coal, and uranium, as well as development of alternate fuels from coal, shale, and tar sands so that consumers may have sufficient options among secure domestic supplies. 

4. Governmental energy research efforts and support of private research should be enlarged for those crucial areas of energy development and use that involve greater benefits socially than to individual companies. Research programs promoting more efficient use of energy deserve special attention by the government. 

The Arthur D. Little report posed a series of questions to indicate the major energy policy issues of the seventies. While these questions do not embody all of the issues to be addressed, they do give a good insight into complex questions which will have to addressed as the reexamination of present policies is made. They included: (1) Does government have an obligation to influence overall energy consumption? (2) To what extent should government try to influence the choice of energy form in the interests of some national goal, such as environmental quality or national security? (3) How should government's role in environmental quality interact with its role in energy supply? (4) What level of domestic energy self-sufficiency should the U.S. seek to achieve and at what cost? (5) How does government policy address the problem of contingency planning? (6) How far should government go in stimulating energy supply? (7) Who bears the costs of national security and environmental quality? (8) How can the conflicting goals of tax equity and incentives for clean domestic energy be reconciled? (9) What should the government R&D role be? (10) Is the historic role of government in preserving competition still adequate in the face of changing industry structure?

(11) How can regulatory conflicts among Federal, state and local governments be resolved? 

(B) Energy Research and Development Reports 

In October, 1971, a report on energy research and development was issued by the National Science Foundation. The report, Energy Research Needs, had been prepared by Resources for the Future and the 

Massachusetts Institute of Technology's Environmental Laboratory. 

The report identified a number of individual questions on which research was needed in order to gain an improved understanding of the longterm energy situation, and to devise policies to deal with these problems. The subjects covered in the report included: 

-Factors Influencing U.S. Energy Consumption -Future of Mineral Fuel Reserves -Gaseous and Liquid Fuels From Unconventional Sources -Electric Energy From Fossil and Nuclear Fuels -Automotive Power Plant Technology -Residential Energy Utilization Technology -Environmental Effects of Energy Production, Conversion and 

Use 

-Public Policies Affecting the Energy Industries 

Specific detailed research suggestions were provided in the report together, with several general questions that provided a cohesive framework to the topics addressed and an insight into the present and future state of energy R&D: (1) How much flexibility does the United States economy possess in respect to the future growth in total energy consumption? (2) How much flexibility does the United States possess in respect to the composition of total energy consumption in terms of energy forms (i.e., gaseous, liquid and electric energy)? (3) How much flexibility does the United States possess in respect to the particular mineral fuels from which the energy forms are derived? (4) To what extent, and at what cost, are the nation's mineral fuel reserves expandable? (5) What are the comparative costs of obtaining greater quantities of fuels from natural sources, and from unconventional sources, such as coal and shale through new technology? (6) To what extent will research and development in respect to energy production and use result in greater flexibility in meeting future energy needs and enlarge the scope of trade-offs at all stages of the energy flow process from mineral exploration through energy conversion and ultimate use?

One area in vital need of research attention, according to the report, was the manner in which existing public policies affect the efficiency with which the energy industries perform, and the potentialities for improved efficiency through changes in public policy. 

As the report noted:

This will need to be approached by way of studies of individual energy industries, but the ultimate objective should be to pull the total picture together, to the extent available information permits, for the energy industries across the board. The significance of the evidence provided would be a demonstration of the potentialities for lowering costs by changes in public policies and in industrial organization and practice; or in indicating the extent to which the overriding noneconomic considerations of importance to society will result in increasing costs.

Lowering costs is just one type of incentive to private energy industries which, to industry, form another area in need of additional research. The incentive structure in the energy industries is affected by public policies in a variety of ways, yet the need for additional research is present. The most important area of research in this area is the cumulative impact of governmental policies:

The widest range of elements affecting incentives is to be found in the policies affecting crude oil; but the exact cumulative consequences are difficult to unravel. 

One long standing federal policy that has its origins in a desire to provide an incentive to the discovery of new reserves is the special tax treatment of minerals income.

Restrictions on the importation of oil into the United States are also meant to provide incentives to the domestic industry. It must be repeated that policies bearing on the energy industries have a cumulative effect and that in any attempt to discuss them in terms of specific areas of public concern and action, a constant reminder of this circumstance is required. 

This is perhaps more the case for oil than for any other mineral fuel. Such positive effect as special tax treatment and import restrictions may have on the discovery of new reserves may be neutralized, to a degree difficult to ascertain; by state regulatory systems. Restrictions on output and inducements to over-investment in reservoir development as a consequence of state conservation regulation seem likely to undermine the incentives for exploration. 

The overall policies with regard to energy R&D are also in need of close examination. As the report noted, "even a cursory look at past government programs in support of energy R&D reveals the episodic and disjointed way in which government activity in this area has developed." The factor missing in past policies has been an overall framework within which R&D choices could be made. Added to this deficiency is the appropriation of large funds to nuclear R&D (up to 80 percent of total R&D appropriations) while money spent on other energy technologies has been quite small and erratic in nature. 

The report also identified a large number of areas in energy R&D which could be the subject of policy studies; a partial list includes: -Investigations into the adequacy of the total energy R&D effort. -Research on the degree to which flexibility and diversity in energy options is desirable. -Analysis of the role of energy R&D in achieving a balance in future energy patterns. -The need for establishment of criteria for deciding when government-financed R&D is justified. -Investigations into the factors favoring government intervention in energy R&D financing on the basis of social versus private cost-benefit considerations. -Research into the interrelationships between energy R&D and other energy policies. -Analysis of the ways in which R&D should be managed to increase its productivity.

Recently, criticism has been voiced over the research and development activities of the electric utility industry. A report prepared by the R&D Goals Task Force of the Electric Research Council entitled Electric Utilities Industry Research and Development Goals Through the Year 2000 acknowledged some shortage of funds for electricity R&D. "One reason not enough money is being spent on R&D today," the report noted, "is that it is particularly vulnerable to budget trimming in both the government sector and the utility sector."

In the past, utilities have relied strongly on manufacturers to undertaken research and development. and the costs have been passed along in the ultimate price of the product. This report observed that while this procedure had led to advances, "it is not an altogether satisfactory arrangement." For example; it does not provide for comprehensive system oriented R&D. Manufacturers generally do not invest tremendous sums of money in projects that will not produce a marketable product and return of their R&D investment for many years. They try to remain competitive price-wise not only against other domestic manufacturers, but foreign manufacturers as well. Much important R&D does not necessarily provide opportunity for mass production and profit. Thus the major efforts of manufacturers tend toward short-term R&D that will produce salable products and a profit within a relatively short time span-typically improvements in present technology. Long-range work on new methods does not get the attention from manufacturers that it needs. Some R&D projects are simply beyond the capability of a single manufacturer. Further, reliance on manufacturers does not give our industry the control over its own future that it needs to discharge its responsibilities to society. 

The findings of the R&D Goals Task Force affirmed that the electric utility industry is responsible for providing the leadership necessary to solve the many R&D problems associated with the production, transmission and distribution of electricity. The report called for improved electricity technology, including better utilization of existing fuels and, in the long term, "new fuels and new methods of production and delivery in short, a strong research and development effort with balance and clear priorities.' 

The report also called for "comprehensive system concept studies" to anticipate the character of power systems ten, twenty and thirty years hence.

For example, should we anticipate remote, large capacity "generation parks" with their own fuel processing and waste disposal systems or should local conversion systems be distributed throughout a power system? Can we combine the conversion of fuels to electric energy with other processes to produce closed cycles, such as incineration of garbage or using waste heat for desalting, space heating and agriculture?

The most interesting finding of the report dealt with the funding of electricity R&D. According to the report:

Present levels of R&D funding are inadequate: financing the levels of R&D recommended in Table [2] should have high priority within the electric utility industry, by suppliers of utility equipment and by government. 

Several interesting conclusions can be reached from information shown in Table 2. According to the table, the level of funding for electricity R&D is not expected to increase significantly over the next 

29 years. Once the funding reaches a high of $1,209 million in 1977 it is expected to taper off. This indicates that the report foresees little change in R&D needs.

The unassigned funds shown in Table 4 reveal some interesting conclusions regarding environmental R&D by the electric utility industry. The table connotes a degree of optimism concerning air and water pollution control and the environmental effects of nuclear power. By 1975, according to the report, a significant decrease in funding should take place for each of these activities and continue at a low level to the year 2000. The table also indicates a change in priorities for environmental R&D. By far the largest account in environmental 

R&D over the next 29 years is "unassigned". As the report observed:

The unassigned area encompasses expanded funding for the long-range projects related to aesthetics, for unforeseen problems arising from advanced conversion techniques, for problems arising from the need to limit emission of other fossil combustion products and environmental effects related to underground or undersea siting. 

(c) Energy/Environment Reports 

The recent surge of interest in environmental quality has resulted in significant changes in decision making processes and in institutional arrangements. A good example of these changes is to found in reorganizational proposals implemented at the Federal level. Both the 

Environmental Protection Agency and the National Oceanic and 

Atmospheric Administration were created to consolidate Federal efforts in pollution control and monitoring of the oceans and atmosphere. Another innovation has been the creation of the Council on 

Environmental Quality (CEQ) to provide assistance and advice to the President in environmental matters. Another function of the CEQ 

is the issuance of an annual report on status and trends in the quality, management and utilization of the environment. 

The intent of the CEQ's second annual report, Environmental 

Quality-1971, was to provide a summary of international, national and local activities aimed at improving the quality of the environment. 

In-depth focus was also directed toward the relationship between the economic and legal institutions of our society and the environment. 

Finally, an assessment of the "urban" environment was provided in the report.

Recent activities at the Federal level have a significant impact on energy industries. The implementation of the Clean Air Act Amend ments of 1970 (42 U.S.C. 1857) has radically altered the air pollution program of the Federal government. The Act requires the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) to establish national air quality standards as well as national standards for significant new pollution sources and for all facilities emitting hazardous substances. It also establishes a framework for the States to set emission standards for existing sources in order to achieve the national air quality standards. 

The 1970 Act also provided for a more effective procedure of Federal enforcement.

Of direct importance to energy industries, is the authority for EPA to set national air quality standards for particulate matter, sulfur oxides, carbon monoxide, photochemical oxidants, hydrocarbons and nitrogen oxides. Moreover, the CEQ's second annual report also noted that:

The EPA Administrator has published an initial list of five categories of stationary sources of pollution for which performance standards requiring use of the best adequately demostrated control technology in new facilities, will be established. Among the five industries listed was fossil fuel steam generators.

Environmental Quality-1971 also described the Administration's proposal for a lead tax and sulfur charge.

Without a tax on lead in gasoline: leaded gasoline is cheaper to produce and hence is less costly to the consumer than unleaded gasoline of comparable octane rating. The tax would help to insure that low-lead or unleaded gasoline is available in sufficient quantities now for automobiles whose octane requirements permit its use and in larger quantities when automotive pollution control devices requiring such gasoline are available to meet the 1975 emission standards.

The charge on sulfur oxide emission would be levied on sulfur emitted into the atmosphere from combustion or distillation of fossil fuels. The charge, according to the report, "is intended to stimulate development and use of sulfur oxides control technology and fuel cleaning technology and to encourage use of low-sulfur fuels."

The CEQ's report also contained a discussion of the major environmental proposals offered by the Administration. Among the most important for energy were measures dealing with water pollution control, power plant siting, national land use policy and reclamation of strip-mined lands.

The chapter on "The Economy and the Environment" contained an extensive discussion of the costs of pollution and the impact of these costs on individual industries and the economy. The report observed, for example, that the total costs of air pollution on health, vegetation, materials and property values was estimated by the EPA at roughly $16 billion annually. The costs of water pollution are even less well documented, and accurate estimates have never been obtained. 

Estimating the costs of pollution control for all types of activities involves certain problems. As Environmental Quality—1971 noted: 

.. The limited data available generally cover only air pollution control, water pollution control, and solid waste management expenditures. Further, even for air and water pollution, the costs of controlling all pollutants or all sources have not been estimated. And some of the data are based not on recent studies but on individual case studies and on engineering estimates of data compiled from a variety of sources. Some estimates are drawn from questionnaires, which may include a disproportionate number of respondents facing high pollution control costs, so there may be some bias in the results. 

Moreover, the basic assumptions in the cost studies vary widely. Byproduct revenues, estimated equipment life, allocation of process change costs between pollution control and increased productive capacity, and numerous other factors are not dealt with uniformly. Costs are based primarily on treatment costs, with inadequate attention given to likely alternative methods of achieving abatementsuch as process changes, improved plant management, and recycling. At best, regional variations in standards and discharge requirements can only be approximated. Also, as new laws are passed, additional pollutants will be regulated, and more rigorous abatement requirements will be applied to currently controlled pollutants. As with the cost of damages, cost estimates for abatement are but rudimentary. In all probability, they underestimate the actual expenses to be incurred probably substantially.

Given these difficulties, the report presented estimates for total pollution abatement costs that will be incurred by for both public and private bodies for 1970 and 1975; the figures are presented in 

Table 5.59 noww 

When comparing these expenditures, a number of important factors must be considered. For instance, the solid waste estimate greatly overstates the costs required for meeting higher standards of environmental quality. The overwhelming bulk of those costs is for collection of wastes, a service traditionally provided in urban areas. Thus, the costs of meeting air and water pollution standards are only $62 billion, or 59 percent of the total costs. 71-900-72- -9

The timing of spending is another important factor. Water quality standards have existed for several years; hence, to meet the standards, expenditures will be spread more evenly over the 1970-76 period. For air pollution, however, implementation plans to meet the Clean Air Act standards are currently only under development, and the stringent new automotive emission standards will not be applicable until 1975. Hence major costs will occur later, especially in 1975 and 

1976. Therefore, the relative importance of air pollution costs would increase appreciably if the period used were to extend beyond 1975. 

The impact of the pollution control costs upon the public and private sectors differs among the pollution media. By far the largest share for solid waste, for example, will be borne either by the public sector or directly by individuals; private industry will meet 25 percent of the total costs. Air and water pollution control is just the opposite. 

Almost two-thirds of the water pollution costs will be paid by the public sector, while almost all of the air pollution costs will be paid by the private sector.

An indication of the costs which will be incurred by energy industries in the pursuit of environmental quality goals was also revealed in another report by the National Petroleum Council entitled Environmental Conservation. In this report, the NPC identified and addressed the environmental issues which it believed to be of concern to the oil and gas industries. In addition, the report presented conclusions and observations on law and regulatory policy, the economics of environmental conservation and specific environmental concerns for air, water and land pollution with respect to the oil and gas industries. 

Regarding air pollution, the report observed that the most serious air pollution problems for the petroleum industry result from the use in some cases, the inefficient use of petroleum products to produce energy. Moreover, "the use of petroleum products is the source of only about one-fifth of the sulfur dioxide in the United States." The report also observed that the amount of nitrogen oxide, carbon monoxide and hydrocarbons emitted from stationary sources is more dependent on "furnace design and operating practices than on the fuel used."

The report also detailed the research activities which have been carried out by the industry: 

The American Petroleum Institute has actively supported research on air pollution since 1953. This current broad research effort represents an expenditure of about $3.5 million a year in direct support of outside research efforts in addition to individual company expenditures and involves projects in both air and water conservation.

The petroleum and automobile industries have worked cooperatively with the Federal Government through the Coordinating Research Council (CRC) and its Air Pollution Research Advisory Committee (APRAC) in developing technical information on the nature and effects of vehicle air pollution. 

Inter-industry sponsored research groups have contributed significant research results from extensive studies on the development of vehicle hardware and fuels fuels required to meet the vehicle emissions goals and regulations. Much of the hardware now used on vehicles for emissions control was developed in these interindustry (petroleum and automobile) research studies. 

Oil spills from offshore production facilities have been the center of more than one controversy. Regarding this problem the report observed:

The potential for pollution from such operations has been substantially reduced by sound practices, regulations and supervision. 

An examination of the industry record offshore reveals that, despite the recent major oil spills during drilling and production processes, the petroleum industry has been highly successful in keeping serious pollution incidents at a low level during its history of operations in the offshore areas. 

The report also saw a need for further research into the containment and recovery of oil at sea; specific areas for research included: 

Methods of off-loading tankers at sea in an emergency situation.

Methods of identifying oils, both in producing areas and in transportation, for proper identification of pollution sources.

Definition and classification of oil-spill treating agents for both offshore and onshore, to provide guidance and safeguards in their use. 

Advanced techniques for rapid and efficient beach and shoreline cleanup, restoration of these areas, and methods of disposal of cleaned-up oil. 

Development of improved material specifications, testing procedures and instrumentation.

The fate and behavior of oil on water and its true effect on the marine environment.

The report also urged the enactment of environmental legislation which accurately assesses the costs and benefits to society of environmental quality control. "Severe, impractical or unnecessarily costly regulation of exploration, production and transportation," the report stated, "reduces the incentives for providing and developing needed reserves of oil and gas and also increases the cost of petroleum products." The pollution control efforts of the petroleum industry will continue to have a major effect on capital spending and operating costs in refining operations of the industry and, therefore, environmental legislation should attempt to achieve a balance between the costs and benefits of control. Additional recommendations of the report on the economics of pollution control included the following: 

Where environmental quality does not meet prescribed standards, the constantly moving limits of technology and economics should be taken into account in setting realistic and stable timetables for achievement of the desired quality. 

The inevitable costs associated with environmental conservation and pollution control must be borne by society. An approach is needed which will achieve environmental objectives at the lowest net cost to society and in a fashion which will maximize current benefits so that the costs incurred may be met on as current a basis as practicable.

Imposition of economic penalties is not an effective means of attaining optimum environmental conservation.

If required, regulations for the reduction of lead in gasoline should be planned realistically to provide for such reductions as are necessary to reach practical emission goals at a minimum overall cost to the public. 

Finally, the report contained several recommendations regarding the role of government policy in pollution control and regulation. "The role of government should be to ascertain the effects of pollutants, prescribe workable standards of air, water and land quality to be maintained and, in general, to leave to private initiative the means whereby the standards will be achieved." The report also viewed at cooperative approach to the solution of an environmental problem as in the public interest. "The Executive Branch," the report noted, "should clarify the extent of cooperation that is consistent with the intent of present antitrust laws and, if necessary, seek enactment of such further legislation as would be advisable to authorize the most effective means of dealing with such problems." 

Finally, the report viewed as essential that the United States must not pursue a policy of precluding or delaying exploration and development of potential petroleum resources of its "submerged continental margins." The conflicting jurisdiction and authority among and within governments with respect to offshore pollution hamper effective control and enforcement, and should be resolved. 

(D) Supply/Demand Reports 

A number of reports relating to supply and demand aspects of the energy system have been prepared and released by noncongressional organizations during the first session of the 92d Congress. The 

Senate Committee on Interior and Insular Affairs requested the 

Federal Power Commission to prepare summary tables of the most significant results of selected recent supply and demand studies. The committee is combining those tables with a similar summary of earlier studies prepared by the Battelle Memorial Institute for the 

Office of Science and Technology in December of 1969. 

The committee's compilation based on both the Battelle Memorial Institute and Federal Power Commission summaries, will be published as a committee print as a part of the background documents for the

national fuel and energy policy study being made pursuant to 

Senate Resolution 45, 92d Congress.

Other energy supply/demand reports of interest are summarized below:

Coal. Other than the annual edition of the Minerals Yearbook published by the Department of the Interior, there were no significant new demand and supply reports issued during 1971. One report by the American Gas Association, still not released contains information of interest to the development of coal gasification. The report indicated that there are 176 sites in the United States where water and coal are available in sufficient quantities to allow the construction of commercial size coal gasification facilities. 

Petroleum. On November 11, 1970, the Gulf Oil Company increased its purchase price of domestic crude oil by $0.25 per barrel and, the next day, increased gasoline prices to its dealers by seven-tenths of one cent ($0.29 per barrel). Judging that this action of Gulf Oil heralded general price increases by the industry, the Office of Emergency Preparedness (OEP) announced it would undertake to determine whether the price increases were necessary to accomplish national security objectives.

The OEP's report, Report on Crude Oil and Gasoline Price Increases of November 1970, was issued in April, 1971. It observed that there are four national security objectives which can serve as criteria in determining whether price increases are justified: (1) A satisfactory level of domestic reserves of crude oil, supplemented from secure foreign sources of supply; (2) Maintaining spare capacity to produce and deliver crude oil when international factors disrupt supplies from other sources; (3) Maintain refinery capacity in the United States adequate to meet both defense and essential civilian needs, in periods of disruption in normal patterns of world oil movement; and (4) Provide a healthy petroleum industry in the United States with a capacity to meet the nation's national security requirements.

The report concluded that "no national security justification" was given for the gasoline price increases and there was "no evidence" that the November price increases would lead to the maintenance of spare domestic capacity for the production of crude oil or the maintenance of refinery capacity. For these and other reasons, the OEP took the position that the November price increases "were not required to meet any national security objectives arising out of the present short-run disruptions in the international flow of oil."

The report did conclude, however, that "because of the urgent need to encourage exploration for and development of crude oil reserves in the long run, the November price increase for crude oil · · may be justified. ." The report noted that price increases will be an "inadequate instrument" for the achievement of national security objectives over the long run and recommended that other measures, such as accelerated offshore leasing and "realistic" pricing of natural gas, should be utilized. The Director of OEP added: 

In my judgment, it has not been established that price increases alone will achieve our national security objectives, even though they certainly will provide a measure of incentive toward that end. For example, development of the great supply potential of the North Slope of Alaska is currently insensitive to price at present levels, because the North Slope discovery is of such magnitude that the unit costs of production will be low and its unavailability to the market is not a

matter of price. On the other hand, revisions and extensions of existing fields and the extent of other exploration efforts may well turn on this marginal increase in crude prices.

The OEP's report was strongly criticized in a committee print of the Joint Economic Committee entitled: Report on Crude Oil and Gasoline Price Increases of November 1970: A Background Study. 

The Joint Committee was critical of the OEP report for failing to examine the national security grounds for the recent price increases. 

The report observed that "the possibility that governmental measures might prevent prices from increasing or that lower prices might be consistent with national security was never considered" in the report. 

The failure of OEP to examine the national security justifications of the past prices increases "and to translate them into a probable explanation for the recent price increase and a potential explanation for future price increases parallels its admission in the Report that OEP has no standards for measuring the relationship between oil imports and national security." Specifically, the Joint Committee found that the four "national security objectives" outlined in the 

OEP report failed to define: (1) "satisfactory" domestic crude oil reserves; (2) adequate productive or refinery capacity; (3) the Nation's security requirements; and (4) national security contingencies.

The Joint Committee concluded that the OEP did not establish criteria for determining how much oil is needed for national securityexcept "the more the better." Beyond this failure to quantify future needs was the "technical shortcoming of including only proven reserves and no other physical factors." 

Over the years oil industry officials have argued that present prices should be sufficient to pay for the investments needed to satisfy future increased requirements as well as present production costs. 

The higher and firmer the growth rate, the greater the justification for the price increase. The Joint Committee print criticized the OEP for not rejecting this concept and for being overly concerned about the advance payments ending up in foreign investments instead of domestic exploration and development. 

According to the Joint Committee print the OEP report had determined that State prorationing (a system whereby the States control the rate of production of oil) was in conflict with national security and recommended that the Federal Government discontinue its voluntary adherence to State production restrictions in Federal offshore areas. It was suggested that the "OEP should have recommended termination or at least suspension of all such controls. The same force which permits crude oil prices to increase without market justification enables the States to thwart the decision to operate the Federal area in a manner more consistent with national security. State market demand prorationing is adverse to the national security." The committee print also had some strong criticism about the use of industry data by the OEP: 

The lack of independent government projections of oil demand during 1970-71 is a prime source of our recent difficulties. Although much of the analysis made by the Cabinet Task Force is accurate, its reliance on an industry concensus demand projection is a serious flaw. Having erred on the low side, industry is now attempting to provide government with stepped-up projections. The OEP has the responsibility of constantly surveiling petroleum imports. 

Yet it has based its "studies" upon data summaries provided by the oil industry.

The OEP has failed to collect the data necessary to make rudimentary cost/ benefit analyses of oil price increases and of the $7 billion indirect subsidy of the oil industry. If higher prices and the subsidy are justified by national security needs, the OEP should be able to evaluate how changes in prices and the subsidy will affect national security. It has not and presently cannot do this. These figures should have been collected before the support program was begun. They are needed now to determine to what extent, if at all, these programs should be continued. 

Too much money-considerably more than $7 billion annually-is at stake for policies to be based on unreliable and inconsistent trade association data. To fulfill the law the OEP must collect this data and carry out the suggested analyses. 

When the committee print was released, there was considerable press coverage concerning the announcement of a large discovery of oil off the south coast of Alaska which was said to be discussed by the Joint Committee report. The existence of such reserves was denied by the Department of the Interior and industry spokesmen. A careful reading of the committee print reveals that the only reference to such a discovery was the following:

Nor has the Department of the Interior made available its preliminary assessment of offshore northern Alaska (Cardova)- an area which is understood by some to be larger than North Slope, but which might interfere with the North 

Slope pricing expected by the oil industry. 

Natural Gas.-The fourth survey of natural gas requirements by the Future Requirements Committee (which was created by the Gas Industry Committee to provide long-range analysis of gas requirements) was published in 1971. Previous surveys had been published in 1964, 1967 and 1969.

As Table 7 shows, the growth of gas requirements during the next quarter century is expected to differ markedly among the various classification of users. Increases in the commercial sector reflect a "continued expansion of the general economy, an increase in apartment construction, and a rise in-conversions from other fuels in those regions of the United States where commercial market saturations now are relatively low." The increase in the industrial sector is expected to show the largest volumetric increase of any category reflecting increased demand for gas in response to stricter air quality control standards and a move by industries to insure uninterrupted gas supplies.

Gas requirements for electric utilities were separately identified in the report for the first time and the requirements were broken down into firm and interruptible accounts. Requirements in the firm catagory are expected to grow from 1.9 trillion cubic feet to 6.9 trillion cubic feet by 1995. This would be equivalent to a growth rate of 5.3 percent compounded annually-the highest of any class. 

The regional estimates of gas requirements are presented in Table 8. The largest increases in absolute quantities are expected to take place in Appalachia, the Great Lakes and the Gulf Coast regions.

The report also contains "operational estimates" which outline the gas requirements of the industry over the next five years. The report cautions, however, that- ... this five year operational estimate is a first attempt by the Committee and the respondents and is only a rough approximation of the requirements expected to be met in this future short-range period. The information on future gas supply possessed by the individual respondents varies considerably in both quality and detail.

In order to make such an estimate, it is necessary to judge the response of newly discovered gas, importation of natural or liquefied gas, and facilities to produce

substitutes for natural gas. There is also a need to determine the rate at which gas can be produced, the capability of the transmission systems to move gas to the market, and the contractual obligations that link the producer, pipeliner, and the distributor.

The operational estimates for 1975 indicated a deficit in natural gas supplies of 3.88 trillion cubic feet and the sector most heavily affected by these deficiencies would be electric utilities; the area most influenced would be the Great Lakes region.

The basis for the domestic forecasts was an examination of the total electric generating capacity installed at the end of 1959, the additions made during the years 1960 through 1970, and the additions expected during 1971 through 1977. The estimates were obtained from Federal 

Power Commission figures. The nuclear fraction multiplied by the additions to electrical generating capacity gives nuclear additions; the resulting figures are presented in Table 9. The total capacity of nuclear power plants in the United States obtained by adding the forecasts for the various areas is about 150,000 megawatts by 1980 and about 300,000 megawatts at the end of 1985.

1962 estimates, for example, correctly forecast the 1970 capacity (5,000 Mw), but differ markedly with subsequent forecasts of the 1975 and 1980 totals.

Electricity. The September 15, 1971 issue of Electrical World con tained the magazine's 22nd annual electrical industry forecast. "Before year-end," the report observed, "U.S. utilities will have added. 

30,000 Mw [Megawatts] to the net generating capability of the nation's

power systems." These additions will increase the reserve margin by 2 percent despite sales which were below normal. This should leave utilities, according to Electric World, "in a better position to supply the soaring consumption that will accompany the coming economic upturn.' 

The predicted growth in electric power sales was estimated to climb at 7 percent to 8 percent each year through 1980 and somewhat slower to 1990. Utilities will have to add system capacity each year to serve these increases and the estimated yearly increases in the forecast were 47,500 Mw to 1980 and 76,000 Mw to 1990. Yearly expenditures required to meet these loads will be $23 billion and $37 billion for 1980 and 1990, respectively. These figures compare to the $15 billion spent by utilities in 1971.

In 1972, U.S. electric utilities reported one of their slowest sales growth rates in a decade according to Electrical World. The growth rate of 5.0 percent was down nearly one and one-half percent below 

1970 and three percentage points below the average for the last five years. As Electrical World observed: "This slowdown can be attributed almost entirely to stagnation in the industrial sector which, in normal years, account for about 43 percent of utility sales."

While Electrical World did forecast a "little gain" for the industrial sector in 1971, "most of this gain is attributable to increased usage by the Atomic Energy Commission as it steps up enrichment of uranium to fuel nuclear power stations." In 1972 "all segments of the industry are expected to recover substantially from this year's low." (Table 11.)

Residential sales in 1971 will show a 7.7 percent gain over 1970 which is below the average of the last 5 years, Electrical World reported. 

About one-half the expected gain "can be attributed to adding 1.2 million customers [and] 600,000 housing units with electrical heat." 

Sales to residential customers were forecasted to increase to 930 billion kilowatt hours (kwhr) in 1980 and 1,700 billion kwhr in 1990

(Table 11). The influence of electric space heating will also have an impact on future sales; according to Electrical World:

Electric space heating in some 5-million dwelling units has already assumed an important role in residential kwhr sales, accounting for about 15% of the 1970 total. With the upturn in residential construction, and the complete absence of pollution from electric heating, installations can be anticipated at an accelerating rate to 15-million units by 1980 and upwards of 30-million by 1990. With such growth, electric heating will account for about 20% of residential sales during the 1980s.

New capacity was added by utilities at a faster rate than expected in 1970. Nearly 30,000 Mw of new generating capability were added during the year despite retirements and slippages in placing new plants into operation. According to Electrical World: "construction schedules are heavier for the years ahead, with enough already in progress to boost net capability by 35,000 Mw next year, 40,000 Mw in 1973, 40,500 Mw in 1974, and 39,000 Mw in 1975." (Table 12). These additions should raise the reserve margin up to 25 percent by 1975.

In 1966, the Federal Power Commission (FPC) created six Regional Advisory Commissions to assist in updating the first National Power Survey (1964) through the development of regional reports. These reports comprised Volumes II and III of the 1970 National Power 

Survey and were issued by the FPC during the first session of the 92nd Congress. Volume I, which contains a summary and analysis of the six regional reports as well as other material, will be issued during 1972.

The FPC utilized the regional commissions for compiling reports largely because electricity, unlike other energy forms, is not generally transported over long distances. Thus, the regions selected by the 

FPC reflect areas which have fairly homogenous demand and supply characteristics, where transmission, distribution and utilization of

electricity is also similar, and consist of areas where power is frequently interchanged among utilities. These characteristics enabled the 

FPC to issue reports which accurately reflect conditions present in the industry in that area. On the other hand, there are differences between the reports issued by the various regional commissionslargely due to the different approaches utilized by each regional commission.

A summary of the data presented in the regional surveys is given below: 

Northeast Region

Study Area A (New England) has an almost constant annual peak load increase of approximately 6.6 percent from 1970 to 1990, resulting in a peak load value in 1990 that is approximately 3.5 times the 1970 level. Study 

Area B (New York) shows an initial annual peak load increase in PSA-3 (Upstate) of 5.2 percent from 1970, gradually declining to 5.1 percent by 1990 while PSA-4 (Downstate, New York City) has an initial 6 percent annual increase, declining to 5.2 percent in the final year. PSA-3 shows a 1990 peak load that is 2.7 times that of 1970, while the PSA-4 load increases by a multiple of 2.9. The Study Area Ć (Pa., N.J., Md., Del., D.C.) annual load growth ranges from 7.7 percent in 1970, to 5.8 percent in 1990 with an overall growth factor of 3.3 times the 1970 load. It is expected that the regional power suppliers will be required to finance and install over 150,000 megawatts of additional generation to supply a total of 165,000 megawatts as the coincident peak load in 1990. Both large fossil-fired and nuclear generating type plants are included in projected additions, in the role of carrying the base electric system load. 

Pumped-storage hydro generating plants have been included to share the job of carrying peak loads. Although gas or oil-fired peaking turbines and limited quantities of conventional hydroelectric generation capacity will also be installed during this period ... The optimum combinations of base load, intermediate, and peaking units must be determined by specific studies, but preliminary analyses suggest a mix by 1990, of 40, 45, and 15 percent, respectively. It is anticipated that the 1990 generating capacity will be about 8 percent diesel or gas turbine, 3 percent conventional hydro, 7 percent pumped storage, 24 percent fossil-fueled thermal, and 58 percent nuclear. 

East Central Region

The non-coincident peak load of the East Central Region for 1965 was 31,000 megawatts (MW) and the electric energy requirements were 180 billion kilowatthours (kWh). The expected non-coincident peak demand will reach about 44,000 MW by 1970 with total energy requirements of approximately 252 billion kWh. By the year 1990, the projected values of demand and energy requirements are expected to be approximately 146,000 MW and 857 billion kWh, respectively. This represents an average annual compound growth rate of 6.3%. In view of the growth of both summer and winter seasonal loads and the already heavy component of industrial load in the region, no significant change in the present relatively high annual load factor of approximately 66% is being projected. The present break-down of consumer usage by major categories of load is 26% for rural and residential, 16% for commercial, 54% for industrial and 4% for all other usage. Again, no significant change in these values is foreseen at this time. To meet the power demands of 1990 it is anticipated that at least 130,000 MW of new generating capacity will be constructed in the East Central Region. These additions, together with existing generation and allowing for retirements, will result in an overall generating capability in the region of about 180,000 MW in 1990 to meet a load of approximately 146,000 MW. 

Current projections show that most of these generating units will be of increasingly larger size, ranging up to 1,500 MW by 1980 and possibly 2,500

MW by 1990. The effects of this trend to larger sized units to achieve in-

creasing economies of scale will be to radically change the composition of unit size in the region from a mix in 1970 where about 70% of the total installed capacity is in units of 300 MW or smaller size to a distribution of sizes in 1990 where almost 50% of all capacity installed is expected to be in unit sizes of 

1,000 MW or larger.

Southeast Region
